#10497. Four ways work was better in the ’90s

The fundamentals of work remain remarkably stable over time. For both our hunter-gatherer ancestors and modern chief AI ethicists, work was, and still is, a critical way to translate skill, effort, and time into food and safety—and being good at it enhances status as well as lubricating our self-esteem. 

But the specific cultural forces shaping what we do and how we do it can change dramatically, even within a few decades.

Take the 1990s, which many living humans today would not have experienced because they were too young. In the scheme of things, three decades isn’t that much time. Yet the differences between then and now are almost comically dramatic.

In the ‘90s, work was dominated by fax machines, pagers, bulky early mobile phones, and painfully slow dial-up internet connections that occupied the telephone line. Email was only emerging, to the point that your exchanges were largely limited to other early adopters, and the content of messages was often less interesting than the process of exchanging electronic mail itself. 

Being online was something you actively did, rather than a permanent state of existence, and work took place almost entirely at work, with the office encapsulating our professional selves and much of life unfolding outside it. Leaving the office generally meant leaving work behind, and being unreachable was the norm rather than a sign of negligence, indulgence, or power. 

Technology was an add-on—a secondary part of work rather than something we ought to supervise by bot-sitting, being a meat proxy, or calling ourselves the “human in the loop.” Since there were few apps or websites through which our personal lives could infiltrate working hours, digital distractions and multitasking were relatively rare: no Amazon shopping, Expedia travel planning, LinkedIn humblebragging, or keeping up with what our neighbor’s cat had for breakfast on Facebook or Instagram.

So, was all of that better or worse? Here are four ways in which it was arguably the former—and four in which it was perhaps the latter.

Four things that were arguably better about work in the 1990s

1. More genuine human connection. Work was a fundamentally more social, in-person experience, with more face-to-face interactions, spontaneous conversations, and opportunities to build relationships beyond scheduled meetings. We lacked digital personas and our reputations were the result of what we actually do in the physical world.

2. Greater focus and fewer distractions. With fewer digital interruptions and less pressure to respond instantly, people had more opportunities for sustained attention, deep work, and uninterrupted concentration. Or at least, our distractions were purely human—which meant that you could actually withdraw from others by staring at your computer (as opposed to the reverse).

3. More independent thinking and intellectual effort. Knowledge workers had to rely more heavily on their own memory, reasoning, creativity, and problem-solving skills, rather than outsourcing cognitive tasks to algorithms or AI. General knowledge, as demonstrated by the art of storytelling and argumentation, as well as engaging others in deep thinking and logical reasoning, all occupied a more central role in our daily working days than they do today.

4. Greater psychological separation between work and life. Work had clearer temporal and physical boundaries, and employees were less likely to feel permanently connected, constantly monitored, or threatened by technologies capable of making their skills obsolete. There were few discussions about “bringing your whole self to work” and employers didn’t need to monitor our social media footprint to alleviate any concerns about reputation management or brand compromising. Oh, and work did not occupy the central part of our identity, unless you were a workaholic: someone who resembles the average worker today in the sense of never disconnecting and always thinking about work and their professional self.

Four things that were arguably worse about work in the 1990s

1. Less flexibility and autonomy. Work was tied to specific locations and fixed schedules, with little opportunity for remote or hybrid working, even when physical presence added little value. This may sound great today to people who miss or like structure and demarcation, but there is a reason why online and hybrid work have become so popular, namely that people enjoy freedom and flexibility—and especially their employers’ trust—vis-à-vis how their work is done.

2. More rigid hierarchies and organizational bureaucracy. Authority was more concentrated, communication more top-down, and employees generally had less freedom to challenge decisions or influence how work was organized. Organizational designs are more fluid, agile, and networked, which has persuaded employers to shift beyond formal org charts and try to evaluate the value each individual brings to their teams and organizations. In essence, this is good news for those who work hard and have talent, and bad news for those who simply pretend to work.

3. Less access to information and expertise. Knowledge was harder to find, slower to circulate, and more dependent on formal education, organizational status, and proximity to influential people. In theory, any novice could produce much more proficient, competent, and high-quality output in any area of the knowledge economy—which of course doesn’t guarantee they will!—because there is no longer any distance or separation between any question and any answer. And the ability to learn and acquire a decent level of expertise in any topic has increased dramatically with AI.

4. Fewer opportunities for inclusion and global collaboration. Geographic barriers, traditional career paths, and less inclusive workplace norms limited opportunities for women, minorities, people with disabilities, and talent outside major economic centers. This means opportunities to thrive and succeed because of your social capital (a euphemism for privilege or “who you know”) were far more common than today. In essence, work has been evolving towards a more meritocratic and talent-centric model, though there’s still a great deal of progress to make.

Does it matter?

In short, there is an interesting paradox in that work in the 1990s offered less freedom over where and when we worked, but arguably more freedom from work when we weren’t working. And while today’s technologies have liberated us from many tedious tasks, they have also made it harder to escape work, focus on it, or feel valued, not least since our professional identity is being rewritten by AI’s impact on our jobs, roles, and skills, and the impact of that is yet to be determined. 

Importantly, since employee engagement levels tend to fluctuate very little across the years, it is safe to conclude that people were no more or less happy then than they are today. Which points to the idea that any objective changes or improvements in working conditions and habits are quickly absorbed by our expectations. We adjust very quickly to normalizing changes, which leaves roughly the same proportion of people happy, indifferent, and miserable at work.

Maybe the more work changes, the more it remains the same.

#10496. Ahead of its IPO filing, Anthropic pledges to prioritize humanity’s benefit. Here’s how

When Anthropic goes public on Wall Street, the new pressure to deliver profits will test a commitment it has made since its founding: to prioritize humanity’s benefit as it develops artificial intelligence.

The San Francisco startup, which plans an initial public offering this month, has sketched out a governance structure that it says will protect the company’s broader mission against the pull of financial interests. But the mechanisms it describes give the company wide leeway to decide how to measure whether it’s benefiting society, experts say.

Like its rival OpenAI, Anthropic is a public benefit corporation, a legal designation used by companies that pledge to balance shareholder interests with the public good. How that will play out for companies worth potentially trillions of dollars is uncharted territory.

“I think then the real test emerges if and when the question of public benefit becomes costly” — or when the public benefit comes into conflict with making a profit, said Sarah Kreps, a professor and director of Cornell University’s Tech Policy Institute.

In its regulatory filings, Anthropic pledges to pursue long-term benefits of humanity through the responsible development of advanced AI. “We strive to help bring humanity safely into an era where transformative AI exerts tremendous and potentially decisive influence over a broad range of human undertakings,” the company said. Anthropic also has pledged at least 5% of its stock to a “public benefit” fund and its founders have committed to donate 80% of their wealth.

A pledge to do good is baked into corporate structure

Other tech companies have pledged to do good including Google, which prominently featured its then-motto, “Don’t Be Evil,” in its investor prospectus when it went public in 2004. But the words were not legally binding, and critics have accused the tech giant of leaving them behind.

Elon Musk’s takeover of Twitter in 2021 shows how the conflict between profit and public good might play out in real life, said Stavros Gadinis, a law professor at UC Berkeley School of Law.

“Let’s say that Twitter’s directors felt that Elon was the wrong guy to police the public square because they didn’t trust him, they didn’t like him, they didn’t think he’d be fair, whatever it was,” Gadinis said. “But he made them an offer that was so high compared to what their advisors told them the company was really worth that it would be against their fiduciary duties to not sell to Elon. This is the situation that the public benefit charter is trying to avoid.”

Had Twitter been a public benefit corporation, it could have been required to consider factors beyond maximizing shareholder value when selling the company.

There aren’t many public benefit corporations whose shares are publicly traded and they are all much smaller than Anthropic and OpenAI. They include the eyewear company Warby Parker, the insurance company Lemonade and software developer Veeva Systems, which had a conventional corporate structure when it went public in 2013 and converted to a public benefit corporation in 2021.

Anthropic’s idealistic ambitions for how it develops AI could run up against competitive pressure, such as from China, Kreps said.

Anthropic and OpenAI’s models have gone from answering almost any question, even if it was dangerous, to becoming much more circumspect. But as they face more competition, including from open-source Chinese models that likely will tell you whatever you want, she said, “will they start to rethink some of those guardrails? And what happens then?”

The setup generally is not preferred by shareholders because it gives more power to the company’s board than it does to shareholders.

Whatever a company decides as its public benefit mission, it will likely be much more difficult to measure than profits. That’s particularly true for AI companies, whose leaders have exalted AI as the solution to many of humanity’s problems — from curing cancer to creating economic prosperity.

“There are these echo chambers, the Silicon Valley bubble where they really think that what they’re doing is for the good of humanity,” Kreps said. And someone immersed in that environment, she added, might not even ask the right questions or understand how “public benefit” should be measured.

A trust aims to hold Anthropic accountable

A Long-Term Benefit Trust created by Anthropic is intended to operate as an independent body of trustees with expertise in AI safety, national security, public policy, and social enterprise.

Through a special Class T common stock, the trust “generally has the authority to elect and remove” Class T directors, who together will constitute a majority of Anthropic’s board of directors, according to the IPO filing. The Class T common stock is a single share that allows the five trustees to vote as a unit. It has no economic rights — meaning they get to vote but they don’t see any financial benefit or losses from the share and can’t sell it.

“The LTBT’s arrangements are designed to minimize the LTBT’s and the Trustees’ exposure to our financial performance and grant the Trustees sufficient independence to ensure we are striking this balance appropriately,” the filing said.

Three of the company’s board directors are elected by the trust directly, and another three are elected by other shareholders, which include the company founders with a 50.1% voting majority. A seventh director, with a potentially tie-breaking vote, is nominated by the trust and the holders of the Class T shares, but cannot be appointed or removed without consent from the board’s nominating committee, which is expected to be controlled by the founders.

Effectively, the trust can only hold Anthropic accountable on issues such as AI safety and the company’s broader mission up to a point, Gadinis said.

The trust “cannot determine the everyday decisions of these directors or the management of the company,” he said. “If they disagree, they will have to go through the effort of removing directors, and they may not be able to remove a majority.”

As a public company, Anthropic also will be required to report finances and generally be more transparent than a privately held one. Many public companies dealing with AI, including Google and Microsoft, regularly update the public on efforts to develop AI responsibly.

The incentive for transparency could be as powerful as other mechanisms set up in Anthropic’s corporate structure, Gadinis said.

“I do think that by being public, we are going to get a lot more information, because a lot of people are going to be asking questions, and companies will be responding to these questions. not only because they’re required by security laws, but also because they want to build this kind of trust with the investing community,” he said.

—Barbara Ortutay, AP Technology Writer

#10494. Family Dollar has to pay $40 million back to customers in an FTC lawsuit settlement: How to claim your payout

Family Dollar has agreed to pay $63 million to settle allegations by the Federal Trade Commission (FTC) and 17 states that it charged customers more at the register than the prices shown on shelves, product tags, its website, and its mobile app. 

According to the complaint, Family Dollar overcharged its customers “millions of dollars” since January 2023, including in low-income communities where residents had limited access to full-service grocery stores.

Family Dollar did not respond to Fast Company’s request for comment as of publication.

Here’s what to know about the settlement and whether you qualify for a payout:

When was the settlement announced? 

The settlement was announced on November 12, the same day that the U.S. District Court for the Eastern District of Virginia filed an order that requires Family Dollar to implement a pricing accuracy program that must be independently certified annually.

While Family Dollar agreed to the settlement, it did not admit to or deny any of the allegations in the complaint, according to the order. 

“Today’s order will ensure Family Dollar stops deceiving consumers about its prices and returns money to consumers harmed by these practices,” Christopher Mufarrige, director of the FTC’s Bureau of Consumer Protection, said in the announcement. 

How much money will customers get?

Of the $63 million, $40 million will be distributed as payments to customers, and $20 million will take the form of a discount program.

The discount program would be a single event: a 15% reduction on most items for at least two consecutive days, excluding alcohol, tobacco and gift cards.

Who is eligible for a payout?

Shoppers who made at least one purchase at a Family Dollar store between January 1, 2023, and October 1, 2026, are eligible to qualify for Family Dollar’s payouts.

How can I submit a claim?

Arkansas Attorney General Tim Griffin said an online claim portal will be posted on Family Dollar’s website within 14 days of the final court order. The order does not specify how much each customer would receive.

Payments would then be issued on a rolling basis until the $40 million is spent. 

What kinds of items did Family Dollar allegedly overcharge for?

The complaint alleges that customers have reported Family Dollar’s pricing inconsistencies for years.

Common household supplies such as eggs, milk, diapers, cleaning products and toilet paper were named among the household items for which customers were overcharged. 

Family Dollar has also faced scrutiny in the past over its inaccurate pricing, according to the complaint.

Over the last two years, Family Dollar reached settlements with three state attorneys general and consumer protection agencies, including a $400,000 settlement in Ohio in February 2024, a $113,960 settlement with Wisconsin in January 2025, and a $300,000 settlement with Arizona in May 2025. 

Family Dollar is not the only company accused of deceptive pricing practices.

In a related complaint filed on October 26, the FTC and state partners sued Dollar General Corporation over similar pricing tactics that resulted in overcharging consumers. The litigation is still ongoing. Dollar General did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

#10492. Stressful jobs do, in fact, pay more. But there’s a catch

Do more stressful jobs come with a bigger paycheck? It depends.

According to a recent study conducted by online resume building platform Resume Genius, the most stressful jobs pay an average of 62% more than the least stressful jobs. And yet, not everyone is enjoying a high-pressure premium.

Workers with a high school diploma or postsecondary certificate typically see a 24% pay premium for taking on a more stressful job; those with a Master’s earn 27% more in high-stress roles, while those with a doctoral or professional degree earn a whopping 78% more.

Among bachelor’s degree holders, however, higher work stress doesn’t pay. In fact, bachelor’s degree holders in high-pressure roles earn 1% less on average than bachelor-holding colleagues in lower-pressure positions.

“Not all forms of workplace stress are rewarded equally,” says Resume Genius career expert Nathan Soto. “The data we found suggests that a stress premium exists, but it’s concentrated in particular educational and occupational groups, rather than being a universal feature of the labor market.”

The study is consistent with other research that suggests workers are less willing to take on more stressful jobs, even if they come with a higher salary. A survey of 14,000 workers by emotional insight platform Hint App, for example, found that 58% would reject a promotion that required them to be available after hours.

Putting a price on workplace pressure

Soto says Resume Genius developed a “Stress Index” to help quantify workplace pressure across 754 occupations.

One-third of that 100-point scale is based on the role’s “Stress Tolerance” level, according to the U.S. Department of Labor’s Occupational Information Network (or O*NET) database. The agency defines “Stress Tolerance” as “a tendency to cope and function effectively in stressful situations at work.”

The remaining two-thirds were based on seven stressful job attributes: time pressure, workplace conflicts, dealing with unpleasant or angry people, the consequence of errors, responsibility for others’ health and safety, competition and long working hours. Stress Index scores were then compared against the latest pay data from the Bureau of Labor Statistics.

“Among six-figure occupations, workers in high-stress jobs were much more likely to deal with unpleasant or angry people, handle conflict regularly and face time pressure every day,” Soto says. “Responsibility for other people’s health and safety and the seriousness of potential mistakes were also major factors.”

Soto explains that high-paying, highly stressful jobs that don’t require a bachelor’s degree often involve physical risk, demanding work conditions or significant responsibility. For example, electrical power-line installers earn an average of $95,320 and have a Stress Index level of 97. Detectives and criminal investigators, who also aren’t required to earn a bachelor’s, scored 100 on the stress index and earned an average of $93,790.

High-paying jobs that require a professional or doctorate degree, meanwhile, often require specialized training or expertise, come with greater responsibility as well as higher stakes. For example, anesthesiologists scored 100 on the Stress Index and earned an average of $391,490 per year.

But: “Jobs that require bachelor’s degrees follow a different pattern,” Soto says. “Many of them pay high salaries without necessarily rewarding additional workplace stress with higher pay.”

More than half of the six-figure jobs included in the study scored above 67 on the Stress Index, but the researchers did identify 19 high-paying low-stress jobs. They include electronics engineer, software developer, statistician, data scientist, computer and information research scientist and actuary.

A high tolerance for stress has a dollar value

Many workers look to avoid stress, and are even willing to take a pay cut for it. But those who can take the heat can often earn more.

According to a 2025 study conducted by researchers at the University of Erlangen-Nuremberg in Germany, workers are willing to take a 9.6% wage cut to avoid frequent, tight deadlines, and earn about 6% less to avoid having to multitask often.

“Avoiding pressure comes at a price. So you have to think whether you’re willing to pay that price,” explains economics professor Markus Nagler, who co-authored the study.

Swapping a low-pressure job for a high-pressure job within the same occupation, according to the study, came with a 12% earnings premium on average, though half of that raise was due to an increase in working hours.

The researchers, however, also found that those who didn’t mind certain stressful job aspects were more likely to find jobs that paid them for that sacrifice. For example, those who take on jobs with tight deadlines may be able to stomach the pressure more than the average worker.

“The kind of people who select into these jobs and remain in these jobs typically are those who have the highest tolerance for these kinds of working conditions,” Nagler says. “The problem is that you choose a job, but you don’t get to choose specific aspects of the job.”

Not everyone gets to choose a lower-stress job

The research suggests that workers in higher-stress jobs tend to earn more. But other research finds that workers in low-wage jobs are more likely to face harsher working conditions.

According to a 2023 study published in the American Economic Review, workers earning lower wages were less willing to give up part of their earnings to enjoy non-work amenities like schedule flexibility, more paid time off and less heavy lifting. The study also found that higher-wage workers are more likely to enjoy those additional perks than workers in lower-wage jobs.

“The people that are getting paid more are also getting better working conditions,” explains one of the study’s co-authors and an associate professor at Penn Medicine, David Powell. “People who are compensated better want a combination of higher wages and better amenities, so when you look at disparities, and you include amenities, they typically widen.”

That is not to say that high earners aren’t dealing with difficult working conditions. However, Powell’s research suggests those with higher earnings are in a better position to sacrifice some of their earnings for a lower-stress role. Those struggling to make ends meet, however, are typically less willing to forfeit some of their pay for better working conditions.

As a result, lower earners tend to enjoy fewer of the amenities that make work less stressful, while higher earners can more easily swap a high-stress job for a more laid-back alternative, even if it pays less.

#10490. Your marketing approval process is spending your budget

“Can we get one more person to weigh in?”

If you work in marketing, you know where this can lead. The campaign is ready, the team has worked through the feedback, and the launch date is approaching. Then another reviewer joins the conversation and questions a decision everyone thought was already settled.

Back it goes. New copy, updated graphics, another round of approvals. Meanwhile, the next project is waiting.

No one approved a bigger budget. But the team is spending more time on the same work.

In June 2026 research from Bloomberg Media and Lippincott, nearly eight in 10 CMOs said bureaucracy frequently interferes with decision-making. Corporate leadership was the primary source of delays for major marketing initiatives.

As leaders, we need to look at our part in that. We expect teams to deliver on time, but every additional review affects their ability to do it.

“ONE MORE REVIEW” IS MORE WORK THAN IT SOUNDS

Changing a headline might take a few minutes. Updating it across a landing page, emails, ads, and a sales deck takes longer. Someone also has to check those changes and send the updated materials back for approval.

That rework has to fit somewhere, and it’s rarely planned for in the workback schedule. So it takes time from a campaign already in production. It forces the creative department to stay late (again), or pushes the deadline to next week. Your team feels overworked and stressed, so it may appear to be a resource problem. But the real problem is in the process.

The solution is to take a hard look at your marketing workflow, both documented and unspoken.

START WITH APPROVALS

If you find your campaigns are consistently missing deadlines, look at how many review rounds you typically have, and how many stakeholders must sign off. Understand who is involved, why they are involved, and how you can make sure they’re providing that value at the right time.

Is the CMO signing off on the campaign messaging before any creative is designed, or are they seeing the campaign messaging the night before launch? Is legal being asked to approve photos from the expensive product shoot without being asked to provide any legal guardrails in the brief? 

Carefully sequencing sign-offs based on dependencies, and allotting time in your workback schedule for their review, will ensure you’re setting your team up for success from day one.

GIVE REVIEWERS CLEAR EXPECTATIONS

Often, the root of friction is in how we request feedback. Send a draft with “Let me know your thoughts,” and you may get a rewrite when you just needed someone to check the facts.

Be specific about each person’s role. Product experts review accuracy. Legal reviews claims. The marketing lead evaluates whether the message speaks to the audience and aligns with the campaign goal.

Establish who makes the final call when those perspectives conflict. A project manager can gather comments and chase responses, but someone with decision-making authority needs to resolve any disagreement. Asking the team to satisfy incompatible feedback just sends it around again.

RANK FEEDBACK BY ITS IMPACT

There are good reasons to reopen approved work. A product feature changes, a claim turns out to be inaccurate, or legal identifies a concern.

If someone wants a late change, bring the conversation back to the campaign’s goal. Will it help the audience understand the message or correct something important? If it doesn’t clear one of those hurdles, stick with the approved version.

As a reviewer, before requesting a change, find out what that change will impact. That includes who must review it again and what will have to move or be sacrificed to accommodate it. You need to understand its impact on the schedule, the team capacity, and ultimately the budget. Make sure it’s worth the cost.

As leaders, we need to trust our team to make the right decisions. But when there are too many decision-makers on a project, nothing gets resolved. Before asking your marketing team to move faster, look at how approvals are handled in your organization. Every review has a cost, and those review cycles may be eating up your budget.

Lisa Larson-Kelley is president and CEO of Quantious.

#10488. From User Feedback to a Better Prompt: 4 Tools Tested in TypeScript

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

When an LLM app gives a wrong answer, nothing in your logs shows it. The request returns 200, the JSON is valid, and no exception fires. Usually the user is the only one who notices. A thumbs-down button records that moment, on the exact trace that went wrong, while the user still remembers why.

That button is easy to add. The hard part is what comes after: turning the thumbs-down into a reviewed test case, a judge score, and a better prompt.

We ran that loop in TypeScript in four tools: LangSmith, Langfuse, Phoenix and AcruxCore. All four handled the first four steps, which are user feedback, reviewer comments, a dataset and an LLM judge. Only AcruxCore rewrote the prompt from TypeScript. On 15 support tickets that no tool had seen, the rewritten prompt routed 13 correctly, against 7 for the original.

Our test app is a support-ticket router. It reads a ticket and returns JSON with a category (account, billing, bug or feature_request) and a priority (P0, P1 or P2). The first version of the prompt describes only the output format:

The support team has a policy that this prompt never states. Sign-in problems are always account. A customer who can't use the product at all is P0. Any billing error is P1, even when the customer says there is no rush. Feature requests are P2, however angry the customer sounds.

Without that policy, the model guesses priority from the customer's tone. Here are three real replies from the first version:

Every reply is valid JSON, so no schema check catches the mistake. A person has to read the reply and say it is wrong. The loop in this article takes that judgement and turns it into a better prompt.

All four tools cover evaluation. We compared the self-hosted ones on tracing in Self-Hosted LangSmith Alternatives: 4 Tools Tested on One TypeScript Agent, and on prompt registries in Prompt Management in TypeScript. For a broader comparison of self-hosted options, see this LangSmith alternatives roundup. This article covers the feedback loop only.

We wrote 30 tickets. Fifteen are the "production traffic" that each tool's app processed. The other 15 are a holdout set. No tool saw them, and we used them only for the final before-and-after check.

Each tool ran the same sequence from one TypeScript script:

The reviewer comments follow one template, so the wording is identical in every tool:

The comments name one rule each. The full policy appears nowhere in the data any tool received.

The app model isn't fully deterministic at temperature 0. Each tool's run got between 6 and 8 of the 15 traffic tickets right, so the tools received 7 to 9 thumbs-down each. The holdout check below repeats every measurement three times.

All four tools accept feedback on a trace from TypeScript. They differ in how a second person, the reviewer, adds the correct answer.

LangSmith stores both as feedback on a run. The thumbs-down runs go into an annotation queue, where a reviewer adds feedback with a comment:

Langfuse stores both as scores. An annotation queue needs a score config first:

Phoenix stores both as span annotations. The reviewer's comment goes in explanation:

Phoenix stores spans asynchronously. Our first run annotated a span before Phoenix had stored it, and the API returned a 404. The script now waits until all 15 spans are visible.

AcruxCore stores both as feedback rows on the trace. The source field separates the end user from the reviewer:

This is the first step where the code differs a lot. Each tool can make a dataset, but only one builds it from feedback in one call.

In LangSmith, a run query finds the thumbs-down runs. Then createExamples copies them into a dataset. You decide where the reviewer's comment goes. We put it in the reference output:

LangSmith can also do this without code. An automation rule in the UI can add runs that match a filter to a dataset.

In Langfuse, the scores API finds the thumbs-down traces. Two details cost us a run each. A boolean score is filtered with value: "false", not 0. And the trace ID is returned only when you ask for the subject field group:

In Phoenix, getSpans can't filter by annotation. So the script lists the app's spans, reads their annotations, and keeps the ones with a thumbs-down. Then createDataset takes the examples. We kept the source span ID in the metadata.

In AcruxCore, buildFromFeedback takes the reviewer's feedback IDs. Each comment becomes that example's grading criteria, and the input comes from the variables captured on the trace:

Each tool scored the first version of the prompt on its own dataset. The judge read the reviewer's comment and the router's reply, and returned 1 for a match and 0 for a mismatch.

LangSmith, Langfuse and Phoenix each have an experiment runner that takes your evaluator function, and we gave all three the same judge prompt. AcruxCore grades with its built-in judge inside the optimize run. All four used the same judge model.

The first version fails almost every reviewed case. That is expected, because these are the cases users marked wrong. A score of 1 means the app model got that case right on the second call, or the judge accepted a wrong reply. We didn't check which.

The judge can also run without your code. Besides the in-code evaluator we used, Langfuse has a managed LLM-as-a-judge that runs on production traces and on experiments, with your own judge prompt. LangSmith has online evaluators, which you set up in its UI, for the same job.

An optimizer reads the failing cases and the reviewer comments, writes new versions of the prompt, and scores each one with the judge. This is the step that turns feedback into a better prompt, and it's where the four tools differ most.

LangSmith's API reference lists an optimize prompt job (POST /api/v1/repos/optimize-job). It takes a prompt name, a dataset name, evaluator IDs and an algorithm, promptim or demo. No method in the TypeScript SDK wraps it, so we called it with fetch. We gave it a code evaluator that checks the reply against the reviewer's correct answer.

The job was accepted and reported successful about ten seconds later. It ran no experiment on the dataset. With auto_commit set to true, it added no new commit to the prompt. We found no guide for this endpoint, so we may have configured it wrongly, but the job record and its logs gave no error to act on.

LangSmith's documented route to prompt optimization is promptim, which its README calls an experimental library. It is a Python package with a CLI, and it can add a human review step through LangSmith annotation queues.

Langfuse has no optimizer. Its blog shows a workflow with Claude Code agent skills that reads Langfuse data and edits the prompt. That workflow runs in a coding agent, not in your app's code.

Phoenix's Optimize Prompts Automatically tutorial uses Arize's prompt-learning package, which you clone from GitHub and install with pip. The Phoenix TypeScript client has no optimizer export, and the tutorial runs outside Phoenix. Arize's commercial platform, Arize AX, has prompt optimization built in.

For Python teams, there are more options: Opik's Agent Optimizer and DSPy both rewrite prompts from a dataset and a metric. This article covers TypeScript.

Its optimize run takes the dataset and drafts three rewrites of the production prompt. It runs each rewrite and the current production version on every example, and the judge grades all of them:

The three rewrites tied, so we took candidate A. Its rationale: "Added explicit priority rules for each category and clarified that access-blocking issues are P0, billing errors are P1, and feature requests are P2, overriding tone/urgency." The Improve a prompt from feedback guide covers the dashboard version of this step.

A rewrite that passes the cases it was built from can still fail on new tickets. So we ran both prompts on the 15 holdout tickets, three times each, outside every tool. The script calls the model directly and checks the category and the priority against the expected answer. No judge is involved.

Both come from one line in the rewrite: "P1 for bugs without workaround." That rule isn't in the team's policy, and no reviewer wrote it. The optimizer made it up to explain the examples it had. There was also no outage in the traffic set's thumbs-down cases, because the first version routed that one correctly. So no comment ever said that a full outage is P0.

The optimizer learns only from what users marked wrong. Anything they never marked stays a guess, and the rationale won't tell you which rules are guesses. Read the rewritten prompt before you promote it.

In LangSmith, Langfuse and Phoenix, shipping a new version is a tag or label move. We showed those calls in the prompt-management article. Here there was no rewrite to ship, so the step didn't apply.

In AcruxCore, promotion commits the candidate as a new prompt version and moves the production alias to it. Nothing else moves:

The app renders production on every call, so the next ticket used version 2 without a deploy.

Use LangSmith if your team already works in it and writes Python. Feedback, queues, datasets and experiments all work from TypeScript, and promptim covers optimization from Python.

Use Langfuse if you want a managed judge that runs on live traces with your own prompt. Plan to write the dataset glue code and the rewrite step yourself.

Use Phoenix if you already trace with OpenTelemetry and annotate spans. Plan to move to Python, or to Arize AX, for optimization.

Use AcruxCore if you want all six steps from TypeScript in one tool, including the rewrite. Keep a human on the promote button.

#10487. The ‘recession indicators’ visible at your workplace

People all over the U.S. have been sharing their “personal recession indicators” in recent months, from only being able to afford Goldfish crackers and water for lunch to filling up their cars with a few dollars’ worth of gas at a time.

According to employment attorney Craig Levey, a partner at Bennett & Belfort, there are recession indicators at your workplace, too—you just have to take a look around. And they could be a sign that bigger cutbacks, or even layoffs, could be around the corner.

Levey makes content on TikTok, where he tracks patterns in his comments and feed that highlight how people feel about the world of work.

“Over the last few months, I noticed that a lot of companies were suffering financial issues. There was a downturn in their numbers,” he tells Fast Company. “People were trying to assess whether a recession was happening in the United States.”

Levey posted a video about workplace recession indicators that employees should look out for, including companies slashing expenses on business trips, bonuses, holiday parties, and even office snacks. Companies often start with the little things when tightening their belts, he says, and these cuts could be an early signal that a business is preparing for tougher economic conditions.

“Upper management and leadership, they probably get together and they say, ‘Okay, we need to cut costs. What can we do? Let’s cut the low-hanging fruit,’” he says.

Cutting now to save later

Companies do this, Levey says, because they’re looking six, 12, or even 18 months ahead, and taking preemptive steps to put themselves in a better position for the next fiscal year.

These cuts may seem trivial to some. But for other employees, they can be incredibly important. Levey says he regularly hears from people who have full-time jobs but have to live in their cars because they can no longer afford rent.

“A lot of employees are really struggling because the day-to-day costs are so high that they can’t pay their bills,” Levey says. “If you’re somebody who can’t pay their rent, trying to figure out how you’re going to get your next meal, and you can go into your company’s kitchen and get a snack—that makes a big difference.”

According to Chris Marvel Davis, cofounder of Pivot, a training and development provider that runs workshops on wellness, burnout, and leadership, snacks may be what people notice first. But there are other, less obvious signs that a company is becoming more cautious with its spending. 

Travel and conference requests getting denied or delayed may not be immediately apparent, he tells Fast Company. Other signs include open roles staying unfilled, job postings disappearing, backfills for departed employees being paused, and contractors being let go before full-time employees.

“Small purchases need bigger approvals,” he adds. “When a $200 expense suddenly needs a director’s sign-off, someone in finance is watching cash closely.”

Is it routine or a sign of something bigger?

Companies make these kinds of cuts first because they’re easier to reverse, Davis says. On their own, they may simply suggest that leadership is being careful.

“They buy leadership time to see how bad things might get without alarming employees or customers,” he says. “One sign by itself doesn’t tell you much, but several at once are worth taking seriously.”

People should also pay attention to what happens the next time someone leaves, Chiquita Hall-Jackson, an employment attorney at Hall-Jackson & Associates, tells Fast Company.

“If the company leaves the position vacant, divides the work among the remaining team, and still expects the same output, that tells you more about its staffing plans than a cut to the travel budget,” she says. “When that pattern shows up across teams, it’s worth asking whether leadership is planning to operate with fewer people.”

According to Hall-Jackson, it’s also a good idea to look at what’s being cut and how long the cuts last. A canceled off-site meeting may save money once, but a hiring freeze changes how the business operates.

“One budget decision can have plenty of explanations,” she says. “Several decisions that consistently reduce staffing or hours are more meaningful.”

Another potential warning sign is a change in the company’s management structure. If there are fewer middle managers than there used to be, and larger teams are now reporting to more senior supervisors, that could suggest the company is looking to operate with fewer people, Levey says.

It’s more important than ever to have leverage

Of course, many of these signs could just mean that an individual company isn’t doing well and may not indicate a wider, looming recession. 

But in this tough economy and job market, employees should consider being more sensitive to changes at work that may hint at something bigger happening, Levey says. He adds that it’s important to maintain a sense of where you stand within the company, how secure your position is, and what the general temperature is when it comes to potential layoffs.

“You want to pay attention to messages from the CEO or upper management. Because if you think that your job is in jeopardy, then while you’re still employed there and making a wage, you’ve got to start looking elsewhere to see what your other options are,” Levey says.

The worst thing is being blindsided, so it’s always a good idea to make sure you have some leverage, he notes. “It doesn’t mean you need to microanalyze every single thing that happens with the company. But be a realist. Keep your eyes open, really understand what’s going on. That way, you’re less surprised if these things happen.”

#10485. Make money or make a difference

Early in your career, you’ve probably been told you have to choose: Make money, or make a difference. Do well first. Then, once you’ve earned it, do good through philanthropy.

I believed that for most of my career. I spent decades on Wall Street, eventually becoming a partner in one of the country’s leading alternative investment firms, where I oversaw its real estate business.

Simultaneously, I spent years as a philanthropist. Both paths left me unsatisfied. Growing net worth felt hollow, and many of the charities I funded treated symptoms without “curing the disease.”

A FALSE CHOICE

What I’ve learned since is that the choice is false. Some of the best investment opportunities for the next generation sit inside society’s hardest problems. They start with the foundations every community depends on: housing, education, and healthcare. That’s where my firm invests, because when those foundations are strong, everything built on them gets stronger too. The investors who see this opportunity first will do well, and they’ll help reshape our world. They will build economic success and solve societal problems at the same time.

When Magic Johnson and I launched our first urban real estate fund in 1998, I thought raising the capital would take six months. It took two-and-a-half years. Investors believed then, as many still do, that attaching a social goal to an investment means sacrificing returns. We proved them wrong. Over 15 years, those funds raised $2 billion, facilitated $6 billion in urban revitalization, and created tens of thousands of jobs while generating strong returns. Potential investors also didn’t believe that an athlete could be a financial success of this scale. Yet today, Magic is a billionaire and proved those doubters wrong. 

WHAT IMPACT INVESTING LOOKS LIKE

Done right, impact investing can deliver better risk-adjusted returns than traditional strategies. This type of investing targets large, growing, unmet needs in markets less tied to the broader economy, where government and philanthropy have historically been the only players.

Take workforce housing. In 2023, over 21 million renter households (49.7 percent of renter households) spent at least 30 percent of their income on housing. Households spending more than 50 percent are considered severely cost-burdened. I’m talking about teachers, police officers, firefighters, and healthcare workers who earn too much for subsidized housing but not enough to buy a home. They don’t need a handout. They need housing that’s within reach.

Building new affordable housing at market-rate returns without public incentives is nearly impossible. Meanwhile, existing affordable units keep disappearing as investors buy them, renovate, and raise rents on the people who can least afford it.

We flipped the model by buying existing properties and keeping rents affordable. Then we offer reduced rent to teachers, law enforcement officers, and healthcare workers who live on-site and provide tutoring, community safety, and health programs in return. 

The result is pride in rentership. When residents feel invested in where they live, they stay longer and treat the property better. In our first housing fund, lease terms increased 32%, crime fell 48%, and economic loss dropped 37%. Net operating income grew 13%, and the fund delivered a 25% internal rate of return, all without raising rents.

The social outcome drove the financial outcome.

IMPACT MARKETING

As impact investing grows, so does impact marketing. Plenty of funds now put “impact” on the label without changing anything underneath. Here are four things to look for.

1. Ask where the impact shows up in the financials. If the social goal could be cut without touching returns, it’s an add-on, and it will be the first thing cut when times get tight. Real impact drives the business. In our housing, the community programs are why tenants stay longer, which is why the numbers work.

2. Look for measured outcomes, not good intentions. Lease duration, crime rates, graduation rates, patient outcomes. If a fund can’t show you the data, the impact is a story, not a strategy.

3. Follow the demand. Durable impact investments meet an existing and growing need. Be wary of anything that depends on creating demand or on a single subsidy that could disappear.

4. Look at who’s on the team. Ask whether the people making decisions understand the problem firsthand. Our team includes former teachers, physicians, law enforcement officers, and frontline workers alongside recovering bankers. That expertise is how we spot financial and social risk before it costs anyone.

FINAL THOUGHTS

Too many Americans have their health, education, and economic outcomes determined by where they were born. That isn’t sustainable, and government and philanthropy can’t solve it alone.

If you’re early in your career, you have something my generation didn’t: proof that you don’t have to choose. You can build a career and a portfolio around solving the problems that matter most, and get paid for doing it.

That’s the conversation I want to have with the next generation of investors. Impact investing has never been more critical, and it has never been more rewarding.

Bobby Turner is founder of Turner Impact Capital.

#10484. Developer creates a 100% valid ZIP file that doubles as a BIOS-bootable operating system

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

The original definition of "hacking" was making hardware and software do something it wasn't supposed to.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Hacking stories these days mostly involve someone using an army of AI agents to find vulnerabilities in networks and software that were carefully hidden in plain sight. Today, for a change, we have a story about hacking in its original meaning — twisting computing artifacts into purposes not in their design spec. David Oberhollenzer, aka Goliath, a greybeard in spirit if not in shade, crafted a 100% valid ZIP file that's simultaneously an archive, BIOS-bootable, and a Multiboot-compatible OS image.

You can unpack the ZIP file just like any other, boot it using Linux's GRUB, QEMU, or any other Multiboot-compatible loader (even if imaged to different media), or alternatively, perform the finest party trick: image-write it to a floppy and boot from it directly from BIOS on any compatible PC, without modification.

Much like me, that sequence of words may be triggering a head-tilt reminiscent of a confused puppy, so we'll explain. The first thing you need to know is that unlike most file types, the metadata (in this case, file directory) of a ZIP is actually at the end of the archive. Up to that point, the data is comprised of every compressed file in sequence.

The file packs all have a predictable structure: "PK" followed by bytes 03 04, metadata (including name), some extra/comment fields, and then the file's contents. Notably, any file can be stored uncompressed, and Goliath made use of that for the very first file, called ZIPHEAD.COM — remember, this is the start of the ZIP archive.

Goliath said that it was "easy" to make the ZIP file a valid Multiboot system image. A valid image only needs to contain bytes 0x1BADB002 in the first 8 KiB, followed by some flags and a checksum. It was trivial to place that datum in ZIPHEAD's data, along with the location of where the bootloader lies.

Grub or QEMU don't care about anything else in the image, as once they have the initialization information, from that point it's the bootloader's problem. The actual bootloader, predictably, is in ZIPHEAD's uncompressed data, and the image will happily boot directly, or, say, from a floppy.

Making the same exact archive bootable via a standard PC BIOS took a little more work and some clever shenanigans. Making a disk bootable is simple enough: the BIOS reads the first exact 512 bytes of the disk's contents, and if they end with bytes 0x55AA, then it's bootable, and the BIOS starts executing whatever code is the first byte.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Placing 0x55AA precisely at position 510 of the ZIP, already in ZIPHEAD's contents, is once again simple, and won't corrupt the Multiboot image, as if you'll recall, the Multiboot spec is flexible with data locations. But you may wonder: once the BIOS runs code in sequence starting from the ZIP's first byte, then ZIPHEAD's header ("PK" + 0x0304, plus all metadata) surely is garbage for practical purposes, and not proper code? Well, yes, but actually no.

The first four bytes represent CPU instructions that don't write anything to memory, so they're harmless, other than a push-to-stack. The next few belong to ZIPHEAD's metadata, and Goliath massaged those values to represent instructions that would either be innocuous or perform operations he could use, namely using the modification time bytes as instructions to jump further down the data.

Sounds simple, but the actual process of choosing bytes that represent (1) non-crashy CPU instructions, (2) useful code for setting up the boot process, and (3) still represent a valid file header in a ZIP archive is far trickier than the above. Do go and check out the full blog post if you want all the details.

Download the Tom's Hardware app for up-to-the-minute updates on iOS and Android. Also follow Tom's Hardware on Google News, or add us as a preferred source, to get our latest news, analysis, & reviews in your feeds.

Bruno Ferreira is a contributing writer for Tom's Hardware. He has decades of experience with PC hardware and assorted sundries, alongside a career as a developer. He's obsessed with detail and has a tendency to ramble on the topics he loves. When not doing that, he's usually playing games, or at live music shows and festivals.

#10482. What’s happening with banks today? Chase, Bank of America, Wells Fargo customers report widespread outages

If you’re having trouble accessing your bank account today, you’re not alone.

Customers at several U.S. financial giants reported problems accessing their accounts on Monday as widespread service disruptions appear to have hit Chase, Bank of America, Wells Fargo, and other financial institutions.

The problems started Monday morning, November 16, when thousands of customers began reporting trouble logging in to their Chase accounts, checking their balances, and completing transactions.

According to outage-tracking website Downdetector, Chase appeared to be the hardest hit, with more than 4,000 reports by early Monday afternoon. But Chase wasn’t alone: Bank of America and Wells Fargo also saw spikes in customer complaints, along with U.S. Bank and payment service Zelle.

Reached for comment by Fast Company, a Chase spokesperson said that a technical issue impacted not only Chase but also other banks it does business with.

“JPMorgan Chase has experienced a temporary issue related to a software bug in on-premise hardware systems—there is no evidence this is cyber-related,” the spokesperson said in a statement. “It is taking longer than usual to service some customers. This is not a systemic issue—other banks may have been impacted by slowness in transactions only to the extent that they were a party to a transaction with us. Customers still have access to their accounts and funds. We’re working diligently to resolve this by the end of the day today.”

Fast Company has also reached out to the other banks for more details.

The banking troubles come after multiple separate major technology disruptions in recent days, including a widespread Microsoft Outlook outage on Thursday and a nationwide Federal Aviation Administration (FAA) ground stop Friday, disrupting air travel across the country.

There’s no confirmed connection between those incidents and Monday’s banking problem.

Is Chase down?

Chase customers began reporting problems around 9:48 a.m. ET Monday, according to Downdetector.

Logging into the website was among the most common complaints, although some customers also ran into issues with the mobile app and viewing their account balances and transaction histories.

And the problems are not limited to online banking.

Some customers on Reddit reported trouble accessing services at physical Chase branches, suggesting the disruption could extend beyond the bank’s website and app.

At one New York City branch visited by Fast Company on Monday, Chase associates were alerting customers as they entered the door that systems were down.

Issues at Bank of America, Wells Fargo, and other financial giants

While possibly the worst hit, Chase wasn’t the only bank having a rough Monday, though Chase indicated that those issues were related to its own technical issues.

Downdetector also recorded a jump in outage reports involving Bank of America, Wells Fargo, U.S. Bank, and Truist. Some customers said they were unable to view transactions or complete payments and transfers.

Zelle, the popular person-to-person payment service used by many major banks, also saw more outage complaints.

It’s worth keeping in mind that Downdetector relies on reports submitted by users. A spike in complaints doesn’t necessarily mean a bank’s entire system is down, and the numbers don’t reflect the total number of customers affected.

#10480. Nearly half of Americans are making the same gamble with their holiday travel plans

Air travel is getting more and more expensive. And as prices continue to soar, American travelers are becoming desperate for deals on flights. Oftentimes, they’re even delaying bookings or totally altering holiday travel in order to score reasonably priced plane tickets.

The findings come from a new report from the online travel agency and flight consolidator CheapOair, which surveyed 1,000 U.S. consumers to understand how travelers are making decisions around holiday travel promotions. The survey found that modern day fliers are more willing to book flights at the last minute, often waiting for promotions that happen on Black Friday, Cyber Monday, and Travel Tuesday. In fact, nearly half (49%) of the survey’s respondents said they have delayed bookings in order to shop holiday travel deals. Another 60% say they are likely to consider doing so in the future.

Still, while fliers are perhaps more deal-driven than ever before, they’re not consistently convinced that they’ll be able to find true savings. More than a quarter (26%) said they believe holiday deals are mostly marketing hype, whereas 17% believe the savings are totally legit. Another 57% say the deals are a mixed bag of both savings and hype.

It’s not all that surprising that consumer confidence is fading when it comes to shopping for deals, however. As companies increasingly utilize tactics like surveillance pricing—which relies on using consumer data and algorithms to adjust prices on things like groceries, home items, and even flights—many people worry about how transparent modern businesses are being with their marketing tactics. 

Last year, Delta Air Lines announced it would more widely deploy the method of price-setting. “The practice of dynamic pricing is certainly not new in the airline industry,” Kerry Tan, professor of economics at Loyola University Maryland, previously told Fast Company of the announcement. “Certainly, Delta, as with any other company, is profit-driven and stands to gain” from the tactic,” he said. 

Tan added: “As an economist, we assume that companies are profit maximizers, and so in a way I see this as companies really making a push toward that objective of profit maximization.”

It’s also not that surprising that customers are increasingly worried about the cost of air travel. That’s true because prices are undoubtedly reaching staggering new highs. According to federal data, airfare was up 22% this past September when compared with the year before. A September 2026 report from Hopper media also found that round-trip domestic airfare for Thanksgiving travel is up 31% from 2025, while Christmas travel is already up by 23%.

As prices keep climbing, cost is by far the biggest concern consumers have when it comes to holiday travel. Per the CheapOair survey, 39% say it’s their biggest worry compared with other concerns like flight delays and cancellations (18%), crowded airports (15%), and winter weather disrupting their plans (10%).

Still, savvy shoppers aren’t just sitting back and fretting. Some are altering their holiday travel plans altogether. While changing their travel destinations is an unpopular choice, with only 7% saying they’re open to the change, 43% will change their preferred travel dates in order to land a better price.

While it’s not unheard of to book flights on cheaper travel days, holiday travelers who do so risk missing out on family traditions or planned events. However, with soaring energy costs making travel less affordable than ever, missing the family football game or even the whole turkey dinner might be what it takes to go home for the holidays.

#10478. Benefits need to change with the times

Over the next five years, AI will change the people companies need and the work they do. At the same time, medical costs will keep taking more of the benefits budget. Employers will have to rethink what it means to take care of their people.

The change is already underway. Agents write code, run tests, and carry out research assignments. OpenAI reports that an internal model has resolved more than 100 long-standing math problems.

For many companies, though, there’s still a gap between giving individuals better tools and changing how the business runs. Agents need access to company information, permission to act, and a way to get help when something goes wrong. Research co-led by Chicago Fed economist Ezra Karger points to uneven adoption as a reason technical progress takes time to reach the economy.

Here’s what I anticipate: By 2028, expect more tasks to be handed to AI, with people checking the results. By 2030, companies will reorganize around that work. By 2032, agents will carry assignments across departments, bringing people in when a decision or approval is needed.

WORK CHANGES AT DIFFERENT SPEEDS

Software, law, and consulting will move fastest. As coding, research, and drafting take fewer hours, people will focus more on deciding what matters and checking the work. Firms will have to figure out how people learn to become experts when the work that used to teach them has been automated. Stanford research already finds weaker hiring for young workers in AI-exposed jobs, although it does not establish causation.

Physical work will change more slowly. Better planning helps a manufacturer, but someone still has to maintain the equipment. Patients still need nurses, and homes still need electricians. Anthropic’s scenarios show how cheaper knowledge work can support demand for hands-on work.

MEDICAL COSTS KEEP CLIMBING

Meanwhile, healthcare keeps getting more expensive. Aon expects costs to rise 9.5% in 2027 before employers take steps to offset the increase. CMS projects health spending growing faster than GDP through 2034. KFF finds workers’ contributions toward family coverage rose 9% this year.

Employers can spend considerably more and still deliver bad news at open enrollment. Employees see a bigger deduction from their paycheck. Explaining that the increase would otherwise have been worse doesn’t make it feel like a better benefit.

Routine assignments will take fewer hours, and companies will depend more on people with experience. Losing someone with hard-earned judgment or trusted customer relationships will be harder for a lean team to absorb.

Good medical coverage remains essential. But competing on medical alone is becoming a losing proposition.

Employers are trying to get medical spending under control through allowances for individual insurance, higher-deductible plans, and medical captives that pool large-claim risk. These approaches change how costs and risks are shared. They don’t stop medical inflation.

LIFE NAVIGATION GIVES PEOPLE PRACTICAL HELP

Non-medical benefits offer more room to make each dollar count. In MetLife’s survey published earlier this year, 73% of employers called them the most cost-effective way to support employee well-being.

One way to put that support into practice is life navigation: someone who helps find care for a parent, sort out a disputed bill, or handle a family emergency. The idea is simple. Give people one place to turn. Someone will do the research, make the calls, and stay with the problem until it’s sorted out.

AI can handle routine legwork, but human support still matters when life gets complicated.

Leave and PTO, housing assistance, income protection, and financial well-being support helps employers stand out. So does life navigation. People remember who helped when life got hard. That matters when deciding where to work—and whether to stay.

Jon Cooper is CEO of Overalls.

#10476. Fed chair weighs AI’s economic impact, but sidesteps inflation risk

Artificial intelligence could accelerate innovation and usher in an era of faster economic growth, Federal Reserve Chairman Kevin Warsh said Monday in remarks that also acknowledged risks from the technology.

The adoption of AI could also lead to widespread job loss or a steep drop in the stock market if the healthy returns expected from the huge investments in AI infrastructure don’t materialize, Warsh said. But he did not mention inflation, even though many of his colleagues blame the buildout of data centers for accelerating price increases.

Warsh’s remarks kicked off a one-day conference on AI and productivity at the Fed’s San Francisco branch. The Fed chairman did not comment on what the Fed’s next steps would be on interest rates, nor did he say anything about recent government data showing that inflation accelerated last month. That data led many of Warsh’s colleagues on the Fed’s rate-setting committee to signal their support for additional interest rate hikes, including at the Fed’s next meeting in December.

“What might set SI apart, as an economic influence, is that it could supercharge idea generation,” Warsh said. “It appears to be changing the method—and, at least as important, the speed—of innovation. If idea generation broadens, growth could be sustained at a level substantially higher than the long-run average.”

The Fed refers to AI as “super intelligence” following an executive order issued by President Donald Trump requiring federal agencies to use the term.

Yet Warsh also spent more time in his remarks considering the downsides of AI than he has in previous speeches. The chairman has previously touted AI as a technology that could significantly boost the economy’s efficiency, which would enable it to grow without worsening inflation. In turn, the Fed could potentially lower interest rates under that scenario.

“As policymakers, we have to be mindful that great opportunities rarely come risk-free,” Warsh said. “And the risks in this case, if overlooked, can be highly disruptive and economically costly.”

For example, “after significant capital investment and stock market gains, expected returns could turn down and equity valuations could fall, leading to an economic slowdown and a more notable rise in unemployment,” he said. Fed staff has begun evaluating different scenarios for how AI’s impact may play out, Warsh added.

The conference Monday was part of a task force Warsh set up to evaluate AI and its impact on the economy’s efficiency. Warsh appointed five different task forces soon after taking the helm at the Fed.

Warsh on Monday did not address AI’s impact on inflation, a top concern of many of his Fed colleagues. Last week, several policymakers expressed concern that AI is fanning inflation because the buildout of AI infrastructure has sharply raised demand for computer chips, electronic components, electricity, and copper.

Those increases, in turn, have lifted the cost of smartphones, computers, and other consumer electronics. Smartphone prices rose last month at the fastest pace on record, the government said last week.

In addition, Fed Vice Chair Philip Jefferson said Friday that if AI does accelerate economic growth, it could lead to higher interest rates over time, even if inflation declines.

“If the new technology leads to permanently higher levels of productivity growth, it may increase firms’ desire to invest and, hence, their demand for funding, raising the level of interest rates,” Jefferson said.

—By Christopher Rugaber, AP economics writer

#10474. Kevin O’Leary tells Gen Z: ‘Every generation has had whiners like you’

Shark Tank investor and businessman Kevin O’Leary is at odds with Gen Z, who say building wealth and success is harder for their generation than it was for his.

In Jubilee Media’s latest Surrounded video—an online series where a single public figure faces 25 individuals with opposing viewpoints—O’Leary, known as “Mr. Wonderful,” takes on a slate of questions regarding success and wealth.

But it’s one particular conversation in the nearly two-hour-long video that has gained traction on social media.

When the discussion turned to O’Leary’s assertion that “if you want to be successful, work-life balance comes second,” one participant pushed back on how realistic that advice is today.

“We used to have work-life balance—we don’t have that anymore,” one of the participants told O’Leary. “My question to you is, how do you believe that our generation is supposed to be successful and have a work-life balance if we can’t even afford what success would look like, which is owning a home and not being straddled by student loans and medical debt.”

O’Leary quickly replied, “Every single generation has had whiners like you in it. Each generation is surprised to find out things change in the economy all the time.”

Twenty years ago, O’Leary continued, a liberal arts or humanities degree could be considered an “economic disaster,” whereas today, videographers and storytellers are “making anywhere from $200,000 to $400,000 working for corporations.”

‘The kids . . . have totally given up’

As the clip made the rounds on social media, some agreed with O’Leary’s stance on the matter, echoing the age-old claim that younger generations are less willing to work than their older counterparts.

“Not a huge Kevin O’Leary guy, but the kids he’s speaking with have totally given up. They need to keep pushing, they break through or change careers,” one user said on X.

But for most, Mr. Wonderful’s comments came across as disconnected from reality.

“With a straight face, Kevin said “photographers, videographers are making $200-$400k a year.” Out of touch doesn’t even begin to describe this guy’s ignorance,” one user replied on YouTube.

“Kevin O’Leary needs to go walk in the shoes of a middle-class American for a week. He has no idea what he’s talking about,” another added on X.

For others, the conversation further highlighted the tensions between generations.

A user on X added: “Kevin’s generation got theirs, bought houses cheap, got money to build businesses, got cheap college, then changed the rules to fuck every other generation after them.”

The video and the discourse surrounding it also pointed to a larger problem: Neither side can reach consensus.

A user added: “Problem is, the truth is somewhere in the middle of both tales being told, and nobody wants to acknowledge that their respective sides are both overstating the opposing ends of the equation.”
