#10438. Samsung’s paid Galaxy AI plan has a giant Gemini problem

Good morning. Today I’m trying desperately not to buy Sony’s new nostalgia-bait headphones, reeling from how emotionally invested I was in Spider-Man: Brand New Day (yes, I know I’m late), trying to figure out what a “private, agentic notebook” is, and slowly coming around to the idea of the New York Times owning Letterboxd.

Inbox is written by Daniel Bader. Read previous issues here, and then catch up with Ben Schoon’s excellent Weekender column published every Sunday.

The relationship between Samsung and Google has, over the years, waxed and waned depending on their respective interdependence. Samsung is Android’s physical emissary, powering a plurality of all its handsets globally. But Google, and in particular its services, is the glue that holds Samsung’s smartphone experience together.

The back and forth is over a decade old; back in early 2014, Samsung shipped a refreshed magazine-style skin with its Galaxy tablets that eschewed many of the design tenets Google had been pushing its partners to adhere to. Not only was Samsung willfully defying its most important partner, but it made a point of barely mentioning Android during its press conferences.

Around the same time, it transitioned its smartwatches from Android Wear to Tizen and began shipping duplicative apps on its devices (many of which persist to this day). The goal was to distance itself from Google as much as possible.

While we don’t know exactly what happened behind closed doors, we saw the outcome quite clearly: Magazine UX lasted one product cycle, and the two companies signed a 10-year cross-license patent deal, which may have helped Samsung prevent any future lawsuits with Apple. Google then sold Motorola to Lenovo, clearing the way for Samsung to continue building its burgeoning hardware business on the back of Google’s mobile ecosystem.

Since then, the two companies have grown even closer. In 2021, they co-announced a new Wear OS along with an overarching wearables partnership that has since grown to include Android XR as well. And Google is still the default search engine for Samsung devices, despite reported inroads made by Microsoft in 2023 to convince the Korean giant to switch to a ChatGPT-powered version of Bing Search. (Samsung’s ongoing relationship with Microsoft, which bundles its Office suite on most Galaxy devices, could be seen as a point of leverage for negotiations with Google, too.)

This brings us to today, and to AI. Samsung was among the first companies to adopt Google’s full suite of Gemini Intelligence products, and its phones often get new AI features before anyone else. But like a decade ago, when Samsung shipped Bixby to kind-of-sort-of compete with Google Assistant, it still wants to offer its own services alongside Google’s.

So a rumor that Samsung may launch a paid Galaxy AI Premium subscription next year for $5/month piqued my interest for the same reason Magazine UX did 12 years ago. Galaxy AI features today are a blend of first-party and third-party capabilities, but none of them are especially useful, particularly when compared to Google’s own Android equivalents powered by Gemini Intelligence. Now Bar and Now Brief, two heavily promoted features in Samsung’s marketing, are wildly disappointing.

At I/O this past May, Google announced Spark, an agent that goes out and performs actions on your behalf. And while it’s since been somewhat eclipsed by more capable mobile options like Meta’s Muse, it’s obvious that Google is working on a follow-up that will perform functions both on the phone and inside apps.

That’s exactly what Samsung is rumored to be working on with Bixby Action, a spiritual successor to its namesake that, when it debuted, prioritized on-device tasks like turning on Wi-Fi over web-based actions Google Assistant did better.

According to leaks from SamMobile, Galaxy AI Premium will offer agentic upgrades for phone users to “create commands triggers and system routines for daily tasks” or “automate routine daily tasks using Galaxy AI.” Gen Video will reportedly “convert static photos into dynamic video clips,” while a new Daily Briefing likely does what it says on the tin.

Will Samsung users see enough value in this hodgepodge of features paywalled only because they use a surfeit of compute? And what models will power them on the backend?

Right now, Google is in the enviable position of being able to offer its AI at three distinct layers: the model itself, which is invisible to the user; the OS level, which can promulgate Gemini across every Android phone that runs Google services; and the hardware level, where Pixel lets it specialize and optimize.

Samsung, not particularly known for its software prowess, will likely need to step gingerly around Google’s AI shadow while also justifying the cost to shore up its much-needed services revenue. Based on this leaked roadmap, though, I’m not confident it will work out.

Instead, Samsung may want to acquire one of the many up-and-coming AI agent startups whose valuations are rising more quickly than their user bases. While Muse and OpenAI’s Dots have taken up most of the recent conversation, I’ve been using Hark, Town, and Instinct pretty regularly for a month or so and could see them being brought in-house by Samsung and offered either as standalone products or customized for Galaxy devices. As Google is doing with its own AI plans, Samsung could provide Galaxy users a year of free service, after which they would be expected to pay.

Samsung doesn’t have a great history of building software, but many of its acquisitions, from SmartThings to Viv Labs (which became “next-gen” Bixby) to LoopPay (which became Samsung Pay) to Whisk (which became Samsung Food), have proven successful. Given the current urgent state of mobile AI, I’d forgo the hodgepodge and bring in some outside expertise.

Jensen Huang’s affair with an alleged Chinese spy is the only thing tech media can talk about right now, likely because it has implications far beyond just Nvidia’s stock price. The Nvidia CEO, who is one of the world’s richest and most powerful tech executives, is believed to have been targeted by Chinese intelligence to potentially influence American trade policy around AI.

Alex Kantrowitz’s Big Technology piece is a useful starting point for why this matters at a global scale:

Beyond the affair, the real issue is that Huang’s love interest, ex-Megaspeed International executive Alice Huang, is someone the United States government believes passed information to the Chinese government while building data centers with Nvidia’s help in Asia. There’s simply no getting away from this detail. This isn’t just an affair, it’s a corporate and national security issue.

Whether Huang can survive the impending investigation by Nvidia’s board remains to be seen, but the CEO has been a stalwart in the tech space for 33 years, and there is no clear successor to his position should he be forced to resign.

Damien reviewed DroidDeck, which I wrote about last week as part of a broader look at Android’s gaming renaissance.

Over the last few days, things have become even more preposterous: the Ocarina of Time remake, which was released on Thursday, has already been recompiled to run natively on Android (though it runs better on PC), largely with the help of frontier AI models.

All of these AI-powered shenanigans come after Anthropic said it would stop making it possible to recreate copies of games, claiming that “recreating someone else’s specific game using Claude to infringe IP is against our usage policy.” Good thing there are no other models capable of doing the same thing.

I think when you zoom out and look at production, AI really has the potential to be the biggest creative unlock in modern history. The last time we saw anything close to this was really the, you know, advent of CGI and digital composites in the early ’90s. That technology gave birth to amazing stories like Jurassic Park and Terminator 2, and it’s hard to imagine that AI is not going to have a more significant impact than that. And I personally believe that’s going to be an incredible unlock on creativity.

—David Ellison, Skydance CEO, when asked about how AI could impact the movie-making process

#10436. Meta issues a high-priority update to prevent some glasses from getting ‘too warm’ when wet [U]

The latest Meta Glasses (not Ray-Ban or Oakley-branded) may temporarily overheat if they get too wet, but a software patch is here to help prevent that.

Folks using Meta Glasses in the Adventurer, Fury, Starfire, Nova, or Capri styles should open the Meta AI app on their smartphones and get their glasses updated as soon as possible. Meta sent out an email to all Glasses owners noting that the company has “an important software update” related to performance when “excessive moisture or sweat” enters the device. Meta specifically says this issue does not affect Ray-Ban or Oakley-branded Meta glasses.

Meta sent out an email to all Meta Glasses owners with the tagline “Action required,” noting that this could be a potentially serious issue:

“We’ve implemented a software update to fix a rare issue that caused a small number of Meta Glasses to get too warm when moisture entered the device. It works similarly to a phone shutting off automatically when it needs to cool down. If people experience this, they can contact Meta Customer Service for a replacement.”

The new software update brings your glasses to version 130, which includes a special software hook that shuts them down if they were to get too warm. Meta told me that the temperature for this threshold is quite low, and that the glasses shut off to prevent any potential issues. The company was very specific about the wording in the statement, carefully using the word “warm” instead of “hot,” so as to avoid the stigma that comes with that term on wearable technology.

While I haven’t seen any specific reports of Meta’s newest glasses models overheating, there are plenty of Reddit threads citing overheating issues on Ray-Ban Meta glasses over the years. Ironically, the one post I found related to sweat-related overheating was from a year ago on Ray-Ban Meta glasses, which this software update does not address.

Meta Glasses were launched this summer as a budget-minded alternative to the company’s Ray-Ban and Oakley-branded AI glasses. The glasses start at $299, which is substantially cheaper than the $379 Ray-Ban Meta Gen 2 or the $449 Ray-Ban Meta Gen 3 options. Meta Glasses come in several different styles, including special LISA and Kylie-branded ones, and offer identical functionality to the more expensive Ray-Ban or Oakley options.

#10435. Samsung Gallery officially adds Google Photos backup, but not for all Galaxy users

Samsung has officially launched Google Photos integration in its Gallery app with backup services, and it’s also now widely rolled out for Galaxy devices.

Samsung Gallery started rolling out support for Google Photos backup in September, but it wasn’t widely available at the time. As of this week, though, Samsung is officially announcing the feature as it is now available broadly to Galaxy users.

“Gallery Sync” in the Samsung Gallery app replaces the previous Microsoft OneDrive integration with Google Photos, letting users sign in with their Google account of choice and use the free 15GB or paid Google One storage to back up photos and videos. This allows the Samsung Gallery app to show media backed up from another Galaxy device, but it doesn’t integrate your full Google Photos library.

At this point, Google Photos backup via Samsung Gallery should be broadly available to Galaxy owners. We’re seeing Samsung’s pop-up (pictured above) about the feature on our Galaxy Z Fold 8, with the feature fully functional beyond that.

You can also access setup via Menu > Settings > “Sync photos and videos” in the Samsung Gallery app.

Samsung requires your device to be running One UI 9 (Android 17) to access this feature, though it says that support will be “expanding to additional Galaxy devices in phases.” For the time being, though, that means the feature is a bit limited for users, as the One UI 9 rollout is still in progress.

Once media has been backed up to Google Photos, Samsung Gallery also now has a “free up space” button that can delete the local copies of your photos and videos to open up storage on your phone.

#10434. Amazon’s new Ring cameras are mounted to roving robots, including $250 Drone Cam

Amazon’s Ring brand is today launching a new “Robotics” division that will start with two offerings – a $250 Ring Drone Cam and a $1,000 Rover Cam.

Indoor cameras can be invaluable for securing your home, but also just in keeping an eye on what’s going on when you’re not home. To that end, Ring has been working on cameras that can be sent around the home remotely, and they’re finally launching early next year.

Ring first showed off its Drone Cam in 2020, but never actually brought the product to market. That changes in January of next year, when the Ring Drone Cam will be available for $249.99. The camera, Amazon expains, is “a flying indoor security camera that goes where you need it, when you need it, and stays docked and out of sight when you don’t.” You can set flight routes on a schedule, have the drone automatically investigate tripped sensors in your Ring system, and more. The camera is only active when the drone is in flight, and it’s emphasized that the drone is not “quiet,” it’s “meant to be seen and heard.”

The other robot Amazon’s Ring is launching is the Rover Cam, which is built on the back of Amazon Astro. It has a telescoping camera and rolls around your home on “scheduled patrols” as well as being able to automatically investigate tripped sensors. It also features two-way audio and a built-in siren to deter intruders from an area, while also features continuous 4K recording.

Ring is mainly pitching this one for businesses, and it costs $999.99 with pre-orders open now ahead of a December 16 release date.

#10433. Fable 5.2 creates an underwater world in a browser in only 96 KB

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Elegant effort is reminiscent of both old- and new-school demoscene.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Claude Fable 5.2 was released recently to mostly rave reviews, with both users and analysis outlets ranking it nipping at the heels of the much pricier Opus 5.5. Perhaps as practical proof of that claim, front-end developer Maksymilian Dendura took inspiration from the demoscene and had Fable roll his own virtual underwater world, Abyssal Garden, in only 96 KB of Javascript/HTML — you can check it out here.

The demo's 3D space and textures are all procedurally generated, meaning they're the result of mathematical equations rather than prebuilt 3D meshes, and all calculated on load. Using actual meshes and images for textures would otherwise take up a significant amount of space.

Building structures procedurally is difficult and has limitations, but it's exceedingly effective when used properly. Some games employ this technique for repeated elements like trees and grass.

Likewise, all the visual effects and even some texturing are done with shaders, which are small programs that change how a mesh or texture appears — for example, to make parts of an object shiny. Games use them extensively, as many players are aware, thanks to endless "loading shaders" screens or Unreal Engine's st-tu-tutering.

The result is definitely impressive, especially but not only as it's purely browser-based, relying on WebGPU and WebGL. Both are rarely-mentioned technologies that have actually existed for a long while now. As their names imply, they let developers render 2D and 3D graphics in a manner similar to that of OpenGL, but in a browser, using JavaScript as the base language.

The demo's most impressive fact is the simplicity of the prompt that got Fable 5.2 to build it. It's only about 40 lines long with word wrapping, and clearly illustrates the degree of autonomy that contemporary agentic AI has when given a specific, yet exceedingly open-ended task like this one. It's reasonable enough to assume the author did a few rounds of tweaking (and he has excellent artistic taste too), but the agent's acumen is nevertheless impressive.

Comparing this demo to an actual game is inevitable, but the reality is that demos have but an atom-sized fraction of the content of a full game, and are limited to little or no interaction, logic, or physics.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Performance is decent for what is a great-looking but otherwise tiny and almost featureless space. The author says that he's getting 180 FPS at 1440p on an RTX 5070 Ti. Likewise, having this world in 97 KB sounds impressive, but it's actually gigantic for this type of demo, even if an X user further optimized it for 52 KB.

Regular computing users, including AI neophytes and acolytes, may not be aware that this kind of space-optimized programming for the purposes of visual/aural demonstrations is a discipline and art form that harkens back to the 1980s when 16-bit computers appeared — called the "demoscene."

By no means discounting the extraordinary fact that Fable did this nearly all by itself — which is the actual achievement here — code wizards have pulled off nearly impossible feats decades ago, including in browsers. The 2009 demo Elevatedn (17 years ago) has a comparable aesthetic, and includes music, in just under 4 KB (yes, four). There's even a browser version of it, taking up all of 4,777 bytes.

Hartverdrahtet (2012 — 14 years ago) has a highly complex 3D space, again in 4 KB. And old-timers in the audience will remember ".kkrieger", an FPS game prototype that fit in all of 96 KB, including meshes, textures, music, and sound effects... back in 2004.

#10430. Building Reliable Product Emails Into Your Application

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A password reset email is part of your login flow. An invoice notification is part of your billing experience. Yet development teams often treat these messages as background tasks: render a template, call a sending API, and move on. That approach leaves an important part of the product experience untested until a user cannot complete the action they started.

Testing the application interface alone leaves a gap. A successful request to an email provider does not establish that the recipient can find the message, understand it, or use its links. Product emails need explicit success criteria, useful diagnostic records, and recovery options when something fails.

A reliable workflow combines application tests, controlled mailbox checks, and pre-send diagnostics for authentication and inbox placement. A comparison of available tools can help you identify which checks your existing setup covers and where additional testing would be useful. The goal is to investigate specific gaps rather than add another service without a clear purpose.

Start with the action the recipient needs to complete. A weekly activity summary can tolerate a delay that would make a sign-in code useless. An invoice needs accurate amounts and a clear payment destination. A security alert needs enough context for someone to recognize the event without exposing sensitive information.

For each message, document its trigger, expected timing, required data, and expiration behavior. Decide what the application should display if submission fails or the user requests another copy. These decisions give developers and testers concrete acceptance criteria.

For a password reset, test the complete journey: requesting the message, receiving it in a controlled mailbox, following the link, and setting a new password. Also check what happens when the link expires or has already been used.

Template tests should cover the data your application actually encounters. Try long names, missing optional fields, non-Latin characters, and large order totals. Escape user-provided values appropriately, and make sure conditional sections disappear cleanly when their data is absent.

Inspect both HTML and plain-text versions. Instructions should remain understandable when images are blocked, while descriptive links and readable text should make the next action clear. Check that every URL points to the correct environment, especially when staging and production share template code.

A development sandbox lets you inspect generated messages without contacting customers. Use it to check headers, attachments, and content. Keep a separate rendering check for the email clients your audience uses, because a sandbox preview cannot reproduce every client’s behavior.

A background queue can prevent a slow sending API from delaying a web request. It also creates another failure boundary: a worker may time out after the provider has accepted the email but before your application records the result.

If the job retries blindly, the recipient may receive two messages. Assign a stable identifier to each logical notification and record the provider’s message identifier when available. Where supported, reuse an idempotency key for retries of the same send operation, following the provider’s documented retention window.

Distinguish retries from intentional resends. A user asking for a new reset link is a different event from a worker repeating an uncertain request. Define which link remains valid when another is issued, and test that behavior explicitly.

Retries also need limits. Temporary service failures may justify another attempt with backoff. A permanently invalid address needs a correction or suppression workflow, rather than repeated sending.

Provider events describe different stages of the sending process. An acceptance response means the provider accepted the request. A delivery event generally means the receiving mail server accepted the message; it does not prove inbox placement or that a person read it.

Capture the events your provider exposes, including bounces, delays, and complaints. Associate them with the original notification so support staff can investigate a particular report. Confirm each event’s documented meaning instead of treating similar labels across providers as interchangeable.

Webhook handlers should verify incoming requests using the provider’s documented mechanism and tolerate duplicate notifications. Avoid assuming events always arrive in chronological order. Record enough history to explain what happened without allowing a late event to overwrite a more informative state.

Before releasing a significant template or infrastructure change, send controlled messages through the production sending configuration to test accounts at commonly used mailbox providers. Use synthetic data, and check placement, rendering, link behavior, and how long the message takes to arrive.

Inspect authentication results in the received headers. Published DNS records are only part of the configuration; the actual message must use the expected signing domain and sending infrastructure. If you change providers or domains, repeat these checks rather than assuming the previous results still apply.

Treat test mailbox results as samples. They can reveal problems, but they cannot guarantee identical placement for every recipient. Mailbox settings, filtering decisions, and recipient history can produce different outcomes.

Provide a resend option with sensible rate limits and explain when users should expect the message. Where appropriate, let signed-in users verify or correct their address. For public account-recovery forms, avoid responses that reveal whether a particular address has an account.

Give support staff a way to distinguish a notification that was never generated from a failed submission or a message accepted by the receiving server. Protect that diagnostic access, limit retention, and avoid logging reset tokens, private message bodies, or unnecessary personal information.

Reliable product email depends on the whole journey, from the triggering event to the recipient’s next action. Testing each stage and making failures observable helps teams resolve problems before users repeatedly click resend or abandon the task.

#10428. Waze rolling out cyclist alerts based on real-time locations from Garmin trackers

Waze is officially rolling out alerts for cyclists along your route that leverages location data from Garmin trackers.

In an effort to “boost driver awareness” of nearby bicycle riders, Waze will start showing real-time alerts for nearby cyclists to drivers in the area. The feature shows both a location on the map ahead as well as a visible alert on screen.

Waze knows the location of a nearby cyclist through a partnership with Garmin, with “LiveTrack” location sharing from the Garmin Edge bike computer or Garmin smartwatches users to share location data with the Google-owned navigation app.

Cyclists need to opt-in to the feature to have their location shown, and the alerts drivers see are temporary and anonymous. When cyclists end their ride, Waze alerts also go away.

This is rolling out starting today to the Waze apps on both Android and iOS.

Further detail isn’t clear, but that includes Gemini, Gemini Enterprise, and Google AI Mode in Search. That goes for each of these byproducts of the new partnership. Google Drive and Gmail integration with Adobe Acrobat will likely be the first to roll out, with Gemini features to follow shortly after.

Existing benchmarks don’t adequately capture how agents reason natively over dense files with charts, images, blueprints, schematics, and tables, so we partnered with Artificial Analysis to launch the new Multimodal Knowledge Agents (MKA) benchmark. Carbon ranks #1 while using 3X fewer tokens than the next best model across 200 tasks that test a model’s multimodal performance in agentic workflows.

The introductory pricing for Carbon is $2 per million input tokens and $10 per million output tokens until November 30. Afterward, it will cost $4 per 1M input tokens and $20 per 1M output tokens.

#10423. One UI 9.5 might bring Pixel 11’s ‘Rambler’ feature to Samsung Keyboard before Google’s

The Pixel 11 launched with one AI tool most people got on board with, called “Rambler.” It’s a transcription tool that overhauls and cleans up voice-to-text messages. Too bad this Rambler clone will only be available on Samsung Keyboard.

The Rambler-like keyboard feature was found in an internal test build of Samsung’s One UI 9.5, thanks to SamMobile. The settings include a feature named “Refine Voice Input,” and the description removes any doubt that it might be something other than a Rambler clone.

Clean up filler words, hesitations, and more. Text appears after you finish speaking.

Much like the Pixel 11’s Rambler tool, the final product is only available after you’re done dictating. The audio is then processed and turned into something coherent, devoid of the onomatopoeic words we often say when gathering our thoughts in a voice-to-text message. The biggest downside to this Rambler variant is that it falls under Samsung Keyboard’s feature set, not Google Keyboard. Even though One UI 9.5 is largely based on Android 17, Samsung still offers its own keyboard as the default experience.

This isn’t the first Google Keyboard feature Samsung Keyboard has emulated. Magic Cue was a massive addition to Samsung Galaxy devices, in the form of Now Nudge. It works wonderfully if you happen to like using Samsung Keyboard.

Google still hasn’t made Rambler available for any other Pixel devices besides the Pixel 11, and it’s the same story for Google Keyboard builds on Galaxy devices. Workarounds exist, but they aren’t official and shouldn’t be treated as such.

#10422. Why Transactional Emails Fail After Deployment: A Practical Debugging Guide for Developers

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Transactional emails are easy to overlook during application development. A registration form works, the database stores the user's information, and the backend successfully triggers an email. Everything appears to be functioning as expected.

Then a user reports that their password-reset message never arrived.

The problem might not be in the email template or the sending function. It could involve DNS configuration, authentication failures, message formatting, queue processing, recipient-server policies, or an incorrectly configured production environment.

For developers building SaaS applications, ecommerce platforms, and web services, reliable email delivery is an important part of the user experience. An account-verification message that never arrives can prevent someone from accessing an application, while a delayed security notification can create unnecessary confusion.

Understanding how to investigate these problems requires looking beyond the email-sending function. This guide explores a practical debugging workflow, the technical layers involved, and the tools developers can use to identify common email delivery failures.

One of the first mistakes developers make when troubleshooting email is assuming that a successful API response means the message has reached the recipient.

When an application submits an email through an SMTP server or an email service provider's API, the provider may accept the request and return a successful response. However, the receiving mail server still needs to process the message.

Depending on its configuration and policies, the receiving server might:

Accept the message and place it in the inbox.

Accept the message but classify it as spam.

Reject the message because of authentication or policy issues.

Accept the message but place it in another folder.

A successful request therefore confirms only that a particular stage of the process completed.

Developers should distinguish between three important states:

Submission: The application successfully handed the message to its sending provider.

Acceptance: The receiving mail server accepted responsibility for the message.

Inbox placement: The message became visible in the recipient's expected inbox.

These distinctions make troubleshooting considerably easier. Instead of immediately rewriting an email template, developers can identify which stage is actually failing.

Before investigating external email infrastructure, examine the application's own implementation.

A common example is a password-reset workflow. The application receives a request, generates a temporary token, creates a reset URL, and sends an email containing that URL.

Several problems can occur before the message even reaches an email provider.

The application generates an expired token.

The reset URL points to a staging environment.

An asynchronous task fails before processing the message.

The email service credentials are incorrect.

An API request times out without being retried.

The application records success before confirming the provider's response.

Start by logging the important stages of the process.

For example, a Node.js application might use structured logging:

This example assumes that the email provider returns a message identifier. Adapt the response fields to the actual SDK being used.

Notice that the logs identify the recipient through an internal ID rather than exposing their complete email address.

Avoid logging passwords, authentication tokens, reset URLs, or other sensitive information.

The objective is to establish a traceable record of what happened without introducing additional security risks.

Email authentication is another important area to investigate when messages are rejected or consistently classified as spam.

Three commonly used mechanisms help receiving servers evaluate whether a message is authorized to represent a particular domain.

Sender Policy Framework (SPF) allows a domain owner to specify which servers are authorized to send messages using that domain.

This information is published through DNS.

A simplified SPF record might look like this:

The actual record must reflect the sending services authorized by the domain owner.

Forgetting to authorize a new email provider.

Publishing multiple SPF records for the same domain.

Leaving outdated sending services in the record.

Remember that SPF evaluates the sending infrastructure against the envelope sender domain. It does not automatically guarantee that the visible From address is aligned.

DomainKeys Identified Mail (DKIM) adds a cryptographic signature to outgoing messages.

The receiving server can retrieve the corresponding public key through DNS and verify that the signature is valid.

A mismatch between the configured selector and published record.

Developers integrating multiple email providers should verify that each provider has the correct signing configuration.

Domain-based Message Authentication, Reporting, and Conformance (DMARC) builds on SPF and DKIM.

It allows domain owners to define how receiving servers should handle messages that fail authentication and alignment requirements.

A basic monitoring-oriented DMARC record might look like this:

This is an illustrative configuration. The reporting address must be valid, and the domain should have an appropriate monitoring and policy-enforcement plan.

Developers can use MXToolbox to inspect DNS records and investigate common authentication problems.

For additional technical background, SitePoint's guide to HTML email development explains why email environments require different implementation considerations from ordinary web pages.

An email template that looks perfect in a browser can behave differently when rendered by Gmail, Outlook, Apple Mail, or another email client.

This happens because email clients do not implement HTML and CSS in exactly the same way.

A layout may break because of unsupported CSS properties, image restrictions, unexpected font rendering, or differences in how tables are handled.

For this reason, email testing should be part of the development process rather than something performed only after users report a problem.

HTML validation: Check the markup for malformed elements, missing attributes, and unnecessary code.

Rendering tests: Preview the email across desktop and mobile email clients.

Link verification: Confirm that buttons, links, and personalized URLs point to the correct destinations.

Image testing: Check whether the message remains understandable when images are blocked.

Content inspection: Review subject lines, sender information, and the plain-text alternative.

Deliverability checks: Examine authentication, content-related warnings, and potential inbox placement problems.

Developers can also incorporate pre-send diagnostics into their release workflow to identify potential deliverability problems before a message is sent to a larger audience.

The important point is to treat these checks as one part of quality assurance, not as a guarantee that every message will reach the primary inbox.

For more information about email rendering differences, SitePoint's article on essential HTML email coding tips provides useful examples of client-specific behavior.

Testing transactional emails with real customer addresses during development can create unnecessary problems.

A better approach is to use a dedicated testing environment that captures outgoing messages without delivering them to actual recipients.

For example, Mailpit provides a local SMTP server and web interface for inspecting messages during development.

The final output generated by the application.

Another option is Mailtrap, which provides email testing capabilities for development workflows.

Consider a registration feature that sends a welcome message whenever a new user creates an account.

Instead of sending hundreds of test messages to real addresses, developers can configure the development environment to capture them in a testing inbox.

This makes it possible to repeatedly test different templates without affecting actual customers or generating unnecessary email traffic.

A separate testing environment also reduces the risk of accidentally sending development notifications to production users.

Applications that send large numbers of emails often use background workers instead of processing every message during the original HTTP request.

This approach helps keep the application responsive, but it introduces another potential source of failures.

Imagine an ecommerce application that sends order confirmations after checkout.

The application creates an order successfully and places an email task in a queue. However, the background worker becomes unavailable before processing the task.

The customer receives an order confirmation much later than expected, or perhaps not at all.

To investigate these problems, developers should monitor:

Retries are particularly important, but they need to be implemented carefully.

A temporary network failure may justify another attempt. A permanent rejection caused by an invalid recipient address usually does not.

Exponential backoff can help prevent repeated requests from overwhelming an unavailable service.

In a production system, add random jitter to avoid having multiple workers retry simultaneously.

Also consider idempotency. If a worker processes the same task twice, the application should avoid sending duplicate transactional messages whenever possible.

A reliable queue architecture should distinguish between temporary failures and permanent failures rather than retrying every error indefinitely.

Testing before deployment is important, but it cannot reveal every issue that may occur in production.

Real email traffic introduces changing conditions, including recipient-server behavior, sender reputation, invalid addresses, and differences between email providers.

Developers should therefore establish ongoing monitoring.

For Gmail-related monitoring, Google Postmaster Tools provides information about domain reputation, spam rates, and authentication.

It is particularly useful for teams sending enough email to generate meaningful reporting data.

Another useful resource is Can I Email, which documents HTML and CSS feature support across email clients.

These tools address different problems. Monitoring sender reputation will not fix a broken password-reset URL, just as validating HTML will not resolve a DNS authentication failure.

The best approach is to monitor each layer independently.

A repeatable checklist helps developers investigate problems without overlooking important details.

Before releasing a new email feature, review the following:

Confirm that the application successfully submits the message.

Verify that provider credentials and environment variables are correct.

Inspect SPF, DKIM, and DMARC configuration.

Validate the HTML and plain-text versions.

Preview the message on desktop and mobile email clients.

Verify that development and production configurations are separated.

Check delivery events and provider error reports.

Monitor bounce and complaint rates after deployment.

Not every application needs an expensive testing platform or a complicated monitoring stack.

A small project might only require a local email testing environment, basic DNS checks, and structured application logs.

A larger SaaS platform may benefit from automated integration tests, provider webhooks, queue monitoring, authentication reports, and dedicated deliverability testing.

The right setup depends on the application's sending volume, complexity, and the importance of its email workflows.

Reliable email delivery requires more than calling an SMTP function or integrating an email API.

Developers need to understand the complete process, from application logic and message construction to authentication, queue processing, recipient-server acceptance, and inbox placement.

The most effective troubleshooting strategy is to isolate these layers instead of treating every missing message as a template problem.

Start with application logs, inspect provider responses, validate authentication records, test email rendering, and monitor production behavior.

By incorporating these checks into development and deployment workflows, teams can reduce avoidable failures and build applications that communicate more reliably with their users.

#10418. Nintendo Switch 2 emulation on Android already has The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake up and running

The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake now runs surprisingly well on phones after just a week of additional emulator development.

Legend of Zelda fans all over the world have been marveling at the Ocarina of Time remake’s visuals and enhanced gameplay systems on the Nintendo Switch 2, but the Android emulator scene has been busy marveling over something just a little bit different. One Redditor got the latest Zelda game running on Banana Native, a free emulator that’s available right now on GitHub.

Ocarina of Time remake performance has now reached a major milestone into the 30 FPS territory with frame gen, which means the game is actually running at around 15 FPS, but then scaled and interpolated using AI frame generation to appear like 30 FPS. Banana Native isn’t a traditional emulator, as it doesn’t emulate hardware. Rather, it uses the game’s own ARM64 code to run on the phone’s CPU. The Switch 2 is powered by a mobile Nvidia chip, after all.

The user is running the Ocarina of Time remake on a Poco F7 Ultra, which sports a two-generation-old Qualcomm Snapdragon 8 Elite (Gen 4) processor. The latest phones that are coming out, like the Redmagic 12 Pro, run a 6th-gen Snapdragon 8 Elite Extreme chip, so performance could be a lot better on a newer phone. Apps like Banana Native require specific driver support for each phone to run, and as I pointed out in my Redmagic 12 Pro review, platforms like Droid Deck and Game Native don’t yet have proper drivers for the newest chips.

9to5Google’s Damien Wilde recently reviewed Droid Deck, another native-type emulator that lets you legally play Steam games you own on your phone by just signing into your Steam account. It works extremely well on lots of Android phones and helps showcase just how far Android emulation has come this year.

#10416. Google Pixel Tag Initial Review: The gold standard for Find Hub trackers, whatever that’s worth

Google’s Find Hub had a tumultuous launch for its performance, but also for a lack of clear direction in hardware. Over two years later, that’s finally arriving in the Google Pixel Tag which, even just a few days in, is the obvious gold standard in the race to build an AirTag for Android devices.

Google Pixel Tag is now available for purchase at the Google Store and Amazon.

There are plenty of really good Android Find Hub trackers on the market. Pebblebee and Chipolo have been long-standing examples of how to build good dual-network trackers that work on both Android and iOS, while Motorola’s Moto Tag series has delivered two Android-only trackers that deliver better performance and more focused features. But even with the Moto Tag, there’s a clear compromise. Features like the ability to ring your phone are tied to a separate app and, in turn, have some inherent clunkiness.

Pixel Tag is the device that finally brings it all together, and it’s brilliant so far.

I’ve only had the Pixel Tag for a week and, frankly, it’s impossible to properly review a tracker in that time. There are only so many ways to artificially test out how a tracker works, as most cases where you’ll find value in this sort of product can only occur in real life.

So, with that said, let’s focus on the fundamentals.

Google Pixel Tag pairs to your Android phone just like any other Find Hub tracker – though much faster compared to some others I’ve tested – but the differences are obvious towards the end of that process. The final screen shows options for setting ringer volume and an option to set up “Ring this phone.”

Those new features are what make the Pixel Tag so good, and so appealing compared to all of the competition. A quick double-tap on the Tag’s button rings whichever phone you connected it to, assuming it’s in range, and it works pretty reliably. You can also set up “left behind” alerts (now available on other Find Hub trackers too) for when the phone leaves the tracker behind – you can set up exclusion zones such as your home or work address.

One of my favorite examples of the added polish from Google is the ability to adjust the ringer volume on the page where you actually want to ring the tracker. In a crowded but quiet space where you know the tracker is close by, being able to quickly set the volume to low is a great addition. In my book, it’s the reason to buy Pixel Tag over any other Android tracker.

UWB, of course, only makes up-close finding all the better. I found this decently reliable on my Pixel 11 Pro XL, but it is a little bit frustrating that the directional cues vanish within about 10-12 feet. Fine for a backpack or something like that, potentially a problem if your Tag is attached to a set of keys.

On that note, my biggest hardware complaint is that Google took the Apple route of having no built-in mounting mechanism. The super thin and light hardware of Pixel Tag is wonderful, don’t get me wrong, but being forced to buy a separate accessory just to attach it to my bag is frustrating! One of Google’s partners, Bellroy, built one of these accessories and it’s simple enough – a silicone case with a rope. I wish I didn’t need it, but it works, and at least you get two of them in a pack for $25.

The hardware itself is pretty good across the board, really, with the battery door also being slightly easier to open compared to the Moto Tag. Inside is a standard CR2032 battery that Google says will last “over one year.” I’d personally still prefer a rechargeable battery with USB-C or wireless charging, but this is a good enough solution given the amount of time the battery lasts – hopefully Google’s claim lives up.

Rounding out some of the key aspects here, it’s worth saying that the built-in speaker is more than loud enough at its highest setting, but the value for me so far has really come in that the low setting is actually low enough to not be a nuisance. I do find it a little strange that there’s no visual indication of where the Tag’s button is, but the packaging and app both do a good enough job of letting you know.

I really just wish it would have a rechargeable battery, preferably via USB-C. It would have been the only tracker on the market to support UWB and be rechargeable.

I’m looking forward to seeing how Pixel Tag performs as the travel season spins back up, so stay tuned for a later long-term review of Google’s tracker in the coming months. Initial impressions, though, are solid. Sound off in the comments below if you have any questions around Pixel Tag, I’ll answer everything I can!

Google Pixel Tag is now available at the Google Store and Amazon.

#10413. Pixel Tag tidbits: Surprise NFC, Find Hub setup, & Bellroy keychain cases

The Made by Google 2026 lineup is finally complete with tomorrow’s retail launch of the Pixel Tag. You can find our review here, while there are some additional details about the device since the August announcement.

The Pixel Tag is the first Find Hub tracker with NFC. Google did not mention this connectivity specification in August, with Near Field Communication letting you tap the tracker to the back of your phone to immediately open device settings. The NFC area on the Pixel Tag is below the circular battery door, which you hold down and twist counterclockwise to open.

On Android 17 QPR2, tapping immediately opens the details page. On QPR1, you first get a system notification that you need to click. NFC joins Bluetooth 6.0 with Channel Sounding and Ultra Wideband (UWB).

The setup process adds a new screen after the initial pairing and terms of use. It lets you configure: Notify when left behind, Ring this phone, Edit name, Edit category, and Adjust volume for this tag.

The Pixel Tag has a physical button on the rear side below the battery cover. Clicking twice plays a loud sound on your phone if both devices are nearby. Once found, you can tap the button once to silence, or turn it off from your phone. This feature can be disabled from the Pixel Tag’s device details page on a tracker-by-tracker basis.

Google does not have a first-party case for the Pixel Tag. Instead, it’s working with Made for Google partner Bellroy.

There are two models available from the Google Store, with the first made of silicone. The Bellroy Lite Case Duo costs $24.99 and comes in a pack of two: Mist and Shadow colorways. It wraps everything but the face of the Pixel Tag (and its speaker cutout), while giving you a hole in the corner and color-matching cord.

There’s also the Bellroy Leather Tag Case in Artichoke, Azalea, Black, Pewter, and Red Ochre for $29.99.

#10411. What Actually Happens When a Kafka Region Fails?

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Most Kafka deployments in the industry plan to survive the loss of a single Kafka broker or an availability zone. But that's not enough for critical Tier-0 infrastructure where downtime or data loss of even a few seconds could have a massive business impact.

In such scenarios, the failure model should consider not just the loss of a broker or a zone, but the loss of an entire region. The business is expected to operate without interruption even when a whole region goes down, and it should be possible to also roll the cluster safely while operating in that state, to apply any break-glass changes.

But what actually happens inside Kafka when an entire region goes down? What happens to the replicas, producer writes, and KRaft quorum? And what happens when that region comes back?

Let's walk through these scenarios using a Tier-0 multi-region Kafka architecture.

For a Tier-0 multi-region Kafka cluster, we need three different regions with isolated failure domains and a minimum of two brokers in each region.

Brokers in each region should be configured with rack awareness to indicate their failure domains as follows:

For Tier-0 workloads, each topic's partitions should be configured to have a replication factor (RF) of 6. Kafka's rack-aware replica assignment will spread the replicas across the three regions. If the automatic replica assignment doesn't allocate two replicas per region, we have to explicitly reassign replicas immediately after topic creation to meet our availability guarantees.

This way, if one region goes down entirely, we still have four replicas surviving and the cluster would still continue to operate.

But just running brokers in multiple regions is not the solution here. In fact, that's the easy part. The hard question is, what guarantee remains when one of those regions disappears, and does it solve the business needs?

The two brokers in that region become unavailable, leaving four surviving replicas across Regions B and C.

For any partitions whose leaders were in Region A, Kafka needs to elect new leaders from the surviving in-sync replicas.

But having four surviving replicas alone does not determine whether Kafka will continue accepting writes.

The next step is to define what is considered a successful write in this Kafka setup. For this, we need two configs:

min.insync.replicas determines the minimum number of replicas that should be present in the in-sync replica set (ISR) of a partition for Kafka to accept writes when producers use acks=all.

acks=all is a producer-side config that ensures that the producer will get an acknowledgment only when the write has been successfully replicated to the required in-sync replicas.

These two settings work together to satisfy the durability guarantees we want.

When all three regions are healthy, ISR would include all six replicas. Let's say one region goes down, then the ISR would drop down to four.

Since minISR is three, writes will still continue to work.

There may be temporary interruptions while Kafka detects the failure, elects new leaders, and clients reconnect to them. But once the surviving leaders are available, the cluster can continue accepting writes as long as the ISR requirements are met.

The goal isn't availability at any cost, but rather the system should be available as long as the durability guarantees that we designed for can be maintained.

Now let's make the scenario more interesting.

Region A is still down. We have four replicas surviving across Regions B and C.

What if we need to roll the cluster one broker at a time, to apply any break-glass changes?

During that time, the ISR can go down from four to three. But since minISR is three, writes will still continue to work.

Let's look at how the cluster behaves under different failure scenarios.

Kafka will stop accepting writes only when the surviving ISR drops below three.

This is an important distinction for Tier-0 workloads.

It's not sufficient to design a cluster that survives a region failure. We also need to consider whether the cluster can continue operating safely while already in that degraded state.

So far, we focused on the data plane. Now switching gears, the KRaft controllers have a completely different availability model.

They form the metadata quorum, and a majority is needed to continue operation.

For our setup, five controllers could be distributed as 2 + 2 + 1 across the three regions, requiring a majority of three to maintain quorum.

If either of the regions containing two controllers goes down, three controllers remain. If the region containing one controller goes down, four remain.

So, the controller quorum survives the loss of any one region.

But, unlike brokers, we cannot necessarily roll the controllers at that point because taking one controller down could cause the quorum to drop below three.

With a static quorum, one way to solve this is to use more controllers, and a 3 + 3 + 3 distribution would provide this. Losing one region would leave six controllers with room to still roll the controllers, as we only need five to maintain quorum.

On the flip side, an increase in quorum size also increases the coordination overhead. The official Kafka documentation typically recommends three or five controllers, which tolerate one or two concurrent failures respectively.

Alternatively, with dynamic KRaft quorum (KIP-853), introduced in Kafka 3.9, we can add or remove controllers from the voting quorum without restarting the cluster.

For example, with our 2 + 2 + 1 setup, if a region containing two controllers goes down, the remaining three controllers can still maintain quorum. We can then remove the failed controllers from the voting membership, one at a time, reducing the quorum size to three. This allows us to roll one of the surviving controllers while still maintaining the new majority of two.

However, dynamic quorum reconfiguration is not automatic. The surviving controllers must be able to maintain quorum and successfully commit the membership changes before we can proceed with the controller restart.

You need to consider whether you want the same availability guarantee for the controllers as that of the data plane, and whether dynamic quorum reconfiguration during a regional outage is acceptable for your Tier-0 workloads.

So far, we assumed that a region either works or goes down completely.

But the WAN itself becomes a failure mode.

In this setup, a partly working WAN between two regions can be more dangerous than a link that's completely down.

Increased latency, packet loss, or intermittent connectivity issues between regions can cause replication to lag, ultimately resulting in replicas falling out of ISR.

The three regions might be independently healthy, but if the connectivity between them is not, then the availability of the cluster might be impacted.

Going back to the CAP theorem, to tolerate a network partition, we must choose between consistency and availability.

So, if a partition's ISR falls below the configured min.insync.replicas of three due to a network partition, Kafka stops accepting writes with acks=all rather than weakening those guarantees.

Similarly, if the KRaft controller quorum is lost, then no metadata changes, like leader elections, can happen in the cluster, even though existing data-plane traffic may continue for some time.

The availability guarantees we discussed also come with their own costs.

The first cost is client latency. With cross-region replication and acks=all, the network latency between these regions directly affects producer latency. Consumers can also pay the WAN latency cost when consuming from partition leaders located in a different region.

The second cost is replication overhead. With RF=6, there is additional storage, network traffic, and replication-related coordination cost in the brokers compared to a smaller number of replicas.

So, in an architecture like this, monitoring the health of the network paths between these regions is as critical as monitoring the Kafka brokers and controllers.

Metrics like round-trip latency (RTT) between regions, packet loss, TCP retransmissions, connection errors/timeouts, and network throughput/bandwidth utilization are a good start.

When one region goes down, all the partition leaders in that region would move to the two other surviving regions.

This implies that those two regions would have to handle more producer and consumer traffic, network throughput, and disk I/O.

Even if the cluster is designed to satisfy its ISR and controller quorum requirements, if it's not sized to handle these failures when one region goes down, the surviving regions would be overloaded, resulting in higher latencies, timeouts, and operational instability.

So, for Tier-0 infrastructure, capacity planning should always be done for failure states and not stop at normal operations.

Now let's say Region A comes back online.

The brokers in the recovered region might have to catch up on a significant amount of data before they become fully in sync again.

This can result in a sudden burst of cross-region network traffic and disk I/O across all regions.

Region recovery needs to be controlled so that it doesn't overwhelm the healthy part of the cluster.

This can be achieved by throttling the replica catch-up, with the trade-off of longer recovery time to minimize the impact on live production traffic.

Replication throttling needs a deeper discussion of its own, so we won't go into details here.

The important point is that bringing a failed region back online is not simply a matter of restarting its brokers. We need to make sure that the recovery process itself doesn't impact the business.

Before putting a Tier-0 multi-region Kafka cluster into production, validate these scenarios:

There is no shortage of ways to deploy Kafka across multiple regions. The challenge here is to pick the right architecture that addresses all our durability guarantees.

For Tier-0 workloads, this means designing the architecture around failures first.

What happens when a region goes down? Can we still roll the cluster? What continues to work, and at what point do we deliberately stop accepting writes?

Lastly, can we safely bring back the region that was down without impacting the business?

That is ultimately the difference between simply running Kafka across multiple regions versus actually supporting a mission-critical Tier-0 workload.

Google is not naming the second network today, but did share that Ultra Wideband is supported. Officially, you are switching between “two of America’s fastest 5G networks.”

Network switching will first be supported on Pixel 9 and newer devices that have a Google Fi eSIM (and no other SIM active). It makes use of Android’s Multiple Enabled Profiles (MEP) eSIM technology that allows for two carrier profiles on a single chip.

#10407. Wyze’s new door lock has speedy fingerprint recognition and 12-month battery life

Wyze is today launching its new “Lever Lock,” a smart lock for your front door that has a year of battery life and fast fingerprint recognition, all for an affordable price tag.

Available starting today, the Wyze Level Lock is not your typical deadbolt smart lock. Rather, this is a smart lock designed for your standard single-bore latch, meaning it’s available for doors that other smart locks aren’t compatible with. You can still have a deadbolt, but you’ll need enough space for both (per Wyze’s guidelines below).

The Lever Lock has a PIN entry and a fingerprint sensor, the latter of which Wyze says recognizes your fingerprint in about half a second. It uses local storage for biometric data and works to improve that data with each successful unlock.

The PIN has a clever feature (though it’s presented in somewhat poor taste) that lets you input random digits before or after your actual code to prevent onlookers from being able to see your code. There’s also a physical key backup as well as a USB-C input on the outside in the case that the battery dies – Wyze says it lasts about 12 months on a charge using eight AA batteries.

The Wyze Lever Lock is available today for $99.98 and shipping immediately.

Time to re-install mid-quarter updates at Intel?

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Intel has been sued by shareholders who accuse the company of misleading investors about the severity of the PC market downturn and its impact on the company's financial performance before its shares plunged by nearly 29% in late October, reports Reuters.

The proposed class action lawsuit led by the Southfield Fire and Police Retirement System pension fund was filed on Friday in a federal court in San Jose, California. The lawsuit alleges that Intel failed to inform investors in a timely manner about the extent to which slow demand for consumer processors and excessive inventories would affect its sales. In particular, the plaintiffs argue that the negative impact of the PC market downturn on Intel's business was greater than the benefits the company enjoyed from the unprecedented buildout of AI data center infrastructure and other AI-related opportunities.

"Intel made statements that were materially false and/or misleading when made because they failed to disclose the following adverse facts pertaining to Intel’s business," the lawsuit reads.

While Intel, like many other major technology companies, has invested heavily in its manufacturing capabilities, supply constraints have prevented the chipmaker from fully capitalizing on strong demand for data center processors. Meanwhile, a severe shortage of memory has driven up DRAM prices and, consequently, the cost of PCs, which has affected demand for consumer systems. As a result, Intel could not sell enough CPUs in the third quarter, which dramatically affected the company's stock price in October.

Intel chief executive Lip-Bu Tan expressed disappointment with the supply constraints, the extraordinary situation in the memory market, and the subsequent weakness of the PC business during the company's earnings call. Yet, the plaintiff believes that the company should have warned about the turmoil situation on the client PC market earlier. In fact, back in the early 2000s, when Intel was led by Craig Barrett, the company routinely issued mid-quarter business updates to inform investors about changes in its financial outlook. In fact, in September 2004, Intel used such an update to cut its third-quarter revenue and gross margin projections due to weaker-than-expected demand for its processors and inventory adjustments by its customers, which were very similar circumstances that the company faced in the third quarter of 2026.

A similar update could have alerted Intel's shareholders to the challenges the chipmaker faced well ahead of its quarterly earnings release. Unfortunately, Intel abandoned the practice of mid-quarter updates in January 2006 under Paul Otellini, though it occasionally revised its guidance between quarterly earnings releases thereafter, particularly during the global financial crisis of 2008 – 2010.

Intel's shareholders also allege that the company had a financial incentive to withhold information about the state of its business as it raised $23 billion through a stock offering at $95 per share in mid-August, roughly halfway through its third quarter. Intel secured the additional capital as it planned to expand its semiconductor production capacity.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Intel’s stock price dropped by 28.6% on October 30, erasing around $140 billion of the company's market capitalization, after the chipmaker issued Q4 revenue and earnings projections that fell short of expectations and warned that the PC market downturn would persist. Meanwhile, despite the massive sell-off, Intel's shares have gained 62% since the beginning of the year.

The lawsuit seeks unspecified damages on behalf of Intel investors who purchased the company's shares between July 23 and October 29, 2026. Intel CEO Lip-Bu Tan and CFO David Zinsner are also named as defendants.

#10400. How to Build an Accessible Gold Value Calculator in Vanilla JavaScript

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Ask anyone selling an old chain or a broken ring what they want to know, and it's one thing: what is this worth? A small calculator can answer that in a second, but most of the ones you find online get the details wrong. They ignore troy ounces, treat 14K as 58.5% one day and 58.3% the next, or show a "value" that no buyer will ever pay.

In this tutorial, we'll build a gold value calculator that gets those details right. It runs on plain HTML and JavaScript, updates as the user types, works with a screen reader, keeps its state in a shareable URL and pulls a live spot price through a small Node.js endpoint. The calculation lives in one pure function, so we can test it in a few lines.

Every gold calculator comes down to three inputs: how much the item weighs, how much of that weight is actually gold, and what pure gold is trading for today.

Purity is measured in karats, out of 24. A 14K ring is 14 parts gold and 10 parts other metals, so it's 14 ÷ 24 = 58.3% gold. You'll also see 14K stamped as "585", which is the hallmark many manufacturers use for the same alloy. For a calculator, the karat fraction is the safer default:

The second detail most tutorials miss is the unit. Gold spot prices are quoted in USD per troy ounce, which is 31.1035 grams, not the 28.35-gram ounce on a kitchen scale. Jewelers also use pennyweight (dwt), which is 1.5552 grams. If your calculator multiplies grams by a per-ounce price without converting, every result is wrong by a factor of 31.

Put together, the formula looks like this:

For example, with gold at $2,400 per troy ounce, 10 grams of 14K gold contains 5.83 grams of pure gold, which is worth $449.85 at melt.

That $449.85 is the value of the gold content alone. Refiners and buyers pay a percentage of it, because refining, assaying and handling cost money. Depending on the buyer, the item and the market, the payout can sit well below melt value.

That gap is why good production tools separate the two numbers. A dedicated scrap jewelry gold calculator, like the one The Alloy Market offers, lets sellers estimate what their gold is worth from its weight and karat before they send anything in. Our calculator does the same job with a payout field: leave it at 100% to see melt value, or enter a buyer's rate to see a realistic offer.

Start with real form controls and a visible

A few choices here matter more than they look:

A little CSS gives touch-friendly controls. Keeping inputs at 16px (1rem) also stops iOS Safari from zooming in when a field gets focus.

The biggest problem with most calculator snippets is that the math is buried inside an event handler, mixed with getElementById calls. You can't test it, and you can't reuse it on a server. Put the calculation and validation in their own module instead:

Both functions take plain values and return plain values. They don't know a DOM exists, which means the same file can run in the browser, in Node.js and in your test runner.

Notice that we don't round inside the calculation. Rounding happens once, at display time. Rounding in the middle of a chain of multiplications is a classic way to end up a few cents off.

Now connect the form to the module. Listening for the input event on the form itself catches changes from every field, including the dropdowns, so there's no need to wire up each control separately.

With the markup, CSS and script in place, here's the finished calculator showing our 10 g, 14K example with an 80% buyer payout:

Asking users to type the spot price works, but prefilling it is friendlier. Don't call a metals price API straight from the browser, though. Your API key would be visible to anyone who opens DevTools, and every visitor would spend one of your API calls.

Instead, put a small endpoint in front of your price provider and cache the result. This example uses Express and the fetch built into Node.js 18 and later:

Three things keep this endpoint dependable:

On the page, the loadSpot() function from Step 3 fills the field and shows the time of the price. The field stays editable, so a seller who has a quote from a specific buyer can type that instead.

A seller comparing offers might want to send their numbers to a family member, or come back to them tomorrow. Because every input has a name, turning the form into a query string is one line with URLSearchParams.

The syncUrl() function in Step 3 runs on every change and uses history.replaceState instead of pushState, so typing doesn't fill the back button with dozens of entries. On load, restoreFromUrl() puts the values back. A link like this opens with the form already filled in:

This is also the progressive enhancement story. If JavaScript fails to load, the form still submits as a GET request. Your server can read the same parameters, import the same gold.js and render the result in HTML.

Because goldValue() is a pure function, testing it needs no browser and no mocks. Node.js ships with a built-in test runner, so there's nothing to install:

Run it with node --test. (Add "type": "module" to your package.json so Node treats the files as ES modules.) The troy ounce test is the one worth keeping forever: if anyone "simplifies" the conversion later, it fails immediately.

A quick checklist for putting this calculator on a real store:

A gold calculator looks like a ten-line script, but the details are what make it useful: converting troy ounces properly, separating melt value from payout, keeping the math testable and making the result accessible and shareable. The same pattern of a pure calculation module, a thin DOM layer and a cached server endpoint works for any calculator where the price depends on several inputs, from shipping estimates to currency conversion.

Mathematically, 14K is 14 ÷ 24 = 58.3%. Many manufacturers stamp 14K jewelry "585" and alloy it to that standard, so both numbers are common. Pick one, state it in the dropdown, and use it consistently.

Let the user choose. Most people weigh jewelry in grams, but spot prices are quoted per troy ounce, so the calculator should accept either and convert internally.

Yes. Drop Step 4 and let users type the spot price, or have a scheduled job write the current price into a static JSON file. Everything else runs entirely in the browser.

For the 25th anniversary of Xbox, Microsoft is beginning to make good on its paid “next-gen game preservation” promise. Starting today, you can play 25 different original Xbox games on your Windows PC, as long as you have (or are willing to pay) for digital copies.

In July, Microsoft originally launched the idea with just four Xbox games, so 25 is progress, and Microsoft says all these games are now playable with keyboard and mouse.

Plus, Microsoft says it’s adding achievements to original Xbox games for the first time, starting with four games: Star Wars: Knights of the Old Republic and its sequel, Blinx: The Time Sweeper, and Conker: Live and Reloaded. You can unlock achievements playing them on Xbox One and Xbox Series consoles as well, after a system update. More games should get them in future.

Here’s the full list of Xbox on PC games, with the 21 new additions in bold:

Yes, that’s a lot of Star Wars games, most of which already have a PC version, and few games that were exclusive to Xbox at the time. (But many of those Star Wars games were excellent.)

You also won’t be able to use an original Xbox disc to play these games, I’m afraid, as original Xbox (and Xbox 360) discs aren’t part of Microsoft’s disc-to-digital program; they predate the unique serial number that Microsoft embedded into Xbox One discs. In case you’re wondering, Xbox on PC backward compatibility appears to be powered by an emulator.

Also, most of the new games aren’t currently included with Xbox Game Pass, according to Microsoft; just Blinx, Conker, Crimson Skies, Fusion Frenzy, Morrowind, and Grabbed by the Ghoulies.

Leaked Microsoft documents previously showed us that October 2026 would be the “full launch” of original Xbox games on PC, while Xbox 360 games would have a “gradual” rollout to next-gen consoles between 2027 and 2028, which are when Microsoft is expected to roll out a handheld and a Helix box respectively. The way things are shaping up, the Xbox Helix should play games from every generation of Xbox, even if Helix fundamentally turns out to be a Windows PC.

#10396. Jailbroken PS4 runs 9-billion-parameter AI model at up to 43 tokens per second

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.
