Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

It needs no PC and no internet, just a console running GoldHEN.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Developer Leonardo Alegría’s open-source project Garlic LLM lets a jailbroken PS4 run the 9-billion-parameter Qwen3.5-9B AI model offline on the console’s 2013-era GPU. The app talks to the GPU directly, and the model is shrunk to about 4.5GiB to fit in the console’s memory, running at 31 to 39 tokens per second.

The project, named after the memory the GPU reads directly — its sibling memory type is “Onion” — relies on a PS4 running GoldHEN, which is a homebrew enabler for exploited consoles. Garlic LLM is a free, single 5.66GB download, available on Hugging Face and installable through the PS4’s Package Installer, with about 6GB free needed, or 12GB if the package is first copied to the console’s internal disk. The developer tested it on a base/Slim PS4 on firmware 11.52.

It’s capable of voice conversation, with spoken replies in seven languages through the Chatterbox speech model and the chat model running at about 22 tokens per second. A held controller button also lets it take dictation with Nvidia’s Parakeet TDT 0.6B v3 speech-to-text model, which supports 25 European languages. Parakeet models also power local transcription and dictation apps such as Meetily and Hex. The main model is Alibaba’s small open-weight Qwen3.5-9B, which, at the claimed 31 to 39 tokens per second in text chat — the prompt and test method are not given — is usable on the console.

The closest comparison is the PS5LM fork, which runs llama.cpp as a native app on the PS5 and reaches the console’s AMD GPU through PS5_Vulkan, a port of the RADV driver. The open-source llama.cpp is a widely used program for running language models locally, and RADV is the open-source driver that lets AMD GPUs run Vulkan, the graphics and compute API. On the PS4 there is no such driver layer for homebrew; Garlic LLM uses neither llama.cpp nor a Vulkan port, or so it would appear.

The model’s operations, such as matrix multiplies over 4-bit weights, are compute kernels written in OpenCL C and compiled with LLVM for AMD’s Graphics Core Next (GCN) architecture. The weights are kept in that “Garlic” memory, while the CPU side, in plain C, handles the tokenizer, the interface, text rendering, and audio. The pipeline runs from the prompt to the CPU tokenizer, then, for each token, a PM4 command buffer — the low-level commands a graphics driver would normally generate, which, in this case, the app builds itself — and the GCN kernels, before the token is displayed or spoken. The whole thing is built with the open-source OpenOrbis PS4 toolchain.

One immediate question is how you fit a 9-billion-parameter model in the console’s 8GB of memory, which the CPU and GPU share. At 8 bits per weight, you have one byte per parameter, meaning you’d need at least 9GB, with typically more than that required overall. Part of the answer is 4-bit (“Q4”) weights, which take half as much, although 3-bit (“Q3”) weights, at about 3.5 bits with scales, are used for the two largest matrices in each layer.

Using the combination gets you to about 4.5GiB, versus about 4.9GiB if it were all 4-bit. The attention, or KV, cache is also 4-bit, which lets a 16K-token context fit, in comparison to the model’s native 256K. The Parakeet dictation model adds 0.33GiB and the Chatterbox voice models 0.29GiB and 0.09GiB, for a grand total of 5.23GiB, or 5.62GB, which closely matches the 5.66GB package size.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

The next question is performance. Qwen3.5 comes with a multi-token prediction (MTP) head, and Garlic LLM uses it to draft up to two tokens ahead, with the full model verifying. This matches what the model was trained for, and the vLLM instructions on Alibaba’s model card use two speculative tokens. The project credits this for its chat speed on 2013 hardware.

Code also comes out faster with this setup, at up to 43 tokens per second. Also detailed is the default temperature, or model variability, which is 0.7 — Alibaba recommends 0.7 for general tasks with thinking off and 1.0 with it on — although the demo video uses 0, so it is predictable. At 35 tokens per second, the middle of the reported performance range, a 300-token answer takes under nine seconds. As a comparison, the PS5 ran the three-times-larger Qwen3.8-27B at about 20 tokens per second and the smaller Gemma 4 E4B and Llama 3.2 3B at 61 and 124, respectively, which is potentially quicker.

The PS4 Pro is “not tested yet,” but the developer is asking users for reports on GitHub. The PS5 is not supported as the app is written specifically for the PS4’s GPU and system software. The app itself contains no exploit, but GoldHEN is installed at the user’s own risk.

#10384. What Breaks When a Self-Hosted LLM Gateway Hits 1,000 Concurrent Streams

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

We sent 1,000 streaming calls at the same moment through three self-hosted LLM gateways: the Langfuse AI Gateway, the LiteLLM proxy and AcruxCore. Each gateway was limited to 2 CPUs. As shipped, Langfuse finished 261 of the 1,000 streams and LiteLLM finished 403. Neither failure came from the gateway's own code.

Langfuse's failures came from Linux's open-file limit, which Docker sets to 1,024 by default. LiteLLM had that problem too, plus a second one: an empty key cache sent hundreds of database lookups at once, and the ones that waited longer than 10 seconds were rejected. This article shows how each failure looked, how we traced it to its cause, and the settings that fixed it.

This benchmark measured the resource consumption of three LLM gateways under scaling concurrent streaming loads.

Full latency, memory, and feature results are available in the Langfuse vs LiteLLM vs AcruxCore benchmark.

First-token times are medians of the streams that succeeded. Up to 250 streams, every gateway succeeded. Then the errors started, and they did not look alike:

A 502 and a 503 point at different layers, so we expected two different causes. Two of the three turned out to share one cause.

Linux counts every network connection as an open file, and it caps how many open files one process may hold. The cap has two values. The soft limit is the cap that is in force now. The hard limit is the ceiling: a process may raise its own soft limit up to that value, and no further.

A streaming gateway holds two connections for each stream: one from the client, and one to the model provider. That makes about 1,000 open files at 500 streams, and about 2,000 at 1,000 streams. A gateway also needs a few more files for its database, its logs and its own libraries.

Docker on our machine gave every container a soft limit of 1,024 and a hard limit of 524,288. All three gateways received the same two numbers. What differed was how each program treats them when it starts:

Node.js raises its own soft limit to the hard limit at startup. The Rust and Python services keep 1,024, and neither project's Compose file sets a higher value. So the two gateways ran out of open files near 500 streams, which is the point where the errors started.

The out-of-files error appeared as a failed connection, not as a clear message. The Langfuse gateway's logs showed failed connections to OpenAI, and also failed connections to Langfuse's own web service, which the gateway asks to check each key. Both come from one cause: a process that cannot open another file cannot open another socket. Its client then receives a 502.

Read the limit the process actually has, not the one you think Docker gave it. Process 1 is usually the main process in a container:

If the gateway runs under a supervisor or several workers, check each worker's process ID instead of process 1. To see how close a process is to its limit under load, count its open file descriptors:

Raise the limit for the gateway service in its Compose file. We used 65,536 for all three gateways:

The same ulimits block works on the LiteLLM service. On Kubernetes or another platform, check /proc//limits inside a running container rather than assuming a value.

With the open-file limit raised, the Langfuse gateway finished all 1,000 streams. LiteLLM still returned 503 errors. To separate the two causes, we ran LiteLLM with one worker and only the open-file limit raised. All 500 streams succeeded in that ladder, but 595 of 1,000 failed with 503.

The 503 message names the authentication database, so we read LiteLLM's logs and its source. The log gave the same reason for all 840 failed streams in these runs, 595 in that ladder and 245 in the cold-cache runs below:

When 500 calls arrive while the key is not in the cache, each call looks the key up in Postgres. All of those lookups queue for 10 connections. The worker was also using its whole CPU allowance at this point, which slows every query it sends and receives. We infer that this CPU load lengthens the queue, but we did not measure it separately. Whatever the share of each, the lookups at the back of the queue miss the 10-second deadline.

If that explanation is right, the failures should depend on whether the key is already in the cache. So we ran 500 streams four more times. Three runs started after 75 seconds with no calls, which is longer than the 60-second cache. One run started right after a single warm-up call:

The warm run had no failures. The cold runs failed by different amounts, 161, 84 and 0, because the result depends on how many lookups miss the deadline, and that changes from run to run. This probably explains why the earlier ladder succeeded at 500 and failed at 1,000. A run is a race between the lookup queue and the deadline, so a single ladder does not show where the limit is.

The warm-up result is the one we measured. Send one call with each virtual key after LiteLLM starts, and avoid quiet periods longer than 60 seconds before a traffic spike. A steady flow of traffic keeps the key in the cache.

LiteLLM also exposes three settings that act on this path. We did not test them, so treat them as places to look rather than proven fixes:

We raised the open-file limit to 65,536 for all three gateways and gave LiteLLM 2 workers, one per CPU, as its production guide recommends for a single machine. Then we ran the full ladder again:

The open-file errors were gone for every gateway, and the Langfuse gateway gave the fastest first token of the three. LiteLLM still returned 120 × 503 at 500. That level started about 60 seconds after the first call of the ladder, so the cached key had probably just expired. All 1,000 succeeded at the next level, which started within 60 seconds of the 500 level.

Once nothing failed, CPU decided the first-token times. LiteLLM used its whole 2-CPU allowance from 100 streams on, and its median first token took 19.2 seconds at 1,000 streams. AcruxCore also used both CPUs from 100 streams on, and its first token at 1,000 streams was 3.8 s at the median and 9.7 s at p95. The Langfuse gateway peaked at 1.65 CPUs and 168 MiB, with a first token of 1.7 s at the median and 9.6 s at p95.

Each gateway also needs the rest of its stack, and that stack uses CPU too. While the Langfuse gateway served 500 streams, its whole stack used up to 3.1 CPUs, because the web service checked keys and the worker and ClickHouse stored usage. AcruxCore's whole stack peaked at 4.0 CPUs, because its API writes a trace for every call to Postgres. Size the stack around the gateway, not only the gateway container.

Talha Anwar is a senior AI and full-stack engineer and writes about building and running AI systems at talhaanwar.com.

Get the freshest news and resources for developers, designers and digital creators in your inbox each week

→ Baca artikel asli

#10383. I’ve waited a long time for the Boox Picco, and I’m not disappointed

At around $100, the Boox Picco offers one of the most portable e-reader experiences you can get. You add MagSafe-like capability with a refreshingly simple UI, and you have a delightful way to read, literally wherever you are.

There is an argument to be made for every single shape and size e-readers come in, and they come in so many different sizes it’s hard to keep track. I love the idea of having a 10-inch display that I can skim, but even if it’s perfect for sitting at home and reading anything from novels to comics, I don’t think it provides a very good travel experience

But even smaller devices like the Boox Palma make you choose between bringing a second device or sticking with your phone. Most often, the phone wins out – especially when you have a flexible approach with a foldable like the Galaxy Z Fold 8 I’ve been using daily.

Unlike the other phone-sized e-readers from Boox, the Picco stands at just 4.15 inches tall. The screen measures at just under that – 3.97 inches. It’s essentially the size of a MagSafe or Qi2 wallet – just small enough to fit on the back of your phone with the included magnetic case.

It’s thin, too. A 5.5mm side profile means it slips anywhere a minimal wallet would without issue. That goes for pockets just as well as crossbody bags; perfect for travel.

And that’s where I think I find so much more joy in using this over what would otherwise be considered a small e-reader, like the Palma. I don’t have a lot of excuses not to carry it around when I’m on the go. I’ll throw it in my Bellroy bag when I’m carrying what I’d considered my minimal EDC. It slides into any internal pocket, and it weighs next to nothing.

The back of the Boox Picco isn’t magnetic, but the included case is. If you want the thinnest possible footprint, forego the case. But adding it lets you do one of the coolest – and slightly copium-inducing – things with your phone: attach the e-ink device directly to your OLED-laden phone.

It does not work well with an unconventional device like my Galaxy Z Fold 8, but if you have nearly any other device like the Pixel 11, you should get a rather perfect fit. The Picco squares up to the back of any slab phone with Qi2 magnets, whether internal or via a case.

But, why? Why slap another device and add weight/thickness to your phone when it’s perfectly capable of displaying e-books?

Because it’s cool! That’s the driving reason. You can make the argument that it doubles your phone’s usability or that it gives you an easy place to store the tiny e-reader. The truth is that it does add extra thickness to your phone, and using your phone with the Picco piggybacking along can be uncomfortable, since your fingers now rest on another display and not a case or phone backing.

You can get used to it, and, as unnecessary as it may seem, it’s a fun way to carry around a much more eye-friendly screen.

The Picco diverts from other Boox devices in another way. It doesn’t run Android, which means you have no access to third-party apps like Google Play Books, Amazon Kindle, or any other store/library. Instead, the Picco uses a companion app to transfer EPUBs from your phone to the tiny e-reader.

I initially opposed this idea when I got a sneak peek during IFA in Berlin this year. But after using it for a few weeks, I’ll admit I’ve come around. But that all comes down to how you consume your media.

I try to source EPUBs no matter what device I use. On Android-powered e-readers, that means downloading Google Play Books and reading from there; I have my EPUBs uploaded to my own Google Play Books library, so they’re available universally.

On the Picco, my approach hasn’t changed much. I take those same EPUBs I have in storage and transfer them from the companion app.

I can understand how the lack of third-party libraries would make the Boox Picco moot. Still, I’ve been a proponent of buying digital books I can store in my own drives, so I can read them wherever, whether that’s the Google Play Books library or from a device like the Picco.

All of that is a roundabout way of saying the Picco, though it doesn’t let you use apps to read, makes it easy to get digital copies loaded and ready to flip through.

It uses a handshake connection that requires you to open the companion app on your phone, hit connect, and do the same on the Picco. Once connected, transferring EPUB files is as easy as selecting them from your phone’s physical or cloud storage and queueing them up. The process takes seconds, and the only issue I’ve had was solved by a force stop and trying again.

The homescreen is simply a carousel of your library, with a delightful little progress bar under each title as they’re sorted. You can access and manage the bookshelf from that page, as well as the minimal “apps” and settings. I appreciate the focus app and reading stats, but the countdown and to-do options require using the companion app. I don’t see the point of those.

It doesn’t even have a control panel. Swiping down from the top surfaces screen brightness and color controls. Even though this little guy is just over $100, it still has a front light controlled by a physical switch on the side. Compare that to e-readers at much higher price points without that game-changing feature.

The buttons opposite don’t control volume. Instead, they’re there for page turning.

It’s just simple and well-designed. Features are there to make reading a better experience, down to granular text controls for your digital books. It does exactly what it needs to do – nothing more, nothing less.

As far as battery goes, it meets my expectations. It’s not massive, so the battery is small in comparison to a smartphone. It still holds its own easily, with an easy week of use if you’re reading for an hour or two a day. Beyond that, it takes little time to charge and does great on idle, which is perfect for such a passive device.

The Picco will hit home for you if you want an ultra-portable option for your e-books. The same goes for those who don’t mind the smaller screen and lack of Android or third-party app support.

I get genuine joy every time I pick it up to read where I left off, and I think that’s because it’s so simple and nothing about it feels bloated, even the companion app, which is also straight to the point.

I think it’s been a while since a device has offered that experience for me. Adding the form factor, optional magnetic attachment for your phone, and price tag just gets me to that conclusion sooner.

The Boox Picco is the most adorable e-reader I’ve used, and it’s $109 on Amazon.

Currently Amazon has 392 broadband satellites in orbit and hoped to move the service out of beta by the end of this year.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Amazon has suffered a major setback in its plans to offer a viable Starlink rival, after 40 satellites destined for low Earth orbit (LEO) were destroyed. Just ten minutes after the Vulcan vehicle launched from Space Launch Complex 41 in Cape Canaveral, Florida, on Sunday, there was an “anomaly,” meaning that the rocket “failed to deliver the Amazon mission to orbit.” The language seems quite vague, but the United Launch Alliance (ULA) says that data needs to be analyzed “to understand what occurred today.”

And LIFTOFF of the first Vulcan Amazon Leo mission, carrying 40 broadband satellites to low Earth orbit! https://t.co/dv1MSjJUMFNovember 15, 2026

Shortly after the triumphant takeoff clip was shared by the ULA, above, the space tech firm updated its X feed with a confirmation that its mission on behalf of Amazon failed.

Based on data received by our Launch Director, we confirm that the Vulcan vehicle launched from Space Launch Complex 41 suffered an anomaly 10 minutes into the flight and failed to deliver the Amazon mission to orbit. Every flight generates data, and every flight teaches us something. Since the anomaly just occurred, we don’t have any specific data yet to understand what occurred today. ULA exists to provide assured access to space for our nation and for the customers who depend on us. That purpose is why we take on some of the hardest missions in spaceflight, and why we hold ourselves to the standard of mission success. Today, we did not meet that standard. We will work closely with our customer and other appropriate organizations to find the cause, determine the appropriate corrective action, and return to flight as quickly as possible. ULA is committed to full transparency with our customers, and they will have visibility into the investigation process, which utilizes rigorous methodology and corrective action. We will provide further updates when new information becomes available.November 15, 2026

Sadly for Amazon, the ULA, and folks who want a viable substitute for Starlink in the satellite internet business, this failure will surely delay the full commercialization of this service. ISPReview notes that Amazon has successfully launched 392 Kuiper communications satellites, but needs at least 500 for basic global coverage. Moreover, Amazon Leo service’s transition from the current commercial beta phase to launching properly at the end of 2026 now looks uncertain.

Reports indicate Amazon has already manufactured 1,000 satellites, but ULA, which is a joint venture of Boeing and Lockheed Martin, will need to analyze and assure customers that Sunday’s failure won’t be repeated. ULA's Vulcan vehicle had already been out of action for nine months after technical investigations into issues with its solid rocket side boosters and core BE-4 engines (made by Amazon Blue Origin).

For some context, the ULA has (or had) a great track record. Sunday's incident was its first total mission failure in its 20-year history. Early telemetry from the organization is already pointing towards some suspected causes of the destruction of the 40 satellites. Likely fault candidates include the first-stage BE-4 engines burning longer than planned, and there may have been some altitude issues near staging.

The headline failure and essential careful analysis of the anomaly won’t hold up Amazon’s other launch partners, though. A week from today, the Amazon Leo constellation is set to grow by 36 more satellites. They will be sent into orbit aboard a European Ariane 64 Block 2 (LE-04) rocket.

Amazon has approval for an initial constellation of 3,236 LEO broadband satellites, with 392 now in orbit, plus 5,105 for 5G service. Satellite tracking sites suggest Starlink already has 11,200+ satellites in orbit.

#10375. Nintendo will soon discontinue Google and Apple sign-ins

Nintendo Switch users have until January 25 to change how they sign in to the Nintendo Switch app on their smartphones.

The news was made available on an official Nintendo support page. That means you won’t be able to use your Google or Apple accounts to log in to your Nintendo account as of January 26. After January 26:

Instead of using 3rd-party account linking, Nintendo is recommending that users register a passkey for an exclusive, secure login experience through the Nintendo Switch app. If you forget to do this before January 26, you can always head to the Nintendo account page and run through the “forgot password” instructions. Nintendo recommends making sure you can log in to your account ASAP via non-Google or Apple links to ensure that you don’t run into problems before January.

Nintendo didn’t elaborate on the policy change, although some surmise it’s based on security-related measures. The advancement of AI has created bots that can hack into systems with unprecedented speed, so it’s possible that Nintendo is simply trying to shore up any possible area it cannot 100% control. Either way, if you use the Nintendo Switch app or have a Nintendo account of any kind, you should change how you log in immediately.

#10371. How to Use Developer Time Tracking Data to Improve Sprint Planning and Estimates

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Sprint estimates usually fail because development teams heavily rely on memory and assumptions instead of evidence. They assume similar tasks will take the same amount of time. Based on these half-baked assumptions, most managers plan around ideal sprint working hours. The backlog then fills with commitments that look realistic on paper but often end up delayed.

Actual development rarely follows that plan. There are always unexpected bugs and task dependencies that consume more time than teams account for in their estimates. Even the buffer time set aside for unexpected work may not be enough. This causes tasks to take longer than expected and pushes sprint commitments behind schedule.

Developer time tracking helps teams make better estimates with historical data. It shows how long previous tasks actually took, which provides managers with an evidence-based reference when they are planning similar work.

Reviewing this data before committing to a sprint scope turns estimation from guesswork into an evidence-based process.

Historical time data offers deeper insights, depending on how you collect and analyze it. Most importantly, it explains why planned work took longer than expected.

Before sprint planning, the following records should be reviewed.

Completed task duration: Check the time spent on finished tasks. Managers should compare similar work to understand typical development effort.

Actual versus estimated hours: Management should observe and identify differences between planned and recorded hours. If there are repeated delays, to some extent, it indicates inaccurate estimates.

Carryover work: Review unfinished tasks from previous sprints. Determine whether poor estimates or external delays caused the spillover..

Interruptions: Measure time spent on urgent requests and production issues. These activities reduce the development capacity that you had planned earlier.

Meetings and ceremonies: Review the time being spent on planning sessions and sprint reviews.

Bug-fix time: Examine hours spent resolving defects. You can also compare it to the time for developing new features.

Blocked tasks: Managers should account for the tasks delayed by missing approvals or technical dependencies.

Capacity differences: Compare team availability across sprints. Include leave and staffing changes in that time as well.

These records provide the most value when reviewed together. For example, a task may finish three days late even if the development work stayed within its estimated hours. The actual issue could be an external dependency.

Teams should understand these differences before using historical data to estimate future work.

Historical records provide a useful starting point for estimating new tasks. However, teams must interpret the data rather than simply copy previous estimates.

The following approaches can improve estimation accuracy.

The first step is to consider completed tasks that resemble upcoming work. Managers should compare their requirements and technical complexity. Moreover, the development environment should be considered.

For example, suppose a team needs to estimate a new API integration. Three previous integrations required 12, 15, and 18 hours of active work.

The average is 15 hours. This gives developers a reference point for the new estimate.

However, the average doesn't tell the entire story. The new integration may involve unfamiliar authentication methods or more complicated testing.

Review the reasons behind differences in previous task durations. Check whether developers encountered unexpected bugs or unclear requirements.

As a project manager, I'd consider these details before estimating for the next project. In my experience, they provide more context than an average alone.

Inaccurate estimates can happen; it doesn’t mean that there is a definite planning problem. However, repeated overruns across similar tasks may reveal an underlying issue.

Research suggests the pattern is widespread. A review of software estimation surveys reports that 60–80% of software projects encounter effort overruns, with the average overrun falling between 30% and 40%.

Start by calculating the difference between estimated and actual effort. Here is the formula for it.

Estimation variance (%) = [(Actual hours − Estimated hours) ÷ Estimated hours] × 100

Suppose a task was estimated at 10 hours but needed 14 hours. The actual effort exceeded the estimate by 40%.

You can compare the results across similar tasks.

For example, database migrations may repeatedly require more time than expected. This means that the team might be overlooking data validation or additional testing. Once the cause is clear, adjust future estimates accordingly.

Avoid increasing every estimate by the same percentage. Different types of work involve different risks. Teams can also break large tasks into smaller parts. This makes hidden development activities easier to identify.

The aim is to correct recurring estimation errors without unnecessarily increasing the entire sprint backlog.

Task duration doesn't always reflect the hours spent developing a feature.

A study of developer workdays by Meyer and colleagues, which tracked 2,197 hours of activity across 20 developers, found that coding accounted for around 21% of the typical day. Collaborative work such as meetings and email took up roughly 24%.

Consider a developer who starts a feature on Monday and finishes it on Wednesday. The task took three days to complete. This is pretty straightforward, and the estimation could be based on it; however, the developer may have spent several hours in meetings. Other than that, urgent support requests could also have consumed additional time.

The actual development effort could be much lower than three full working days. This distinction matters during estimation.

Teams should record focused development time separately from interruptions. They should also track waiting periods caused by blocked tasks. A more appropriate approach would be to use active development hours to estimate similar future work. They should also account for recurring interruptions when calculating sprint capacity.

For example, a task may regularly wait for security approval. Adding more development hours to its estimate won't resolve that delay.

Instead, the team should recognize the dependency and consider its effect on delivery.

If managers are able to separate these activities, it will help create better estimates without confusing effort with elapsed time.

A precise estimate can create false confidence when a task involves substantial uncertainty.

Suppose similar tasks previously required between 12 and 20 hours. If you estimate the next task at exactly 14 hours, it will overlook important differences.

A range of 12–20 hours provides a clearer picture of the possible effort. Developers can then discuss what might push the task toward either end of the range.

For example, the lower estimate can be directly applied to integration, and the upper estimate might be considered for unfamiliar code or additional testing.

In such scenarios, if the requirements stay unclear, consider a short technical investigation before finalizing the estimate. This can help identify dependencies and reduce uncertainty.

However, estimate ranges should still support practical planning decisions. Teams need to determine how much capacity to reserve for the work.

Choose a planning assumption that reflects the task's risk and available capacity.

As developers learn more about the task, they can refine the estimate.

Historical time data should affect how much work a team accepts during sprint planning. Better estimates offer little value if teams continue making unrealistic commitments.

If several sprints end with unfinished tasks, it is best that you first review why the team missed its commitments in the first place. Once you have identified the causes, use those findings to reduce the next sprint's scope.

Teams should prioritize work that supports the sprint goal. Moreover, avoid adding extra tasks just because they appear manageable during planning.

Production incidents and urgent requests can disrupt planned development. To get more reliable estimations, review how much time these activities consumed in previous sprints. Then reserve an appropriate amount of capacity.

Historical estimates become less reliable in case unfamiliar technology or additional dependencies are involved. It is best to break complex tasks into smaller components where possible. This helps developers assess the work more accurately.

Nominal capacity represents scheduled working hours, whereas actual capacity reflects the time available after accounting for other commitments.

The Scrum Guide identifies previous performance and upcoming capacity as important inputs to sprint forecasting.

When you are selecting backlog items, consider both. Additionally, avoid committing every available hour or deducting the same interruption twice.

Historical time data is useful for sprint planning. Most development teams already use project management tools that include basic time tracking features.

However, these tools don't always break down how developers spend their working hours. For clearer time data, a feature-specific time tracker is more useful because it provides more details about tasks and workloads.

TimeBee is one such developer time tracking tool I've used. It tracks working hours and time spent on projects and tasks. It also provides attendance records and workforce activity reports.

With this data, teams can review how much time previous tasks required. For example, developers can record hours spent building a feature. They can also track debugging and revisions separately. Later, managers can compare these records with the original estimates. This helps them plan similar work more accurately.

TimeBee also tracks website and application activity. These reports can help teams understand how working hours are distributed.

However, not all activities reflect actual development effort. Sometimes a developer just spends time reviewing documentation or discussing technical problems. Such work may not always appear in TimeBee as active computer use.

Apart from that, attendance, leave, and overtime records from TimeBee can help managers calculate sprint capacity. They can review developer availability before deciding how much work to include in the next sprint.

One thing to remember is that developer time tracking should help teams understand workload patterns. It shouldn't be used to monitor every minute of a developer's day. Management should be careful that the aim is to improve estimates and make more realistic sprint commitments.

Sprint retrospectives should include a review of how the planning is going. If you have made a plan, it doesn’t mean you cannot make changes to it. During sprints, keep comparing original estimates with actual task hours. Track your unfinished work for recurring delays.

Ask developers what caused significant differences. Some tasks may have required additional testing. Others may have been delayed by dependencies. Select one specific improvement for the next sprint.

Not every estimation difference indicates a planning failure. Unexpected technical challenges are part of software development.

The objective is to improve forecasts over time rather than eliminate every variance.

Historical time data provides a more stable foundation for sprint planning. Developer time tracking helps teams understand what is actually happening within the sprint rather than creating unnecessary pressure for them.

Other than that, the data also reveals how interruptions affect available development hours.

However, time data should guide planning rather than dictate it.

1. What is the difference between sprint velocity and time-based capacity planning?

Sprint velocity measures how much work a team has completed during a sprint. It is usually measured in story points. Contrary to this, time-based capacity planning calculates the hours available for planned work. It accounts for meetings and other responsibilities.

Velocity helps predict future delivery based on past performance. Capacity planning shows whether the team has enough time to meet its commitments.

2. Which time tracking metrics are most useful for improving Agile sprint forecasts? The most useful metrics include estimated versus actual hours, task completion time, and unplanned work.

Bug-fix time, rework, and blocked tasks also provide valuable insights. Teams should also review available capacity across previous sprints.

These metrics help sort out recurring delays and estimation errors. They allow teams to forecast workloads more accurately and make realistic sprint commitments.

**3. How can development teams improve estimates when historical time tracking data is incomplete? **

Teams can start with available task records and completed sprint backlogs. Developer feedback can help fill information gaps. Similar tasks from previous projects may also provide useful estimates.

When data is limited, teams should use estimate ranges instead of fixed hours. Consistent time tracking in future sprints will gradually provide more reliable planning data.

4. How can time tracking data help balance workloads across developers during sprint planning?

Time tracking data shows how much time developers spend on assigned tasks and other responsibilities. Managers can review these records to identify uneven workloads. They can then distribute upcoming tasks based on available capacity.

Technical expertise and task complexity should also influence assignments. This helps prevent over commitment and supports a more balanced workload across the development team.

#10369. Apple should have made the MacBook logo RGB

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The PROLED MacBook Pro’s logo is lit with boring white LEDs. Missed opportunity.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The PROLED MacBook Pro’s logo is lit with boring white LEDs. Missed opportunity.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

When Apple CEO John Ternus announced the first touchscreen Mac last month, the biggest reaction in the room wasn’t for the PROLED MacBook Pro’s OLED touchscreen or the thinner and lighter chassis; it was for the illuminated lid logo. It’s a throwback to the light-up logos on the old MacBooks, which Apple started phasing out in 2015, and which were completely gone by the 2018 MacBook Air redesign. It’s a nostalgia play, but boy did it work. It’s fun!

Just like in the old days, the new logo’s brightness is linked to the screen’s: The brighter or dimmer the screen, the brighter or dimmer the logo. But the light-up logo on the old MacBooks was illuminated by the screen’s backlight, and an OLED display doesn’t have one. Thanks to iFixit, we now know it’s lit by four white LEDs and synced with the screen’s brightness by software. But that means Apple could have used RGB LEDs.

Red, orange, yellow, green, blue, indigo, violet, or anything in between? On the lid of your new MacBook Pro? Why not? After all, Apple insists it’s still serious about gaming, and nothing says “serious about gaming” like RGB lighting.

I was secretly hoping users would be able to customize the illuminated lid cutout much like the Xbox logo on Microsoft’s Elite Series 2 controllers. But alas, no luck. You can turn off the illumination with some Terminal commands or take control of it with a third-party app. But when it’s lit, it’s only lit with white light. And iFixit’s teardown also showed the difficulty of accessing those LEDs (the screen delaminated and broke in the process). So we’re unlikely to see LED RGB mods for it unless someone’s brave enough to void their warranty and risk a pricey screen repair on their $3,000 laptop.

The most fun you can have with the Apple lid logo is to install a free third-party app like LogoLight and have it pulse, breathe, match the beat of any music you’re playing, or flash some Morse code. There’s some fun there, but if you combined these features with a splash of color we’d really be cooking.

MacBooks aren’t gaming laptops, so RGB lighting may seem like a mismatch, but multicolor LEDs could have been a cool Easter egg. Nobody thought Apple would put a touchscreen on a MacBook either, and the iPhone Duo and HomeView also show Apple is in a new age of experimentation during the early Ternus era. Maybe if the light-up lid logo ever comes to future MacBook Airs or a MacBook Neo, Apple could cut loose a little.

#10359. How to Improve Email Deliverability Rates

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

You wrote a good email. You hit send. Then half your list never saw it, because it went straight to spam.

That is a deliverability problem, and it costs more than most teams think. Every email that misses the inbox is a lead you paid for and never reached.

The good part is that deliverability is not luck. Mailbox providers like Gmail, Yahoo and Outlook judge you on a short list of things: who you are, who you send to, and how people react. Fix those and your inbox placement goes up.

This guide covers 10 email deliverability best practices in the order you should fix them, starting with SPF and ending with sender reputation.

Email deliverability is the share of your emails that land in the inbox instead of the spam folder or nowhere at all.

People often mix it up with delivery rate. They are not the same thing.

You can have a 99% delivery rate and still have most of your emails sitting in spam. That is why your email tool can say "delivered" while your open rate keeps dropping.

The big mailbox providers stopped treating authentication as optional.

Microsoft followed on May 5, 2025. If you send more than 5,000 emails a day to Outlook.com, Hotmail or Live addresses, you need all three records in place or your mail goes to junk.

Send less than 5,000 a day? Follow the rules anyway. The same checks decide inbox placement for small senders too, just without the public warning.

SPF (Sender Policy Framework) is a TXT record in your DNS. It lists the servers allowed to send email from your domain.

When your email arrives, the receiving server checks that list. If the sending server is not on it, the email looks suspicious.

DKIM (DomainKeys Identified Mail) adds a digital signature to each email. The receiving server uses a public key in your DNS to check two things: the email came from you, and nobody changed it on the way.

Your email provider gives you the DKIM record. You paste it into your DNS and switch signing on.

DKIM also survives forwarding, which SPF does not. If you can only get one of the two perfect, get DKIM right.

DMARC ties SPF and DKIM together. It tells mailbox providers what to do when an email fails both checks, and it sends you reports on who is sending mail under your domain name.

p=none means "take no action, just send me reports." It is the minimum Gmail, Yahoo and Microsoft accept from bulk senders.

Don't stop there. Read the reports for a few weeks, fix any real sender that is failing, then move up:

One more thing: DMARC needs alignment. The domain in your From address has to match the domain used in SPF or DKIM. Plenty of senders have all three records and still fail DMARC because their email tool signs with its own domain.

If you'd rather not handle this in-house, you can also hire a B2B cold email marketing agency such as IntentSignal, Martal or Belkins. A good agency will set up the domains, mailboxes, warm-up and SPF, DKIM and DMARC records for you, and keep an eye on deliverability while the campaigns run.

A new domain has no history. If it sends 10,000 emails on day one, it looks exactly like a spammer who just bought a domain.

Warm-up means starting small and growing a little each day, so mailbox providers can watch how people react to you.

Send to your most active contacts first. Their opens and replies are what build trust early. If bounces or complaints jump, hold at that volume until they settle.

And if you run cold outreach, do it from a separate domain or subdomain. That way a bad campaign can't hurt the domain your invoices and password resets come from.

A bigger list is not a better list. Dead addresses and people who stopped caring pull your numbers down.

Never buy a list. Bought lists are full of spam traps, which are addresses that exist only to catch senders who email people without permission. Hitting a few of them can get your domain blocklisted.

Yes, you lose some sign-ups. What you keep is real people with working inboxes who actually want your emails. That means fewer bounces, fewer complaints and better opens from day one.

It also keeps bots and fake addresses out of your forms, which matters a lot if your sign-up form is public.

Most email platforms add these for you. Check that yours does.

These two numbers tell you the most about where you stand.

The complaint figures come from Gmail's own rules. The bounce figures are the working limits most email platforms use.

A 0.3% complaint rate is only 3 complaints per 1,000 emails. It does not take much to cross that line.

If complaints are climbing, ask three questions:

Most complaint problems come from one of those three, not from the email itself.

Spam filters got smarter. A single word like "free" won't sink you anymore. Patterns will.

Replies help a lot. When someone replies, the mailbox provider sees a real conversation. Ask a question in your welcome email and you'll get some.

Sender reputation is the score mailbox providers give your domain and IP. Everything above feeds into it. A good score means inbox. A bad one means spam, no matter how good the email is.

You can't see the exact score, but these free tools get you close:

Check them weekly, and right after any big campaign.

A few habits protect your reputation over time:

Aim for 95% or higher inbox placement. Below 90% means something needs fixing, usually authentication or list quality.

The common causes are missing or broken SPF, DKIM or DMARC records, a high complaint rate, an old list with lots of dead addresses, or a new domain that sent too much too soon. Check authentication first because it is the fastest to fix.

DNS fixes like SPF and DKIM take effect within a day or two. Reputation is slower. Expect two to six weeks of steady, clean sending before inbox placement recovers.

Yes. The 5,000 mark is where Gmail, Yahoo and Microsoft start enforcing openly. Small senders are still judged on the same signals, and Gmail asks every sender for at least SPF or DKIM.

Only if you send enough to keep it warm, roughly 100,000 emails a month or more. Below that, a good shared IP from a trusted provider usually performs better.

Sender reputation covers both your IP address and your domain. Domain reputation is the part tied to your domain name, and it follows you even if you switch email providers. Gmail leans on domain reputation more than IP.

If you only have an hour this week, spend it on SPF, DKIM and DMARC. Nothing else works until those pass.

After that, clean your list and keep an eye on complaints. Send emails people asked for, to people who still want them, from a domain that proves who it is. Do that for a few weeks and your emails will land in the inbox again.

#10358. Overlocker uses vibecoded BIOS modification to target Intel's non-K overclocking restrictions

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

In an attempt to resurrect ASRock's discontinued SkyOC overclocking feature, an enthusiast has vibe-coded a motherboard BIOS using AI, potentially bringing non-K CPU overclocking to modern Intel CPUs. According to a post on X, Hardware 360º used Chat-GPT Astra to decompile and compare two versions of an ASRock BIOS, one released with SkyOC and another with the feature disabled. The AI reportedly identified the code responsible for enabling the overclocking workaround and modified it to create a BIOS ready for implementation on a compatible motherboard. Unfortunately, it remains to be seen whether the modified firmware can restore SkyOC on modern Intel platforms, as the workaround is yet to be fully tested.

For context, Intel offers multiplier-based overclocking only on its unlocked K-series processors like the Core i9-14900K and the newer Core Ultra 270K Plus. Non-K models have locked multipliers, which restrict users from pushing CPU frequencies. However, there’s another way to raise the speed of a processor by pushing the base clock itself. The base clock, or BCLK, typically runs at 100 MHz and works alongside the CPU multiplier to determine its operating frequency. For example, raising the base clock speed of a CPU to 105 MHz increases the clock speed by 5%. The only downside is that BCLK adjustments can also affect the entire motherboard, including memory and PCIe-based components, thereby potentially causing system instability.

ASRock's SkyOC technology, which was introduced for Intel's Skylake processors in 2016, allowed users to overclock locked non-K CPUs through BCLK adjustments. Certain motherboards also featured independent clock generators, allowing CPU clock adjustments without affecting the rest of the system. Intel was not particularly pleased with the workaround, and ASRock eventually had to remove SkyOC from its motherboards via BIOS updates. Additionally, Intel introduced microcode changes to prevent non-K overclocking.

The current experiment revisits that workaround by identifying the original BIOS code and adapting it for modern motherboards. Hardware 360º claims Intel's restrictions include a mechanism that triggers a shutdown when BCLK exceeds 103 MHz, and that SkyOC originally worked around this by tricking the processor with a false report of the actual BCLK value.

Finding the original code for SkyOC does not necessarily mean it can be successfully ported to a modern motherboard BIOS. Newer Intel processors use different firmware, microcode, and clock-management mechanisms, which would make adapting the workaround considerably more complicated. Flashing modified motherboard firmware also comes with its own set of risks, including the possibility of rendering the board completely unusable. Nevertheless, it is interesting to see how AI-assisted reverse engineering can help gain access to BIOS modifications that would otherwise require a considerable amount of effort.

#10346. How to Use IP Geolocation to Stop Bots and Fraud (Without Hurting UX)

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Several methods can help you deal with online threats and strengthen security. All are effective, but each has a trade-off. For example, the harder you make it for bots to affect your website’s performance, the more likely you are to frustrate real customers.

Those of you who browse online daily know that credential stuffing, fake signups & payment fraud keep growing. To prevent fraud, businesses install CAPTCHAs & hard blocks that treat bots and legitimate users the same, diverting them to your competitors.

IP geolocation maps an IP address to a physical location & network details, letting you spot suspicious traffic before it reaches sensitive actions. Used well, it’s an invisible, low-friction risk signal. In this guide, you will learn how to flag high-risk activity and apply soft geo-fencing with step-up authentication.

Today, an IP lookup returns far more than a country & city. It can also reveal several other parameters, like

Each of these fields acts as a clue telling who is really behind a request.

This data powers anomaly detection. You can start by building a baseline for normal behavior for each user with aspects like

Then, you can watch for deviations from that pattern. An attacker won’t look like the account’s real owner, so you'll catch them.

Geolocation makes several common attack patterns easy to spot:

The first is traffic bursts from data center ASNs. These are bots, as real customers don’t browse from cloud servers

Many accounts created from one IP or subnet. This is a clear sign of fake signups

Impossible travel, such as logins from two distant locations within minutes

Timezone vs. IP mismatch. A device’s clock disagrees with its apparent location.

Note: Geolocation is just a signal, not a verdict. VPNs, travel, & mobile carriers can all create false alarms, so it works best when combined with other signals. That’s exactly why a proportional, soft response beats a hard block.

In short, IP geolocation turns a bare IP address into context, helping you separate real users from automated or fraudulent traffic.

Not every IP geolocation anomaly deserves the same response. The simplest way to stay consistent is to define a clear risk-signal framework.

That can be a short list of checks. Each check can have a weight that reflects how strongly it suggests fraud.

With risk scoring, you can turn these checks into a decision. You just have to assign each signal a numeric weight.

For example, medium can be 10 & 30 can be high.

Now, you need to add up every signal that comes up & map the total to thresholds like

A single medium signal passes quietly, but several can trigger action.

If you treat this as an automatic block, it would punish legitimate customers. Instead, let it raise the score & you take action only if it stacks with other signals, like a hosting IP or a new device.

Now that you know how to quantify the risk, here is how you can protect yourself without damaging UX.

Before soft, let us learn about hard blocking. This is where you completely deny or permanently ban traffic from specific geographic regions.

Now, hard blocking by country may look effective, but it can backfire quickly. Here is how.

In the worst case, determined bot operators simply rotate to IPs in allowed regions, so the only people you stop are your real customers.

This is where the balance between security and user experience becomes critical. As the digital strategist and Founder at LawRank, Mariano Rodriguez emphasizes, "Your website's security should be a silent guardian, not a roadblock. If your fraud prevention creates friction, you aren't just stopping bots—you're turning away real clients. True digital trust is built when backend protections are seamless and invisible to the legitimate user."

Now, with soft geo-fencing, you can respond to risk proportionally instead of making a simple allow-or-deny call. Instead of totally banning the users, you adjust the level of friction to match the risk score from the previous section:

**Note: **For soft geo-fencing, passkeys are safest because they are phishing-resistant. Only a legitimate user can take the biometric tap and pass.

Before we start, let’s understand Express.js middleware. An Express.js middleware for Geolocation Risk Scores is a custom or 3rd party function that intercepts the incoming HTTP requests, extracts the client's IP address, fetches a security risk profile based on their geographic location & attaches that data to the request object (req) before it reaches your route handlers.

Here is how you can use it for fraud prevention and bot mitigation:

Note: You can make it stronger by adding velocity checks (too many attempts from one IP or account) & device fingerprints.

From a broader angle, IP geolocation isn't the final verdict. It is just a signal. But with this signal, you can score each request, respond in proportion to the risk & save hard blocks for clear abuse. This increases the friction for bots and keeps the flow easy for legitimate users.

#10345. How to replace the battery on Pixel Tag [Video]

The battery on the Pixel Tag is rated for about a year, but what happens when you need to replace it? Here’s how to replace it.

Due to the shape and design of the Pixel Tag, you might have a bit of difficulty getting the backplate off quickly to throw in a new battery when it’s time.

Having had a few problems myself (yes, a me problem most likely) due to the slick, smooth shape, I figured that, even though it might insult the intelligence of many, the process was at least documented so that anyone having issues has a reference to come to if they’re struggling to get that backplate off.

You will have difficulty if you try to do this one-handed. Instead, clasp the Pixel Tag with one thumb at the base or midframe, then with your other thumb twist anticlockwise until the backplate comes loose and the battery will drop out.

The first time you do this, it will be harder due to the tight factory seal. However, once it’s open, the process gets easier to replicate. Here’s a little demonstration of how to get it off without it slipping and being flung into the ether:

Be aware that you may need to open and reseat the battery if Find Hub does not recognize the Pixel Tag again when you’ve done this. Even with the factory-supplied battery, it can misalign if you don’t place it in the correct position and fully close the door when reassembling.

If you’re wondering what type of battery you need, CR2032 are the standard for watches and most Android trackers. They’re super cheap with a 10-pack of Amazon Basics batteries for just $10, Duracell offers a 4-pack for under $7 but you can even get unbranded 20-packs for around $9. All fairly cheap at various points in the quality spectrum.

It can’t hurt to have some on hand when you do eventually need to replace the battery in your Pixel Tag. You can get the tracker from Amazon and the Google Store right now.

#10341. Anthropic says Claude Mythos Preview cut AMD MI355X onboarding from up to 12 months to around three days

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

If AI can write the low-level code that ties models to Nvidia, CUDA matters less.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Anthropic told IPO investors that its Claude Mythos Preview model did the software work required to get its AI running on AMD’s GPUs in about three days, with “basically no human intervention,” a job it says used to take six to 12 months. The three days cover prompt processing for one Anthropic model on AMD MI355X GPUs “at a useful level of performance,” with Mythos porting or writing “almost 100% of the kernels.” That kind of low-level code, which ties an AI model to one company’s chips, has long been part of Nvidia’s CUDA moat, and an AI that can write it in days would suggest that moat is eroding.

Tom Brown, Anthropic’s co-founder and chief compute officer, made the time comparison, noting to investors that the original meant using a team of “dozens of engineers.” The company already runs its models on multiple kinds of chips — on GPUs from Nvidia and AMD, Google’s TPUs, and AWS Trainium — and its fleet is managed as “one fungible pool of compute.” Brown called the early multi-chip decision “an unconventional bet.” It may have been a wise one, given that Google’s TPUs can now natively run AI models built in PyTorch, and Meta uses them in production.

Anthropic's claim that the job used to take six to 12 months comes from past work, from its “historical experience bringing up new accelerators using traditional engineering approaches,” where tailoring the code to each hardware platform was a challenge. Removing that onboarding barrier could reduce things to a purchase decision, and Anthropic started working with AMD GPUs for the first time this year, with an agreement for up to 2 gigawatts now in place.

CUDA, Nvidia’s programming platform, is very mature at nearly two decades old. AMD has an equivalent, ROCm, with HIP as its programming layer. Code written for CUDA has to be transformed to work there, and AMD even built a tool called HIPify for the process. There is still a conversion cost to switching, and AMD’s vice president of AI software, Anush Elangovan, told EE Times in April that “Claude is better than HIPify because it has web search built in.”

AMD has been making the argument that AI can write this type of code since August of last year, presenting a research agent that fell short of human experts at the time. In September of that year, AMD CEO Lisa Su said on the Possible podcast that much of AI software is written in CUDA, which needs to be “translated to AMD,” and “a ton of that can now be done in software.”

Moving forward to July earlier this year, AMD’s senior vice president of AI, Vamsi Boppana, said its engineers were using AI models to generate GPU kernels, and Su said that when she asked whether her team was helping the Anthropic engineer working on the MI355X, she was told they didn’t need any help. Boppana said that in some cases, the resulting kernels were even “better than the most manually tuned versions.” Under its July agreement with Anthropic, AMD is also using Claude to optimize workloads for its Instinct GPUs and to speed up ROCm development.

At AMD’s Advancing AI event in July, OpenAI’s Philippe Tillet said that once OpenAI got its Helios racks, a single engineer confirmed everything worked within a few days, and that “agents are taking over our jobs, in a sense, as kernel engineers.” Before the event, AMD’s Andrew Dieckman, corporate vice president and general manager of the company’s data center GPU business, told reporters that he has had practically zero conversations with AMD’s customers about CUDA.

Published GEAK, an AI agent that writes GPU kernels; against 30 expert-written kernels it got 19 right, at 0.92 times the experts’ speed on average

Asked about AI coding tools, said much of the code written for CUDA can now be translated to AMD in software: “We’re using software to significantly optimize our customers’ code so that they can run on AMD”

Anush Elangovan, AMD vice president of AI software

Compared Claude with HIPify, AMD’s tool for converting CUDA code: “Claude is better than HIPify because it has web search built in”

“We use a ton of AI to create the kernels that we have”; “nobody knows our architecture better than we do”

Andrew Dieckman, AMD corporate vice president and general manager, data center GPU business

“I have almost zero conversations with our customers about CUDA at this point in time”

Vamsi Boppana, AMD senior vice president of AI

AI-assisted GPU programming will “remove any remaining barriers to broad adoption”

“agents are taking over our jobs, in a sense, as kernel engineers”

Tom Brown, Anthropic co-founder and chief compute officer

One engineer asked Claude to bring up an MI355X rack and left it running “over the weekend”

Tom Brown, Anthropic co-founder and chief compute officer

Mythos “got Claude running on AMD in just three days with basically no human intervention”; the slide footnote limits the three days to prompt processing for one model

Until this week, though, all of this came from AMD or its stage, not from Anthropic to its own investors. Anthropic buys the chips rather than selling them, and it’s heading into its IPO next week, so its multi-chip approach — now with at least some numbers to back it up — carries more weight than a chipmaker’s claims. Its prospectus says it in writing, too, though without a number: “We can onboard new chip platforms in a fraction of the time it once took.”

Not stated; left running “over the weekend”

Its “leading model” on an MI355X rack, with performance climbing

Mythos, with “basically no human intervention”

Getting “Claude running on AMD”; Claude “ported or wrote almost 100% of the kernels”

Work that “took dozens of engineers months”

IPO roadshow video, slide footnote, Nov. 10

Bringing up “prompt processing for one Anthropic model on AMD MI355X GPUs at a useful level of performance”

Six to 12 months (“~6-12-month estimate”), from “historical experience bringing up new accelerators”

Its engineering teams, “leveraging Claude”

Bringing “new chip platforms into production”; no chip named

What it calls “intense engineering effort over many months”

The prompt processing (prefill) part of the workload is the step where a model reads and processes what it’s been given before it starts replying, and Anthropic hasn’t said whether the replying step (decode) was included as well. Anthropic’s chart, with its axis labeled “Time to onboard new silicon,” shows both lines reaching the same “~100%” mark without saying 100% of what. The company also hasn’t said how fast the model ran on AMD’s GPUs versus Nvidia’s, and that’s the comparison we want to see to show whether AMD’s chips, running AI-written code, can match Nvidia silicon.

On the other hand, Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang said in an April interview with Dwarkesh Patel that Nvidia already uses AI on its own kernels and knows its chips best — “nobody knows our architecture better than we do” — and can get the most out of them. Asked why labs like Anthropic use other accelerators, he called Anthropic “a unique instance, not a trend.” Nvidia and AMD, among seven companies, are buying $26.0 billion of Anthropic’s stock at the IPO price, so Nvidia is investing in the very company Huang called the exception.

In late July, SemiAnalysis published an article, “Can AMD break the CUDA Moat?,” which essentially said that software creation is not the same moat it was even a year ago. That said, the catch for AMD is that with agentic coding, “each agent requires GPUs to test their code against,” which means that the AI writing the code still needs the hardware. It has found a constant lack of GPUs inside AMD for this type of development, which has held AMD back and may be a new bottleneck.

That leaves CUDA’s moat somewhere in between. Getting a model running on a new chip, once the expensive part, appears to be getting cheaper, and better AI may wear down Nvidia’s claim that it knows its hardware best. As for Anthropic’s fungible pool, the chips in it aren’t fully interchangeable — Google’s TPUs are increasingly specialized, for example — and getting the most out of any of them still takes the hardware to test on. The moat is narrowing, but is not gone.

Anthropic’s first AMD MI450 chips, in Helios racks, are expected in the first half of 2027. That’s a new generation and an opportunity to see if the three-day bring-up can be repeated. Anthropic’s IPO comes sooner, after which it will report as a public company.

#10340. Google Voice appears to finally be working on RCS support

It might finally be happening. A new Google Voice update plants seeds of RCS support, though the exact details remain unclear.

About APK Insight: In this “APK Insight” post, we’ve decompiled the latest version of an application that Google uploaded to the Play Store. When we decompile these files (called APKs, in the case of Android apps), we’re able to see various lines of code within that hint at possible future features. Keep in mind that Google may or may not ever ship these features, and our interpretation of what they are may be imperfect. We’ll try to enable those that are closer to being finished, however, to show you how they’ll look in case that they do ship. With that in mind, read on.

Google Voice is an invaluable tool for accessing one phone number across a host of different devices, but to say it’s becoming less impactful without support for RCS is a bit of an understatement. Now that virtually every Android phone and iPhone supports the new messaging experience, Google Voice is less useful both for business and consumer users.

Google Voice for Android version 2026.11.01.1014278023 doesn’t enable RCS, but it does, for the first time, actually hint that the functionality might be on its way. In a teardown of the app, one simple string stands out:

That points to a new send button used for RCS messages rather than SMS. Google Messages similarly changes its send button to differentiate between the two standards.

This is great news. I can have both options now to text friends and businesses with RCS as a fallback option on Google Voice because I don't want to give out my Google messages number.

This update contains a host of other new strings around images, videos, and more, but none of them are directly tied to RCS support.

The folks over at Android Authority dug up some other tidbits about RCS including an “RCS chat preference” toggle and various RCS actions and messages. But, again, nothing is live just yet, and there’s no word on when it might arrive, if ever.

Google has been championing RCS support for over a decade now, so it’s long past due for Voice, a long-running Google product, to get on board. While the tidbits in this update don’t serve as any confirmation RCS if coming to Voice, it’s the most exciting update on the matter in years.

#10339. This tiny gamepad with dual analog thumbsticks pushes the limits of portable controllers

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Larger hands may struggle with the Atom Plus, but it squeezes a lot of functionality into an extremely tiny controller.

Larger hands may struggle with the Atom Plus, but it squeezes a lot of functionality into an extremely tiny controller.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

As much as I appreciate the ingenious design of the Nintendo Switch’s Joy-Con, my family and I can’t stand using them as standalone gamepads. So when we travel with the Switch 2, we always bring at least one full-size gamepad, plus a couple of 8BitDo’s Zero 2 controllers for games that don’t use analog thumbsticks. I’ve spent years hunting for an alternative that pairs the size of the Zero 2 with as much of the functionality of a larger controller as I can get, and CRKD’s new Atom Plus is nearly exactly that. But if you have larger hands like I do, you may find the gamepad’s focus on portability a struggle.

Measuring around 3.5 inches wide, the $29.99 Atom Plus is about a half-inch smaller than the 8BitDo Zero 2 but squeezes in considerably more features and functionality. The most obvious is a pair of symmetrical analog thumbsticks with anti-drift tunneling magnetoresistance technology. You also get shoulder buttons and triggers, rumble feedback, a dedicated home button, motion controls, and customization through a mobile app.

Like many third-party controllers, the Atom Plus works with multiple platforms, including the Switch and Switch 2, PCs, Android, iOS, and various smart TVs. And just like other gamepads, pairing to different devices requires putting the Atom Plus into one of several Bluetooth modes by holding down a combination of buttons at power-up, which in this case is the home button plus a specific direction on the D-pad. It’s easy to do, but never easy to remember: I frequently needed to grab the manual or quickly Google which direction I need to press when switching devices. The Atom Plus already features a bunch of technical info etched into the back of the controller, so I’d love to see these important pairing instructions included there, too. (And that goes for all multiplatform gamepads.)

I primarily tested the Atom Plus with the Nintendo Switch 2 since I mostly plan to use the controller as a travel accessory. The thumbsticks are about the same size as those on the original Switch Joy-Con, which means they’re smaller than the Switch 2’s. And while I prefer an asymmetrical layout, the Atom Plus’ sticks are very good, with a little more tension than Nintendo’s that I find actually helps with precision movements given their limited travel.

While the overall build quality feels excellent, the Atom Plus’ tiny footprint creates some compromises and challenges. The front action buttons and D-pad feel nice and squishy, but the gamepad’s controls are a little crowded. When pressing down or right on the D-pad, my thumb regularly bumps the left thumbstick. It doesn’t hinder gameplay, but I do find it an annoying distraction.

The shoulder buttons use digital microswitches, as do the triggers, which means the latter can’t be used for racing games that support analog acceleration controls. I like that the two sets of buttons on the top edge sit at slightly different heights, but I would have preferred a little more differentiation since I’ve occasionally accidentally pressed the triggers when I meant to hit the shoulder buttons.

I was impressed with the Atom Plus’ customizability, however. When paired to an accompanying mobile app, you can check the controller’s remaining charge, turn off rumble feedback, see its unique product number (by tapping your phone to an NFC chip inside the controller), and remap any of its controls. That includes the pair of dual-function shortcut levers that replicate the Switch’s plus and minus buttons and screenshot shortcut.

Those levers move up and down and cleverly reduce the number of buttons crowding the front of the Atom Plus, but they feel a little too small, which is more or less the general theme. It’s a tiny gamepad that’s great for travel or stashing in your everyday carry. But, unsurprisingly, it probably won’t find its way into your regular gaming rotation. It’s assumed that players will be willing to settle for the compromise of occasionally contorting their fingers in weird, new ways for one of the smallest fully featured controllers on the market.

I don’t think a dual-stick controller could get any smaller than the Atom Plus without making too many ergonomic compromises. So while I applaud CRKD for the careful balancing act behind the Atom Plus’ design, and would certainly recommend it for those wanting a very capable controller that’s also cheap and portable, I’m now curious how it compares to Microsoft’s recently announced Xbox Nano controller that’s “surprisingly comfortable,” according to The Verge’s Tom Warren. It’s $20 more expensive than CRKD’s and noticeably larger, but it adds analog triggers, a more spacious design, and stubby grips making it easier to hold for those of us with beefy mitts.
