#10335. 5 Best AI Anime Video Generators in 2026, and Are AI Anime Videos Worth Making?

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

AI video generators can now turn a prompt or a character image into a few seconds of animation, and anime is one of the most popular styles people ask for. But anime is also one of the hardest styles to get right: flat colors, thin linework and stylized motion expose the weaknesses of video models quickly. For developers and designers, the useful question is not which tool is “best”, but what each one actually offers, what it cannot do yet, and how to fit it into a real production workflow.

This article compares five generators on what their own pages state, covers the problems they all share, asks whether AI anime videos are worth making, and then explains how the models work and how designers and developers can build a practical workflow, including ffmpeg and API-integration examples. “Best” here depends on the job: the tools are listed in no ranking order, each is linked once to its official site for reference, and the comparison is based on public vendor pages reviewed in November 2026. Features, models and pricing change often, so verify current details before relying on any of them.

We reviewed each vendor’s own site and compared what it states on the same points. This is a documentation-based comparison, not a lab benchmark. Where a page does not state something, we say so instead of guessing.

AI anime video generator by Pollo AI sits within a general AI video platform. On its main site, Pollo describes generating video from text prompts or uploaded images and editing existing videos, such as adding, changing, removing or restyling content. It also offers AI image generation, so a character image and the animation can be made in one place.

Strengths for anime work: text-to-video and image-to-video in the same tool, plus video editing and restyling, which suits experimenting with looks.

Limitations and gaps: the pages we could read do not name the underlying models, list credit amounts or pricing, or describe anime-specific controls such as reference characters or first and last frame inputs. You will need to test consistency yourself.

Fits: creators who want image, video and editing tools in one platform and are comfortable testing results before committing.

Vidu lists text-to-video, image-to-video and reference-to-video modes. For image-to-video you can set first and last frames. Reference-to-video lets you upload several images of a character, object or scene, and saved references can be reused through a My References feature. The site highlights 2D animation and diverse artistic styles, mentions 1080p output and offers an API platform aimed at enterprise use.

Strengths for anime work: the clearest set of consistency tools among the five pages we reviewed, with first and last frames, multi-image references and reusable saved characters.

Limitations and gaps: the page does not specify clip durations beyond examples, detail its anime-specific templates, or list prices. Free credits are mentioned without an amount.

Fits: sequences built around a recurring character, and teams that want an API.

Kling AI presents Kling 4.0 for video, described as offering stable dynamic motion and an audio-visual experience, alongside an image model, a Motion Control tool and a node-based Canvas editor for working through scripts, storyboards, images and video. It also lists MCP and CLI support for batch generation and automated production, and an API platform with documentation.

Strengths for anime work: motion-focused tooling and a workflow environment for storyboards and multi-step production, plus automation options for developers.

Limitations and gaps: the page we reviewed does not detail image-to-video specifics, first and last frame controls, camera control, durations, video resolution or pricing, and it gives no anime-specific guidance.

Fits: teams that value motion control and scripted or batch pipelines.

Hailuo AI, from MiniMax, describes creating AI videos and images from text or photos. Its page shows a reference field and an Omni Reference option, a banner for a model that mentions multimodal generation and editing, and many photo-based templates such as photo-to-motion, transformation and style transfer.

Strengths for anime work: an accessible, template-driven way to animate a still image and try stylized effects quickly.

Limitations and gaps: the page does not explain how reference works, or state camera control, durations, resolution, API availability or pricing. Template-driven results may be harder to control precisely than prompt- and frame-driven workflows.

Fits: quick experiments and social clips made from a single image.

Dreamina describes itself as an all-in-one creative platform for images, videos and talking avatars. For video it lists text-to-video, image-to-video, sketch-to-video, cartoon video generation and reference-to-video, using several Seedance models, along with editing examples such as green-screen replacement. It also offers image generation and upscaling.

Strengths for anime work: a broad toolset, including cartoon-oriented generation, references and editing in one environment.

Limitations and gaps: no official maximum duration or video resolution is stated, and the page notes that displayed pricing is for reference only and that final pricing requires signing in. The breadth of features and models can make it harder to know which one to use for a given shot.

Fits: creators who want images, video and avatars under one roof.

“Not stated” means the vendor page we reviewed did not say, not that the feature does not exist. Treat the table as a checklist of questions to answer in your own tests.

It depends on the job and on what you compare them with. Against a full studio production they are limited. Against having no animation at all, they change what a small team or solo creator can attempt.

Treat the generator as a fast prototyping and shot-making assistant, not a replacement for writing, direction and design. Audience reaction also varies, and some viewers are openly critical of AI-made work, so quality and disclosure both matter.

Most current generators are diffusion models extended to video. As in image diffusion, the model starts from random noise and removes it step by step, guided by your inputs. The extension to video, described in research such as Video Diffusion Models, is that the model denoises a whole sequence of frames together, so that motion and appearance stay coherent over time. Many systems work in a compressed latent space rather than on raw pixels to keep generation affordable.

Create an original character sheet with front, side and three-quarter views, a fixed color palette and a clear silhouette. Keep it simple: fewer fine details means less flicker.

Break the sequence into short shots with one action and one camera move each. A four-shot, twenty-second sequence is more realistic than one long generation.

Start each shot from a clean keyframe of your character. Where a tool supports first and last frames or reference images, use them to define the pose and keep identity stable.

Describe subject, action, camera and mood, and add a style anchor. For example: “The girl slowly turns toward the camera, wind moves her hair, petals drift past, gentle push-in, 2D cel shading, clean linework.”

Scrub through each clip for warped hands, drifting faces and flickering lines. Discard or regenerate failures instead of hoping nobody notices.

Join the best takes, color-match them, add sound and captions, and add disclosure where a platform requires it.

Developers can make the pipeline repeatable. Two patterns help: scripted post-processing with ffmpeg, and a resilient job-based client for any generation API.

The script below extracts a last frame to seed the next shot, normalizes clips to a common size and frame rate so they can be joined, concatenates them, and optionally holds each frame twice for an “on twos” look. Adjust sizes and frame rates to your project.

Video generation is usually asynchronous: you submit a job, then poll or receive a callback. The sketch below shows the pattern with a timeout and backoff. The endpoints and fields are placeholders, so follow your provider’s documentation for real ones.

Most are diffusion models for video. They start from noise and remove it step by step across a sequence of frames, guided by a text prompt and often a starting image or reference images, until coherent motion appears.

Usually, for consistency. The image locks the character and style, while the prompt only needs to describe motion.

Thin lines and flat color regions make small frame-to-frame changes visible. Simpler designs, slower motion and post-processing reduce it.

Partly. Reference images, saved characters and shared keyframes help, but you should still review every shot for drift.

For short clips, concept previews and storyboards, often yes. For long, dialogue-driven stories, not yet.

It depends on the tool and plan. Read the current terms and make sure you have rights to any characters or assets you upload.

Run the same character image and prompt through each, then compare consistency, line stability, motion quality and cost per usable second.

AI anime video generators are useful, but uneven. The five tools reviewed here differ in input modes, consistency tools, developer access and how much their own pages say, and none removes the core problems of consistency, flicker and limited acting. The practical approach is to test several tools on the same shots, keep your workflow tool-agnostic, design characters that are simple and consistent, and review every clip before it ships. Used that way, they are a capable prototyping and shot-making aid, not a substitute for design and direction.

“It’s really happening. I promise. For real.” Those are the first words from Roy Graham, Head of Story at Magic: The Gathering, in a video posted yesterday announcing Planeswalker, the animated series heading to Netflix in 2027.

#10331. Minisforum Elite Mini M2 Air-304 review: A quiet and compact Wildcat

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Minisforum’s smallest and cheapest mini PC packing Intel's Wildcat Lake silicon is here. It is small, quiet, and easy to stow away, but this machine's performance is really only for light workloads.

Capable enough for home office and media consumption

Only old or lightweight games and emulators are playable

Not a cheap option unless you have spare parts and go barebones

Why you can trust Tom's Hardware Our expert reviewers spend hours testing and comparing products and services so you can choose the best for you. Find out more about how we test.

Minisforum’s Intel-based M2 line includes mini PCs with powerful processors such as the Core Ultra X7 Processor 358H (with 16C/16T CPU and B390 graphics), and the Core Ultra 7 356H (16C/16T). But there are more modest systems, too. The M2 Air-304 here is the most affordable option, based around the Intel Core 3 processor 304 with its 5C/5T CPU (1x Cougar Cove P-core and 4x Darkmont LP E-cores) and an iGPU with just a single Xe3 core.

No surprise, the little brother in the pack, both in terms of physical size and computing resources, is the most affordable. It currently starts at $263 for a barebones version. However, our test sample with 16GB DDR5-5600, a 500GB SSD, and Windows 11 Pro is listed at $559 - direct. That’s an introductory price, and Minisforum’s small text threatens that our configuration will bump up to $699 at some point in the future. Such a rise would put this product offering on very shaky ground, and we see B&H Photo has already raised the price at $729.

Overall, this Mini PC was pleasant to use, though. It ran quietly and only strained in gaming and productivity benchmarks which put it under loads that casual users aren’t going to be running on such a machine.

The Mini M2 Air looks stylish enough, and feels solid. Looks can be deceiving, though, and the top shell is a silver colored plastic, not a metallic alloy. Sometimes a metal shell can be used to help conduct away heat, but that seems less important with this actively cooled machine with a 15W processor. We note that there are thermal pads connecting the installed RAM and SSD to the bottom of this mini PC, which is metal.

A VESA mounting bracket is provided in the box if you prefer to hide the tiny device out of sight. It doesn’t have a remote power switch like the MSI Cubi NUC AI+ 3MG we reviewed earlier in the year, though, so you’ll have to consider reachability. A simple LED-lit power button is on the front of the device, alongside a couple of handy ports (more on the ports later). It also has a reset / clear CMOS hole on the front, and a Kensington lock slot on the rear.

Minisforum’s component choices mean this is a particularly small and light mini PC. The official M2 Mini specs state it is 112 x 112 x 37.15mm, which is roughly 4.4 x 4.4 x 1.5-inches. That’s a volume of 0.47 liters. It weighs in at just 440 grams, which is 0.97 pounds.

The power supply isn’t built-in. There’s a surprisingly bulky 65W power brick in the box, which connects to using a detachable figure-eight cable. However, this mini PC can be powered by USB-C, too, so you could pack a far more compact multipurpose power brick if portability is important.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Intel Core 3 304 (5C/5T) ‘Wildcat Lake,’ up to 4.3 GHz, 15W

Intel integrated graphics, 1x Xe3 core runs at up to 2.3 GHz, supports up to 3x displays

16GB in single SODIMM slot which can fit up to a 64GB DDR5-6400 module

1x M.2 2280 PCIe 4.0 x4 NVMe SSD slot (up to 2TB), microSD card expansion

Front: 2x USB-A 3.2 Gen 2, 3.5mm audio. Rear: 1x USB4 (40Gbps, DP Alt Mode, PD), 1x USB-C 10Gbps, 1x USB 2.0, 2x HDMI 2.0, 2x RJ-45 2.5GbE LAN, DC-IN. Side: microSD card slot.

Windows 11; barebone model available without OS

$559.00 limited-time price(16GB RAM, 500GB SSD, Windows 11 Pro)

On the front of the Minisforum Elite Mini M2 Air-304 there are 2x USB-A 3.2 Gen 2 ports and a 3.5mm audio jack. Turn it to the side and you’ll see the microSD card slot. Then, around the back you have access to 1x USB4 (40Gbps, DP Alt Mode, PD), 1x USB-C 10Gbps, 1x USB 2.0, 2x HDMI 2.0, 2x RJ-45 2.5GbE LAN, and a DC-IN barrel jack.

Some might like to have at least one of the Type-C ports on the front, instead of two Type-As. But for its size the Mini M2 Air packs quite a few ports, including the very capable USB4, two HDMI ports, and two Ethernet ports. Realistically, a machine like this won’t be connected to oodles of wired peripherals, but if you found the port count lacking, USB4 allows for a capable and fast dock.

Wireless connectivity seems adequate, with an M.2 2230 card inside providing Wi-Fi 6E and Bluetooth 5.4. I had no problems with either during the test period. The wireless card is socketed. It's located under the SSD, making it easy to repair or upgrade down the line.

Accessing the components inside the Minisforum Mini M2 Air is straightforward, but you need to peel off all the four rubber feet to get to four long fastening screws. When they’re removed, just flip off the metal bottom to access the M.2 slots and SODIMM. Be aware there’s some adhesive force holding this metal plate in place, due to the sticky thermal pads on the RAM and storage.

There’s just a single M.2 2280 storage slot and one SODIMM slot, both populated. That means, there’s no way to upgrade the RAM than swapping the module you already have in place. Our sample came with a 16GB DDR5-5600 SODIMM, but the system supports up to DDR5-6400. This only non-barebones configuration also came with a 500GB SSD in the M.2 slot.

Intel Core Ultra X7 358H (16C/16T: 4P + 8E + 4 LPE), up to 4.8 GHz

Intel Arc B390 (Xe3, 12 cores, up to 2.5 GHz)

Intel Core Ultra 9 386H (16C/16T: 6P + 8E + 2LPE), up to 4.9 GHz

Intel Graphics (Xe3, 4 Cores, up to 2.5 GHz)

AMD Ryzen AI 9 HX 470 (12C / 24T:  4x Zen 5 , 8x Zen 5c), up to 5.2 GHz

Radeon 890M (RDNA 3.5, 16 CUs, up to 3.1 GHz)

AMD Ryzen AI 9 HX 370  (12C / 24T:  4x Zen 5 , 8x Zen 5c), up to 5.1 GHz

Radeon 890M (RDNA 3.5, 16 CUs, up to 2.9 GHz)

AMD Ryzen AI 9 HX 470 (12C / 24T:  4x Zen 5 , 8x Zen 5c), up to 5.2 GHz

Radeon 890M (RDNA 3.5, 16 CUs, up to 3.1 GHz)

Intel Core Ultra 9 185H (16C/22T: 6P + 8E + 2LPE), up to 5.1 GHz

Intel Arc graphics (Xe-LPG, 8 cores, up to 2.35 GHz)

AMD Ryzen AI Max+ 395 (16C/32T: 16x Zen 5), up to 5.1 GHz

Radeon 8060S (RDNA 3.5, 40 CUs, up to 2.9 GHz)

Intel Core 3 processor 304 (5C/5TL 1P + 4LPE), up to 4.3 GHz

Intel Graphics (Xe3, 1 Cores, up to 2.3 GHz)

We have Intel’s latest 18A silicon inside this tiny PC, but not that much of it. Our review unit came with an Intel Core 3 304 "Wildcat Lake" chip that runs up to 4.3 GHz. Previous mini PCs based on Intel 300 series mobile silicon like the previously MSI and the GMKtec Evo-T2 came with a 16C/16T CPU. However, here we have just 5C/5T, and that’s just one performance core supported by four low power efficiency (LPE) cores.

Still, single core performance continues to be important for system and app responsiveness. The proof of the pudding is in the testing, though. And while this system felt perfectly usable in Windows 11, internet browsers, and word processing — preparing and writing this review, for example — the benchmarks are somewhat tougher.

As we expected, the single core tests show Wildcat Lake has some bite. It easily beats its AMD-based rivals in the POV Ray single core rendering tests, for example. Moreover, in tests like Cinebench 2024 single core, the Mini M2 Air might trail the pack, but not by very much. It's roughly 107 points is actually within touching distance of the 120 points scored by ‘high performance’ mini PCs like the Beelink SER10 MAX (Gorgon Point) and MSI Cubi NUC AI+ 3MG (Panther Lake).

Multicore tests exposed obvious weaknesses of the 5C/5T CPU configuration with single-channel RAM. All the other mini PCs we’ve tested this year come with 12C/24T (AMD) or 16C/16T (Intel), so the productivity benchmarks we run which can take advantage of multithreading for demanding rendering or encoding tasks make the Minisforum here seem weak. However, it might still be acceptable for transcoding a short video or two, occasionally, as long as you aren’t in a rush. If you are coming from an older, less efficient desktop sporting something like an i7-7700K or i5-10400, this 15W Wildcat Lake chip is an improvement in both single and multicore performance.

During our demanding tests, the Minisforum Elite Mini M2 Air-304 stayed cool and quiet thanks to its 15W processor and capable cooling.

For anything other than the most casual gaming or emulation of older consoles you should look at other devices. We’ve looked at Intel 300 silicon powered devices with iGPUs packing 12 Xe3 cores, and four Xe3 cores, but this just has one, and it is further hampered by sharing the single-channel system RAM.

Prepare to see some low frame rates / scores in our standard tests. I felt that some of these tests probably shouldn’t be run on this mini PC, but did it anyway, for completeness. However, some 3DMark benchmarks simply wouldn’t run, as Intel has pruned the features that work on this iGPU due to limited resources. For example, 3DMark Solar Bay doesn’t run on this system, and neither does Speed Way. Error messages about unsupported hardware pop up when you try these tests.

Steel Nomad ran, though, and the Minisforum and its solo Xe3 core achieved a score of 135. The aforementioned Evo-T2 with Intel Core Ultra X7 358H and (B390) iGPU with 12x Xe3 cores scored 1,624. That’s incredibly close to 12x faster than the Core 3 304’s ‘Intel Graphics.’ A score of 135 means Steel Nomad ran at just a little more than 1 FPS, which actually doesn’t hurt your eyes to watch as much as 10 FPS.

Using the multi-scene benchmarks built into Shadow of the Tomb Raider and Cyberpunk 2077 using our standard settings selections resulted in some unpleasant experiences. Performance of no better than 10 or 12 FPS was observed in these tests. To achieve 25 or even 30 FPS in these titles I found that reducing the game resolution to 720p and low/lowest settings would just about get there.

So, for some actual fun on the Mini M2 Air I decided to try the latest version of the Sony PSP emulator PPSSPP and play some Wipeout Pure. I managed to run this game on my 1440p monitor with a 6x render resolution, and it achieved a nice steady 60 FPS, delivering an enjoyable futuristic racing experience. It would also therefore likely be good for retro emulation of similar era and older consoles as well as home computers.

During my day-to-day use, the Minisforum system consumed around 9W when idling on the desktop. When under the heaviest loads I threw at it, the highest system power consumption I saw was around 31W.

I didn't find it surprising that this low-power system ran cool and quiet. System tools showed that during light activities like browsing the web the CPU temperature remained in the low 30s Celsius. Playing games or benchmarking for length periods, I never saw the reported CPU temperature rise beyond the low 70s Celsius.

Minisforum’s tiny mini PC was a quiet operator at idle and under load. My measurements from about half a foot in front of the device show noise levels under 30 dBA at idle, which is very quiet and actually said to be beneath the measurement threshold of my meter. Stressing the system with simultaneous CPU and GPU loads I managed to observe just a reading of a little over 37 dBA from the system.

The Mini M2 Air ships with Windows 11 Pro installed in the configuration we received. I found no pre-installed bloatware. The official specs don’t mention compatibility with other OSes like Linux.

Minisforum sells this mini PC with a two-year warranty and a 30-day money-back guarantee.

Minisforum sells only two Elite Mini M2 Air-304 configurations in the U.S. There’s a barebones model, or the configuration we tested with pre-installed RAM, SSD, and OS.

At the time of writing our 16GB / 500GB / Windows 11 Pro configuration is listed by the mini PC maker at $559 but the product page says the price is normally $699. The barebone model is currently $263, discounted from $329. We don’t know when the prices might get adjusted. However, we’ve noted U.S. retailers like B&H Photo are now selling the 16GB / 500GB model at $729.

Though the Mini M2 Air’s performance may lack in performance, it also probably the smallest cheapest, and least power-hungry mini PC we’ve tested all year. If these kinds of qualities are important to you, the Minisforum might be appealing.

It is admirable that this mini PC is so small but leaves RAM and storage upgrades on the table for later. Other positives are the sturdy build, despite the use of plastic for the top shell, and a good port selection considering its size. In addition to being easy to hide due to its small stature, and supplied VESA mount, you probably won’t hear it very much, either.

Of course, the performance is a weakness that can’t be ignored, especially in the context of our ever-growing tested mini PCs chart from 2026. It is great to see this latest-gen processor barebones at just over $260 direct from Minisforum. If you have a spare DDR5 SODIMM and SSD, perhaps from a laptop upgrade or wherever, this might help you get a new mini PC system up and running cheaply. However, the 16GB RAM and 500GB SSD (and Windows 11 Pro license) currently more than doubles the list price (it adds $296). That’s a bit painful.

Furthermore, if the Minisforum Elite Mini M2 Air ever rises to its apparent MSRP of $699, we’d definitely look elsewhere. Around this price, especially during sale periods, you can shop for mini PCs employing far more powerful previous-gen processors. One good alternative candidate might be the Geekom IT13 Max 2026 we reviewed in August at $799. Price trackers show this Core Ultra 185H machine with 24GB RAM has been available for under $600 with discount / coupon over the last few months. There are also a lot of capable older gen AMD mini PCs to ponder over at attractive prices.

#10328. Google Wallet rolling out digital Google Home keys with supported locks on Android

Google Wallet for Android is rolling out support for digital home keys if you have a compatible smart lock in Google Home.

After adding a home key to Google Wallet, you can Tap to Unlock with your phone. This leverages NFC and “works even when your phone screen is locked” by default. However, you can adjust verification settings in the Google Wallet app to require screen lock or biometric authentication. This requires Android 9+.

There’s also support for Unlock on Approach with an Aliro Ultra Wideband (UWB)-compatible Android phone (see below) and an Aliro UWB-compatible lock. Wear OS watches are not supported at launch.

Behind the scenes, the digital key is linked to the Google Home app rather than a single lock. As such, “each of your compatible Aliro locks will work with a single key in your Google Wallet.” Keys are added to Google Wallet using the Home app, which will prompt you to save after setup. You can also go to Home settings > Google Wallet key.

Crazy to me that Google's own smart lock with Yale doesn't have this functionality. Seems like a huge miss for them to not be forward looking with their own hardware here.

Afterward, your key “automatically syncs across compatible Android phones signed into your Google Wallet account.”

The card in Google Wallet features a simple Google Home logo with your home’s name in the top-right corner.

The following locks are supported at launch: Aqara u400 (UWB and NFC), Schlage Sense Pro (NFC at launch and UWB to follow), and Nuki Keypad 2 NFC. Support for more models is coming.

Additionally, support is rolling out first to the Google Home Public Preview before coming to all users “over the coming weeks.”

#10326. Top 6 AI Text to Speech Tools in 2026: Features, Strengths and Limits

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Not long ago, a synthetic voice was easy to spot: flat, rushed and strangely stressed. Today, the best AI text to speech tools can read a script with natural pacing, switch accents, whisper or laugh on cue, and even speak in a voice cloned from a short recording. That makes them useful far beyond screen readers. Developers use them for product voice prompts, marketers for video voiceovers, teachers for course narration and writers for listening back to their own drafts.

This guide explains how modern text-to-speech works, what to look for when choosing a tool, and compares six options: OpenArt, ElevenLabs, Murf AI, Speechify, WellSaid Labs and Amazon Polly. Each section covers strengths, limits and the best use case. The details come from each vendor’s own pages at the time of writing, and plans, models and prices change often, so confirm current terms before you commit.

OpenArt’s AI text to speech tool lives inside a wider creative platform. You open the Audio tab, choose Create voice-over, select a model, paste your script, pick a voice, generate, listen and download. It runs in the browser with nothing to install. According to OpenArt’s page, you can switch between models for each script, including Fish Audio Drama 3, Eleven v4 and Eleven v4 Turbo.

The direction system is what makes it practical for storytelling. You add notes in square brackets, such as [whispering], [laughs] or [pause], directly in the script. OpenArt says Fish Audio Drama 3 gives close control over single words and mid-line changes of mood, while Eleven v4 reads the whole script to infer the emotion of each line and keeps the voice steady when you regenerate a line. Preset voices are labeled by accent, gender and age, and you can upload a recording of your own voice to clone it.

Because OpenArt also makes video and background music, you can build the voiceover, visuals and soundtrack in one place. It describes a Director mode for planning stories up to five minutes, and the voiceover can also be added in any video editor.

Pricing: New accounts get 40 free credits. Paid plans add monthly credits and watermark-free downloads, and commercial rights apply on eligible paid plans. Check the pricing page for current plans.

Best for: creators who want expressive voiceovers together with AI video and music.

ElevenLabs is one of the best-known names in AI voice. Its text to speech page describes a large voice library, voice design and both instant and professional voice cloning, with the latest model, Eleven v4, positioned as the most expressive. It also lists Eleven v3, Multilingual v2 and a low-latency Flash and Turbo family aimed at real-time use, and it claims support for more than 90 languages and accents, with the exact count varying by model.

For developers, there is a text to speech API with SDKs, which makes it practical for apps, games, agents and automated pipelines. The free plan includes 10,000 credits a month, and paid plans start with Starter at $6 a month, then Creator at $22 and Pro at $99, according to the vendor page, with introductory offers on the first month.

Pricing: Free plan with 10,000 credits a month. Paid plans from $6 a month as listed on the vendor page.

Best for: expressive narration, multilingual content and apps that need a voice API.

Murf AI positions itself as a studio for voiceovers. Its page lists more than 200 voices, built with permission and partnership from professional voice actors who earn royalties, and support for 35 or more languages for text to speech, with dubbing in 40 or more. The Studio editor offers pitch, speed and intonation controls and a custom pronunciation library, which helps with brand names and technical terms.

Murf also has an API. Its page describes Falcon 2 for conversational use with time-to-first-audio under 100 milliseconds, priced at $0.01 per minute, and Gen 2 for controllable voiceovers. Gen 2’s “Say it My Way” feature lets you record your own rendition so the model reflects your tone and inflections.

Pricing: Free plan with limits. Paid plan prices are on Murf’s pricing page.

Best for: marketing videos, training and presentations where you want to fine-tune delivery.

Speechify is built around reading aloud. You paste text or upload a PDF, DOCX, TXT or EPUB file, pick a voice, adjust playback speed and listen or download the audio. Its page lists more than 1,000 voices across 60 or more languages, including several regional English accents, and the tool works in the browser and in apps for iOS, Android, Mac and Windows, plus Chrome and Edge extensions.

The use cases lean toward productivity and accessibility: studying, going through emails and documents, language learning and support for people with visual impairments, dyslexia or ADHD. A free account saves your documents and syncs them across devices.

Pricing: You can start free. Paid plans are listed on Speechify’s pricing page.

Best for: students, professionals and anyone who wants to listen to documents and articles.

WellSaid Labs focuses on professional voiceover for businesses. Every voice is built from recordings of a real voice actor who consented and is paid when the voice is used, and the voices are licensed for commercial use. Its page describes hundreds of voices across languages, accents, tones and styles.

Pronunciation control is a highlight. You can highlight a word to see suggested pronunciations and save your choice to a team library that applies to every project, and set pacing, emphasis and pronunciation line by line. WellSaid also describes a Producer product that connects AI assistants to WellSaid through MCP and its API.

Pricing: Contact sales for a quote based on your voiceover volume.

Best for: corporate training, product marketing, courses and advertising where licensing and consistency matter.

Amazon Polly is Amazon Web Services’ text to speech service, and it is aimed squarely at developers. According to AWS, it supports more than 40 languages and language variants with over 100 male and female voices, and it works through APIs in your code. It supports SSML, the W3C standard markup for phrasing, emphasis and intonation, and custom lexicons that fix the pronunciation of acronyms and terms. Output formats include MP3 and OGG.

Pricing is usage-based. AWS lists a free tier for new accounts, with usage allowances that depend on the voice engine, so estimate your character volume before you decide. A short example shows how little code a basic request takes:

The sample uses the AWS SDK for JavaScript, calls the neural engine and sends SSML so that the pause and emphasis are explicit. Adjust the region, voice and engine to match what your account supports.

Pricing: Usage-based with a free tier for new accounts. See the AWS pricing page for current rates.

Best for: apps, IVR systems, notifications and any product that needs automated speech at scale.

Treat this table as a starting point. Features and limits change often, so test two or three tools with the same script.

Modern text-to-speech systems turn written words into audio through several stages.

Newer expressive systems go further by reading the whole script to infer emotion and context, and by accepting direction in the text itself, such as a bracketed note that says to whisper. That is why a single script can produce very different reads depending on the model and the instructions.

If you use a tool with SSML support, you can make a spoken code or number far clearer:

The say-as element tells the engine to read the number digit by digit, and the break and emphasis tags shape the rhythm. Supported tags and attributes differ between engines, so check the documentation for yours.

What is the best AI text to speech tool? It depends on the job. OpenArt suits voiceovers with video and music, ElevenLabs suits expressive multilingual audio, Murf AI suits studio-style control, Speechify suits listening, WellSaid Labs suits licensed corporate work and Amazon Polly suits developers.

Is AI text to speech free? Several tools offer free plans or credits, but limits are common. Examples include OpenArt’s free credits for new accounts, ElevenLabs’ free monthly credits and Murf’s limited free plan, which has no commercial licence.

Can I use AI voices commercially? Often yes on paid plans, but the rules differ by tool. Always check the current licence before publishing.

Can AI text to speech clone my voice? Some tools support it, including OpenArt and ElevenLabs, usually from a short recording of your own voice. Only clone voices you have permission to use.

What is SSML? Speech Synthesis Markup Language is a W3C standard that lets you control pauses, emphasis, pronunciation and pitch in synthetic speech. Amazon Polly is one service that supports it.

Which tool is best for developers? Amazon Polly and ElevenLabs both offer APIs, and Murf describes API options as well. Evaluate latency, languages, pricing and SDK support for your use case.

How do I make AI voices sound less robotic? Write conversationally, add pauses and emphasis, correct pronunciations and split long scripts into short sections. Choose a model that is designed for expressive reads.

AI text to speech is now good enough for real projects, but the right tool depends on what you are making. OpenArt is a strong first stop for expressive voiceovers alongside video and music, ElevenLabs offers a deep voice library and API, Murf AI and WellSaid Labs suit polished, controlled narration, Speechify turns documents into audio and Amazon Polly fits straight into developer workflows. Test two or three with your own script, check the licence and always listen to the full result before it goes live.

#10323. Intel bets on Classiq acquisition to turn Quantum efforts into commercially viable business

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Intel becomes a top-to-bottom quantum computing company.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

After having spent more than a decade pursuing quantum computing research, Intel is making its first real push at commercializing its efforts. First, with the pending acquisition of quantum computing company Classiq, which has an existing list of enterprise customers. Intel says it'll be the only company that can produce qubit chips and cryogenic control electronics at scale; the deal would give it a complete top-to-bottom hardware-to-software stack, and Intel will also gain the chance to become a leading vendor of quantum computing products. Intel's acquisition of Classiq is expected to complete in the first half of 2027.

The key ingredient of that strategy is the pending acquisition of Classiq, which offers a hardware-agnostic quantum software development platform that enables engineers to design, synthesize, optimize, simulate, and execute quantum algorithms using high-level programming tools and AI-assisted automation, essentially untying software design from the complexity of quantum hardware. Meanwhile, Intel also plans to manufacture quantum processors using specialized versions of its 18A process technology, offer foundry services, and eventually sell complete quantum computers. Although the company expects its quantum business to become profitable, it acknowledges that commercial success will take time.

Intel has spent more than a decade developing quantum computing technologies, including silicon spin-qubit processors, cryogenic control electronics, and software for hybrid quantum-classical computing. However, the company has largely treated these activities as research and development projects rather than an actual business. The acquisition of Classiq, which Intel expects to complete in the first half of 2027, marks a shift toward commercialization as Classiq already has many clients and established revenue streams.

"Up until now […] our quantum computing group for the last ten years was […] purely a research and development project," said Pushkar Ranade, chief technology officer at Intel, during a media briefing. "It was not really viewed as a commercial activity."

Intel's quantum computing journey kicked off in 2015 with a research collaboration with QuTech — the quantum research institute of TU Delft and TNO — to develop quantum computing technologies, including silicon spin qubits. The idea was to leverage Intel's extensive expertise in silicon transistor development and its high-volume semiconductor manufacturing infrastructure to produce quantum processors in the same fabs that make conventional CPUs, overcoming the manufacturing scalability challenges alternative quantum computing architectures faced at the time. Intel subsequently developed Horse Ridge, a cryogenic control SoC manufactured using a 22nm process technology in 2020. Three years later, the company introduced Tunnel Falls, a 12-qubit silicon spin processor manufactured on a 300mm wafer.

Intel's quantum computing effort focuses on silicon spin qubits, which use spin states of individual electrons to store and manipulate quantum information. The company chose this approach partly because silicon spin qubits can be manufactured using equipment and processes similar to those employed for conventional semiconductor devices. This compatibility enables Intel to re-use most of its existing manufacturing infrastructure and expertise rather than build dedicated semiconductor fabrication facilities for quantum processors that yet have to become commercial products.

Despite the recent cuts at Intel, the company sustained its quantum computing project. Intel has developed two specialized versions of its 18A manufacturing technology for quantum computing: 18A-Q for silicon spin-qubit processors and 18A-CC for cryogenic control and readout electronics. To define/build 18A-Q, Intel has collaborated with Hitachi, AIST, and NEDO (three major supporters of quantum computing from Japan), and there appears to be a design kit intended to support large quantum computing systems. Without a doubt, 18A-Q and 18A-CC put Intel on the map of quantum computing chips in a big way. A couple of things should be kept in mind, though.

"The underlying device that we use, underlying process that we use to make the actual qubit device is a unique process, but it fits into those design rules," Ranade explained. "We can make these chips […] in the same fab that we use to make our CPUs."

Although Intel 18AQ uses BEOL interconnect geometry and design rules derived from Intel 18A, its underlying qubit devices require a different manufacturing process. According to Ranade, the technology requires a specialized thin-film stack and several additional manufacturing tools. Nevertheless, more than 90% of the manufacturing equipment is identical to that used for conventional semiconductor production, according to Intel.

"Intel 18A-CC, meanwhile, uses 18A-based geometry but requires additional characterization for operation at cryogenic temperatures," Ranade said. "Crucially, Intel can manufacture quantum processors alongside conventional CPUs in existing semiconductor fabs, which could reduce the capital expenditure required to scale production. […] More than 90% of the process equipment is exactly the same."

For conventional x86 architectures and IA-64 (while it was active), Intel has historically offered compilers that made software run better on its hardware (and sometimes on AMD hardware too). By contrast, with its quantum computing efforts, Intel has generally focused on hardware and while it has its own Quantum SDK that includes a compiler, this is not enough in the quantum computing realm as Classiq's offering operates at a higher abstraction level. With the acquisition of Classiq, the company intends to fill that gap.

Classiq develops a hardware-agnostic quantum software platform that applies electronic design automation (EDA) principles to quantum programming, which lets developers design, synthesize, and optimize quantum algorithms without manually constructing circuits from individual quantum gates. The company's software provides high-level modeling, automated algorithm synthesis, optimization, debugging, and execution capabilities across different quantum computing architectures, which somewhat resembles Intel's approach to its x86 compilers, albeit working at a different level.

Intel already has its Quantum SDK, which includes a C++-based compiler, runtime environment, and simulator for hybrid quantum-classical applications. Classiq's platform will sit above these tools and provide an application-development environment for programmers who lack extensive expertise in quantum computing.

Intel compares the potential significance of Classiq's software layer to Microsoft's MS-DOS, which helped establish the personal computer software ecosystem; Nvidia's CUDA, which enabled developers to exploit GPUs for general-purpose accelerated computing; and EDA tools, which abstracted high-level design descriptions from the complexities of transistor-level integrated circuit implementation and essentially set the stage for the multi-billion foundry industry today.

Furthermore, unlike Intel's quantum hardware business, Classiq already has commercial customers and generates revenue. The company employs approximately 130 people and serves more than 50 enterprise customers, including BMW, Comcast, Lufthansa, Rolls-Royce, and Toshiba. It also participates in more than 10 national quantum computing programs and supports over 50 quantum processors and simulators.

As existing quantum processors remain insufficient for many practical applications, companies can develop and optimize software ahead of hardware availability. As a result, these customers primarily use Classiq products to develop quantum algorithms, train engineers, and create intellectual property in preparation for commercially useful quantum computers.

Intel's CTO described Classiq's revenue and anticipated growth as healthy, but did not disclose actual financial figures.

"They have a very healthy revenue this year and a very healthy revenue CAGR over the coming years," Ranade said. "We hope that… we want that to continue."

It is necessary to note that Intel intends to preserve Classiq's hardware-agnostic approach, which means allowing the company to continue supporting quantum processors and simulators from competing vendors. This is essential to maintain Classiq's existing customer relationships and expand its revenue opportunities, as its clients can/will be able to develop quantum applications without committing to a particular hardware architecture. Obviously, Intel can generate software revenue regardless of which quantum computing technologies ultimately become commercially successful.

"We very much want to maintain that hardware-agnostic platform that they have developed to attract more and more customers," Ranade said.

When it comes to quantum computing, Intel's ambitions extend beyond manufacturing silicon spin-qubit processors. The company intends to become a supplier of complete quantum computing systems, which would combine quantum processors, cryogenic control electronics, classical computing resources, and software. For now, Intel has most of the building blocks required to develop complete quantum computing systems, but it still has to demonstrate that it can integrate them into commercially viable quantum computing at scale.

"We want to be in the business of building quantum foundry components, but also complete quantum systems," said Pushkar Ranade, chief technology officer at Intel.

Intel is also considering opportunities to provide quantum foundry services to other hardware developers. While its internal processor development will remain focused on silicon spin qubits, the company is open to manufacturing components for alternative quantum computing technologies, which somewhat resembles its approach to its x86 CPU and foundry businesses.

"We are committed to spin qubits," Ranade said. "But we are also open to providing foundry services to other modalities."

Intel believes that its capabilities in photonics, advanced packaging, and semiconductor manufacturing could become commercially relevant even to companies developing quantum processors based on different physical principles than silicon qubits.

Intel's decision to expand its quantum computing activities with the takeover of Classiq comes as chief executive Lip-Bu Tan emphasized profitability, financial discipline, and high-margin products across the company’s established businesses. Given that Intel's quantum computing initiative barely generates any revenue, let alone profits, the CEO stance raises questions about the viability of quantum computing for Intel, especially in a situation where the industry has yet to demonstrate widespread practical applications that justify the cost of large-scale quantum hardware.

When asked how Intel’s quantum computing strategy fits Tan's profitability requirements, Ranade explained that the company's immediate financial priorities primarily concern its established client and server processor businesses. Nevertheless, Intel must continue investing in emerging technologies that could shape future computing markets.

"This is really a bet on the future," Ranade said. "This is something we also have to do in addition to our mainstream businesses today."

The Intel CTO acknowledged that quantum computing is not set to become a high-margin business in the near future. Meanwhile, the acquisition of Classiq gives Intel an established commercial software operation and revenue stream here and now, as well as an opportunity to expand customer relationships well before its own quantum processors reach commercial maturity. Intel also hopes that combining its hardware and software capabilities (probably including Classiq) will attract customers interested in complete quantum computing solutions.

When asked whether vertical integration would make Intel's quantum computing operations profitable, Ranade acknowledged that he could not predict gross margins of the quantum computing business because the industry is still in its infancy. Nevertheless, he expressed confidence in the commercial prospects of Intel's quantum hardware and software.

"I expect it to become a profitable business very quickly on the hardware side, but too early to tell," Ranade said.

Even though quantum processors evolve at a rapid pace, the technology is unlikely to replace existing CPUs and GPUs for conventional computing any time soon. Instead, Intel expects quantum computing systems to operate alongside the usual processors in data centers and supercomputing environments.

Classiq's software platform will provide high-level quantum algorithm development capabilities, while Intel Quantum SDK will handle compilation, simulation, and hybrid quantum-classical execution. This approach differs from Nvidia's CUDA-Q, which is designed to integrate quantum computing workflows with existing accelerated computing infrastructure. According to Classiq chief executive Nir Minerbi, Classiq operates at a higher level of abstraction and can complement CUDA-Q rather than compete with it.

"CUDA Q is very much low-level; it is gate-level. It is a good platform, by the way, but it is very low-level," said Minerbi. "The reason companies like Rolls-Royce and BMW and others use the Classiq platform on top of CUDA Q is due to the level of abstraction. It is much, much easier to develop quantum programs with Classiq and then to simulate it with CUDA Q rather than only with CUDA Q."

Meanwhile, commercial success of quantum computing still depends on substantial advances in quantum hardware, including qubit scalability, error correction, control electronics, and system integration. For now, many Classiq customers are developing applications for quantum computers that have yet to reach the scale required for commercially valuable workloads.

Intel expects to complete its acquisition of Classiq in the first half of 2027, subject to regulatory approvals and customary closing conditions. The company has not disclosed the purchase price or other financial terms.

The transaction will bring Classiq's software development capabilities and established customer relationships into Intel's quantum computing organization, but will preserve the platform's compatibility with competing hardware technologies. For Intel, the acquisition represents an opportunity to turn more than a decade of quantum computing research into a commercial business unit as it will have all the building blocks required to create quantum systems. Furthermore, Intel's quantum computing business will be open to third parties in the sense that Intel will be able to build quantum processors for partners using its 18AQ and 18ACC process technologies.

Whether Intel's quantum computing business eventually delivers the high margins Intel seeks will depend not only on Intel's ability to build systems, produce quantum chips for others, or Classiq's software revenue, but also on the industry's ability to develop quantum processors and systems capable of solving real-world problems that people are willing to pay for.

#10320. The Lenovo Legion Tab Gen 5 would be my perfect Steam tablet if it didn’t cost $900

For every Android-powered slate I’ve used, only Nvidia’s Shield Tablet really stands out in my memory. Part of the company’s occasional (and all-but-dead) dip into hardware products, the Shield Tablet — and its revitalized K1 follow-up, which I also owned through a recall program on the original — carried me through the back half of college, frequently keeping my Chromebook in my backpack in lieu of a mini Bluetooth keyboard or the Shield Tablet’s built-in stylus. Its front-firing speakers made it great for movie-watching, and while I’m not much of a mobile gamer, I did find it to be a great device for emulation, especially when paired with an external controller.

Even if Nvidia hadn’t abandoned the hardware game at the end of the last decade, it’s found new and much more destructive ways to make piles of cash at the expense of your easy access to RAM. And considering the market for Android tablets in general, it’s not all too surprising to see very few companies trying to make headway into offering a miniature, gaming-focused tablet that can easily be thrown in a backpack. Lenovo, however, has managed to fill its spot quite well. 9to5Google’s own Ben Schoon found himself smitten with the Gen 3 model nearly two years ago, and now, its Gen 5 successor has found its way into my nostalgic hands.

But expectations around mobile experiences have come a long way since 2016. Whether the Legion Tab Gen 5 can win over your heart really depends on your patience with some old-school Android jank — and whether your wallet can withstand an exceptionally-high asking price. Still, with recent advancements in PC emulation, it’s hard to say Lenovo isn’t onto something.

Considering the decade between their respective releases, I’m actually quite surprised just how much this tablet reminds me of the original Shield Tablet. Its 8.8-inch 16:10 LCD panel combined with a boxy matte-black design and a near-identical weight meant that I immediately felt at home holding this thing in my hands. You lose out on a couple of clutch features — most notably the built-in stylus — but largely speaking, this feels like the correct size and shape for a miniature gaming tablet.

Still, it’s not all perfect. Take, for example, the completely unnecessary camera bump that prevents this slate from properly laying flat on a desk. No one is buying this thing to use the camera, and judging by my very limited experience trying it out, I’m fairly confident whatever potato sensor is shoved in here would fit fine flush with the chassis. I’m pretty fed up with OEMs leaning on camera bumps to make any device look or feel more premium than it actually is, and that goes double quadruple for tablets like this.

Just like its predecessor, this thing still picks up smudges and fingerprints at an unreasonable level, its matte-black finish unable to hide anything. 360 grams is also a little heavier than I’d expect for a slate of this size, though to be fair, it’s much more noticeable in portrait than landscape. Holding the tablet with two hands to watch a movie or make my way through another round of Balatro made its top-heaviness much less noticeable.

This display is sharp and fast — with one minor caveat — and reasonably bright in all circumstances except direct sunlight thanks to its 600 nits of brightness (and 800 in HBM). Really, it’s the LCD of it all that gets me here; this thing is nearly $1,000, and the lack of OLED alone makes it difficult to recommend at this price. It’s not an outright bad panel, but I found its contrast levels to be pretty lacking, with most blacks only ever showing up as dark grays on-screen.

Oh, and that caveat: On two different occasions, I caught the tablet stuck in 60Hz mode, regardless of what refresh rate option I selected in settings. Switching back and forth between modes a few times eventually unstuck it, but it’s definitely a bug worth being aware of.

I think I’d feel a lot better about recommending this to someone looking for a content consumption device if this screen looked a little more premium. Your mileage may vary though — if you can get your hands on a demo unit before buying one, if only to see the display, I’d recommend it. For everyone else, this price tag might have you waiting for the OLED panel rumored to be arriving on next year’s successor.

At $900 — or potentially more, considering this tablet actually launched at $850 — the Legion Tablet’s hardware leaves something to be desired. Biometrics are the most obvious weak spot; camera-based face unlock is the only biometric option provided here, without a fingerprint scanner in sight. I wouldn’t blame some if they saw this as a dealbreaker, because even if you’re willing to put up with the security concerns that a basic front-facing camera brings to the table, I can promise you you’ll be typing the code in all the time. Whether you’re trying to unlock in a darker environment or it’s just been four hours since you last unlocked the slate, make sure you have your pin memorized.

If you’re primarily buying this as a content consumption machine, I think you’ll mostly have a great time. Lenovo built stereo sound into this slate, but did so in one of the smartest ways I’ve ever seen, ensuring all speakers are located to the right side along the bottom and top of the device. This might sound a little unbalanced in portrait mode, but in landscape, it guarantees you’re never actually covering up the speakers while holding the slate in your hands, something practically every other equivalent tablet is guilty of. No headphone jack here, unfortunately, something I would’ve liked to have seen on a gaming-focused device.

I find battery life genuinely difficult to judge on tablets, especially if you don’t pick it up every single hour of every single day, but I’ve been impressed overall with the Legion Tab’s longevity. Some weekend YouTube binge watching sessions at relatively high brightness settings left me with plenty of juice in the tank, and its standby time between sessions has been excellent. Obviously, if you’re really pushing your graphical limits on this thing — or if you’re emulating non-native software through something like DroidDeck — you’re going to see a bit of a dip, but combined with fast charging and two USB-C ports for both portrait and landscape access, battery life gets a big thumbs-up from me.

Lenovo’s packed a couple of accessories in the box with this thing, but I wouldn’t get too excited. While it’s nice to see a 68W charger included for those aforementioned fast top-offs, the included folio case is just awful. Not only does the triangle-shaped flap barely stick together to form a proper stand, but one of the corners of my case was loose enough to frequently flex back and forth. Holding the tablet while wrapped in its case just felt bad, in the way that so many bundled cases often feel, and a quick look at Amazon shows that basically no recognizable brand — or unrecognizable, for that matter — has stepped up to offer something better.

The Legion Tablet also includes a screen protector in the box, if that’s how you roll.

Unsurprisingly, the Snapdragon 8 Elite Gen 5 in here can handle effectively anything you dare to throw at it. While my mobile games might be limited to more casual, indie fare like Balatro — and, by the by, if you’re still enjoying Balatro a couple of years later, the size and shape of this device makes it perfect — graphically-intense games played fine. Asphalt Legends or the HoYoverse games might not be my cup of tea, but they perform excellently on this device, and compared to the 12-inch tablets companies like Samsung and Amazon have become obsessed with, the display size here actually feels perfect.

We definitely have to talk about emulation, though, because it’s increasingly becoming the main focus of mobile gamers everywhere, and this feels like a tablet perfectly designed for something like DroidDeck. Getting Steam emulation — complete with the Deck’s usual Big Picture mode UI — available as an application on the Legion Tab Gen 5 takes all of two minutes and a handful of taps on your display, and I have to say, I’m pretty shocked at just how impressive the overall experience is right now.

DroidDeck gave me the option to bring my mini gaming tablet experience full-circle. While my Steam library has no shortage of games I think could run without stressing this slate, I wanted to push the envelope a little bit. Persona 3 Reload felt like as good a candidate as any: a modern title that doesn’t necessarily require quick response times and fits virtual controls quite nicely. Plus, most of my gaming time on Nvidia’s tablet a decade ago was spent working through Persona 3 Portable; like poetry, it rhymes. I installed the game, ran it on the Legion Tab, and to my surprise and delight, it was absolutely in a playable state.

Now, there are some caveats here. I did drop the game’s graphics preset to low, and even still, there were some occasional hiccups while running around an early dungeon. The game’s initial intros and disclaimers actually gave the impression the title wouldn’t run at all, but I was able to sneak up on a shadow, use a slash attack to begin the battle — basically the only thing in P3R that remotely requires accurate timing — and issue commands to my player character and party mates.

Obviously, your experience emulating Steam titles on the Legion Tab will vary on what you want to play, and with such wide variables between experiences, I probably wouldn’t push most gamers to run out and pick one up right away. It might be early days for DroidDeck, but considering how we’ve already seen it progress, I wouldn’t be surprised to see some buyers picking this up as a Valve-esque take on a gaming tablet.

With all of that power under the hood, though, it’s Lenovo’s take on Android that leaves a lot to be desired. It’s not just that this feels like a rough-and-ready take on AOSP, an unrefined package that would feel more in line with devices from a decade ago, though, yes, that’s half the struggle here. But at its worst, the Legion Tab Gen 5 simply feels unoptimized in daily use.

Scrolling through web pages or various apps often felt sluggish and heavy, like the UI elements on screen never actually match the movement of my finger, and it’s unclear how much of that was due to the refresh rate bug I described above. Auto-rotate was both super finnicky and super sluggish, alternating back and forth between one state or the other, and the auto-rotate button that pops up when it’s disabled would frequently fail to appear. Occasionally, I’d run into app issues, such as choppy playback on a 1440p YouTube video that got so bad — regardless of what resolution I selected — that I had to fully force close the app in settings to fix it. Even getting the slate to reliably wake up (or not wake up) when picking it up was annoying, as it’s simultaneously sensitive enough to frequently turn on when I don’t want it to, or to stay asleep when I’m trying to unlock it. Inevitably, I turned that setting off.

You might think that shelling out $900 for an Android tablet would buy you an out from pre-installed bloatware; you’d be wrong. During setup, the Legion Tab attempts to install a whole host of apps and games, including Perplexity, TikTok, Temu, several match-three games that I’ve truly never heard of, and no less than two mahjong and solitaire titles each. In fact, there might actually be three solitaire titles depending on what “Jigsawcard Solitaire Puzzle” is, but I unchecked all of these apps during setup and didn’t deem the title worthy of bothering to check.

While Lenovo has been trying to improve its software update policies on some recent devices, this slate isn’t among that group. To my knowledge, there is no official update schedule for this device, but based on past performance, I’d expect somewhere around 2 OS upgrades and 4 years of security patches, give or take.

I would’ve loved nothing more than to recommend this year’s Legion Tab to small tablet fans and gamers everywhere, but when this thing is $200 more than Apple’s latest iPad Mini — which in and of itself is absolutely overpriced — and $100 more than the base OLED Steam Deck post-price hike, I just have a hard time telling anyone to buy it. Really, its Steam performance was one of the few areas that made me feel truly excited by this thing, but it’s simply too unpredictable an experience at this point (and price) for me to recommend.

Even setting aside the price, there’s simply too much here that feels unfinished or rough around the edges for my liking. A handful of bug fixes and a couple of additional optimization passes could fix a lot of my more minor quibbles, but despite what I just said, you can only ignore the $900 price tag for so long before it bites you in the ass.

We have seen a post-iPad Mini discount bringing this slate to an Apple-matching $700 ahead of the holidays, but even that price is $150 more than its predecessor was at launch without a whole lot to show for it. If you’re looking for a gamer-friendly tiny tablet, I do think Legion is a brand worth keeping your eye on, especially as DroidDeck and other Steam emulation apps continue to evolve. But at this point — and for this price — your best bet is waiting for whatever model follows this one. It might still be stupid expensive, but at least you’ll be getting OLED.

A decade ago, Nvidia’s Shield Tablet felt like a no-brainer addition to my everyday carry. But in this economy, gamers and tablet fans alike are simply better off dropping $900 elsewhere.

#10317. Data-Driven Pages That Never Go Stale: A Practical Guide

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Every developer has shipped the other kind of page: a lovingly written article or landing page that peaks on launch day and then quietly rots. Screenshots age, statistics expire, rankings slide. Data-driven pages play the opposite game. Built from a living dataset, they republish themselves daily, grow to thousands of URLs, and answer questions nobody had asked when the site launched. This guide breaks down how they work, using a real fan-run stats site as the running example: one public dataset in, more than ten thousand fresh pages out.

Static pages lose ground because the web moves and they do not. Facts expire, competitors publish fresher answers, and search engines reward pages whose information matches today. A page that cannot update itself must be rewritten by hand, so most sites update a few pages and abandon the rest.

Freshness is not one rule for the whole web; it depends on the query. Nobody needs a CSS tutorial rewritten weekly, but statistics, prices, availability and anything with a season attached decays on a schedule. Developers misjudge this constantly, treating every page like documentation when half their catalogue is really a perishable good. Serve a three-month-old stat as if it were current and readers learn the most damaging lesson a site can teach: do not trust the numbers here.

Then there is the maintenance maths. A hundred hand-written pages each needing one review a year is a hundred small debts, and the interest compounds as the catalogue grows. The team updates the flagship pages, promises to return for the rest, and quietly never does. Data-driven architecture is really a staffing decision disguised as a technical one: humans design the system once, and the system does the updating forever.

A data-driven site has three layers: a dataset that refreshes on a schedule, templates that turn records into pages, and URL patterns that map every meaningful combination users might search for. Change the data and every page changes; improve a template and ten thousand pages improve at once.

The running example makes this concrete. A fan-built Overwatch stats tracker republishes Blizzard's public hero data on a daily cycle. From that one feed it generates hero profiles, head-to-head matchup pages and multi-condition tier lists: 10,291 URLs in its sitemap, across six languages, with matchup pages alone accounting for 8,586 of them. Nobody wrote those pages. Somebody designed the system that writes them, which is a very different, and much more scalable, job.

The example runs on Next.js behind a CDN, but the stack is the least important layer of the three. The same pipeline feeding WordPress, Astro or a static site generator would look identical from the reader's side of the glass. Choose boring technology you already know, and spend your originality budget on the dataset and the templates, because that is where readers will feel the difference.

Generated pages stay useful when every URL must earn its existence with data. Set minimum thresholds before a page is published, give each template unique modules its siblings do not have, and noindex combinations with nothing to say. The line between a resource and spam is not automation; it is emptiness.

Quality gates work best when they are public and mechanical. The example site publishes its tier formula openly: 70% of the score from win rate, 30% from pick rate, and a hard floor that excludes heroes picked in under 0.5% of matches from topping the ranking, because a tiny sample cannot prove anything. That single floor is editorial policy expressed as code. Your version might be a minimum number of data points, a minimum price history, or a minimum review count — the number matters less than the fact that the gate exists and applies to every page equally.

Each template also needs modules its siblings cannot copy. A hero profile carries a seven-day trend and its best and worst matchups; a matchup page carries the head-to-head history; a tier list carries the movement since yesterday. Add a short human layer on top — a methodology page explaining the scoring, changelogs narrating what moved — and the catalogue stops reading as output and starts reading as a publication. Google's scaled-content rules target pages manufactured to manipulate rankings with little value; a page that answers a real query with real, current data is the opposite of that. The honest test for any generated URL is simple: if it vanished tonight, would a searcher lose an answer?

Start embarrassingly small: one dataset, one entity type, one template. A football club's results archive, a property portal's sold prices, your own product catalogue — anything structured and refreshed beats a grand plan. Prove one template earns traffic, then add combinations, languages and dashboards in that order.

What is programmatic SEO? Programmatic SEO means generating many pages from a structured dataset using templates, instead of writing each page by hand. Done well, each page answers a distinct search with real data. Done badly, it produces thousands of near-identical pages, which is where its reputation suffers.

Is programmatic SEO against Google's rules? No. Google's spam policies target scaled content created mainly to manipulate rankings, with little or no value for users. Pages generated from real data that answer real queries are publishing. The deciding question is whether a page helps its reader, not how it was produced.

How often should the data behind pages refresh? Match the refresh to how fast the underlying facts change. Sports and game statistics justify daily pulls, prices often need hourly checks, and reference data may only move monthly. Refreshing faster than reality changes wastes crawl budget and makes 'updated' stamps meaningless.

Do generated pages need unique written text? They need unique value, which is not the same thing. Identical paragraphs on ten thousand pages add nothing, but unique data, comparisons and trends are content in their own right. Add short human-written context per template, and let the data carry the differences between pages.

How many pages should a first project launch with? Launch with the smallest set that proves the template: one entity type, a few hundred pages at most. Validate that search engines index them and users engage before adding combinations and languages. Scaling an unproven template just manufactures tomorrow's cleanup job at industrial size.

The sites quietly winning the long game are not publishing more; they are publishing systems. One honest dataset, one well-built template and strict quality gates will outlast a hundred hand-written pages competing on freshness alone. Try it this week: take one dataset you already own, design a single template with four real modules, and ship fifty pages. Watch what the data does next.

#10315. The iPhone Duo’s rocky launch is a reminder of how far Android foldables have come

Good morning. Today I’m finding open source alternatives to all of my apps, poring through Pitchfork’s list of the 100 best indie rock albums to find ones I haven’t listened to, and preparing my body for November 19th by pre-loading GTA VI (and replaying GTA IV in the meantime).

Inbox is written by Daniel Bader. Read previous issues here, and then catch up with Ben Schoon’s excellent Weekender column published every Sunday.

On Monday, I unboxed the iPhone Duo I pre-ordered back in October and experienced a disarming sense of déjà vu. It’s beautiful hardware married to a messy, often painful software onboarding experience atypical of Apple’s usual polish. It reminds me of the Galaxy Fold’s early years.

I was most looking forward to the combination of a foldable form factor and Apple’s superior app ecosystem, but even the apps that have been updated to support the new aspect ratio, on both the front and back, feel ungainly and buggy. In moving UI elements from the bottom to the right, even with the display open, the Duo forces a rebuilding of essential muscle memory that’s prevailed across mobile platforms, including Android foldables, for 20 years.

The front display, in particular, is a massive test of one’s patience: apps, even ones ostensibly optimized for its stout dimensions, often feel unusable, with UI elements that would be prominent on the iPhone 18 Pro hidden behind overflow menus on the Duo. It’s exasperating and, frankly, boggling.

But what’s interesting is the enthusiasm with which developers are approaching the new form factor. Reddit threads reveal dozens of Duo-optimized app recommendations that feel novel and, if not polished, then propulsive. There’s a fascinating delta between waiting for my bank, or Starbucks, or Kobo, to get its act together to support the Duo and seeking out net-new opportunities and experiences.

I spent most of the summer using the Galaxy Z Fold 8, and I didn’t expect going back to it to feel so stately and predictable: the showcase of a platform with fewer highs but none of the Duo’s current lows.

I’m not sure if I’m going to keep the Duo and wait out its colicky infancy. But I do know that until now I hadn’t given Samsung enough credit for the amount of work it did to make foldable software feel ordinary, and that’s the highest compliment I can pay.

This section has some of our favorite stuff from around the internet. Great videos, weird memes, fun reads, recommended apps, whatever. Send us your recommendations!

Andrew’s look at the Boox Picco is a nice reminder that a gadget can be interesting because it does less. The $109 e-reader has a 4-inch screen, physical page-turn buttons, and an included magnetic case that can attach to a compatible phone. It competes directly with XTEINK’s excellent X4 Pro, which features a rudimentary touchscreen and backlight for around $100, though its magnets are built in.

Both products are wonderful examples of hardware companies adapting to the growing size and complexity of smartphones and seeing an opening for single-purpose products that do one thing really well. Plus, it’s fun to stick a thing to the back of your phone and… read it.

For something you can explore without buying another device, Paolo Mainardi turned his personal website into a point-and-click adventure. Articles live in books, links sit on a notice board, and his about page is a letter in a lighthouse. The Context Island is an incredibly thoughtful and unique approach to “web” design, and it’s another example of how AI can really help bring crazy ideas to fruition.

We’ll likely find out today whether Jensen Huang will be removed as Nvidia’s CEO. According to the WSJ, the company’s board is preparing to oust the company’s singular chief executive, who co-founded it in 1993.

Momentum picked up after the New York Times reported that in addition to his three-year affair with Alice Huang, an alleged Chinese spy, Jensen had a relationship with one of his direct reports, who still works at the company.

The UGREEN Nexode 45W Retractable Charger Block is a versatile device that can handle up to three devices at a time. The charging block has two built-in USB-C ports, along with a retractable USB-C cable that extends up to 2 meters.26 feet.

The Acer 3-in-1 Charging Station is a portable device that can simultaneously charge your iPhone, Apple Watch, and AirPods. Wireless charging supports up to 15 watts.

The Eissix MagSafe Power Bank offers 12,000 mAh of capacity for your smartphone and includes a built-in stand. This is perfect for keeping your smartphone battery topped off if you're frequently far away from a power outlet.
